LETTER OF TRANSMITTAL 

SACRAMENTO, CALIFORNIA, March, 1955

HON. HAROLD J. POWERS, President and Members of the Senate: 

GENTLEMEN: Your Senate Interim Judiciary Committee, created by 

Senate Resolution No. 135 (Senate Journal, June 10, 1953, page 4136) presents herewith a third progress report of its activities and results of its investigations, together with its recommendations. 

Respectfully submitted,

BURT W. BUSCH GERALD J. O'GARA DONALD L. GRUNSKY EDWIN J. REGAN JACK B. TENNEY 

(5)

SENATE RESOLUTION No. 135, AS AMENDED 

Relative to the creation of the Senate Interim Judiciary Committee 

Resolved by the Senate of the State of California, As follows: 

1. The Senate Interim Judiciary Committee is hereby created and authorized and directed to ascertain, study and analyze all facts relating to the judicial system of the State, and the administration of justice therein, including but not limited to: (a) The composition, structure, jurisdiction and administration of the several courts; (b) The method of selection, compensation and retirement of judicial officers and attaches; (c) The procedure, process, and rules of the courts, statutory and otherwise; (d) All provisions of the law relating to civil actions and remedies with particular attention to the doctrine of comparative negligence; (e) All laws relating to publication of notices and service of process; (f) The organization of the inferior courts of this State; (g) All laws relating to divorce, alimony, and domestic relations; (h) The study of the recommendations of the Senate Interim Committee on Judiciary in its 1953 Report to the Legislature; including but not limited to the operation, effect, administration, enforcement and needed revision of any and all laws in any way bearing upon or relating to the subject of this resolution, and to report thereon to the Senate, including in the reports its recommendations for appropriate legislation. 

2. The committee shall consist of five Members of the Senate appointed by the Committee on Rules thereof. Vacancies occurring in the membership of the committee shall be filled by the appointing power. 

3. The committee is authorized to act during this session of the 

Legislature, including any recess, and after final adjournment until the commencement of the 1955 Regular Session, with authority to file its final report not later than the fifteenth day after the end of the constitutional recess. The time for filing the final report shall not be extended. 

4. The committee and its members shall have and exercise all of the rights, duties and powers conferred upon investigating committees and their members by the provisions of the Joint Rules of the Senate and Assembly and of the Standing Rules of the Senate as they are adopted and amended from time to time at this session, which provisions are incorporated herein and made applicable to this committee and its members. 

5. The committee has the following additional powers and duties: (a) To select a chairman and a vice chairman from its membership. (b) To contract with such other agencies, public or private, as it deems necessary for the rendition and affording of such services, facili- (7)

JUDICIARY COMMITTEE PROGRESS REPORT 

ties, studies and reports to the committee as will best assist it to carry out the purposes for which it is created. (c) To cooperate with and secure the cooperation of county, city, city and county, and other local law enforcement agencies in investigating any matter within the scope of this resolution and to direct the sheriff of any county to serve subpenas, orders and other process issued by the committee. (d) To report its findings and recommendations to the Legislature and to the people from time to time and at any time, not later than herein provided. (e) To do any and all other things necessary or convenient to enable it fully and adequately to exercise its powers, perform its duties, and accomplish the objects and purposes of this resolution. 

6. The sum of eight thousand five hundred dollars ($8,500) or so much thereof as may be necessary is hereby made available from the Contingent Fund of the Senate for the expenses of the committee and its members for any charges, expenses or claims it may incur under this resolution, to be paid from the said contingent fund and disbursed, after certification by the chairman of the committee, upon warrants drawn by the State Controller upon the State Treasurer.

INTRODUCTION The Senate Interim Judiciary Committee differs from other interim and investigating committees in a rather marked degree. This committee seeks to directly coordinate its work with the Standing Judiciary Committees of the Senate which meet only during sessions of the Legislature, and members of the staff responsible for the routine work of the committee have been customarily employed by both the Senate Interim 

Judiciary Committees and the Senate Standing Judiciary Committees in order that there might be a maximum of coordination in the consideration of the many complex legal problems which are presented to the Members of the Senate. The Senate Standing Judiciary Committees are composed entirely of attorney Members of the Senate, and members of the interim committees are likewise attorneys chosen from among the larger membership of the standing committees.

The type of legislation referred to the Judiciary Committee of the Senate is extremely technical in character and in almost every instance affects the liberty, property or rights of every citizen of this State. 

At the same time the legislation considered by this committee is in many instances not of the sort which attracts wide public attention, and it has, therefore, been deemed advisable by the California Senate to provide, insofar as possible, a screening and analyzing process prior to consideration of the bills which are referred to this committee. To this end each of the bills referred to the Standing Judiciary Committee are analyzed prior to committee hearing on the bill, and throughout the interim between sessions the Senate Interim Judiciary Committee gathers information, conducts surveys and holds hearings on bills which have either been referred to it by the preceding Legislature or upon proposed legislation likely to be presented to the succeeding Session of the Legislature.

This current third progress report of the interim committee created by the 1953 Legislature continues the work of the Senate Interim Judiciary Committees created in 1949 and in 1951. It also supplements the work of the 1949, 1951 and the 1953 Sessions of the California Legislature insofar as the work of the Senate Judiciary Committees of these Legislatures is concerned. 

In order to give some indication of the volume of work required of the Judiciary Committees of both houses of the Legislature, it is interesting to note that during the 1955 Legislative Session, prior to the constitutional recess, over 750 bills were introduced in the Assembly and referred to the Assembly Judiciary Committee, while in excess of 150 bills were introduced in the Senate and referred to the Senate Judiciary Committee. (A complete list of the code sections affected by these measures is set forth in Appendix "A" to this report.) It may reasonably be estimated, therefore, that the Senate Judiciary Committee will be required to pass judgment on somewhere between 600 and 700 bills during the current session of the Legislature, making due allow- (9)

ance for some bills which will not be passed out of the Assembly Judiciary Committee. It would seem apparent, therefore, that continuing study and analysis is required between sessions of the Legislature in order that a carefully considered determination can be made by the Standing Committee of the Legislature of the merits of the far-reaching proposals submitted during legislative sessions.

SPECIAL SURVEYS AND INVESTIGATIONS 

In addition to the work of screening legislation presented to the Standing Judiciary Committees of the Senate, the Senate Interim Judiciary Committee has from time to time undertaken special studies of matters of unusual interest or complexity. In some instances these investigations and special studies have been concluded during one interim period between sessions of the Legislature, while in other cases the committee has continued to study and observe certain broad policies and subjects. For instance, the 1951 Legislature referred to the Senate 

Interim Judiciary Committee for study the subject of comparative negligence and assigned to the committee a series of bills which had been introduced at that session of the Legislature pertaining to the subject matter. At the 1953 Session of the Legislature a comprehensive report was submitted by the Senate Interim Judiciary Committee, analyzing the law pertaining to this very controversial legislation. Bills introduced at the 1953 Session of the Legislature pertaining to the subject were not enacted into law and the matter was again referred to this interim committee for further study. Likewise, the matter of the regulation and control of organizations soliciting charitable contributions in California has received the continuing study of several Interim Judiciary Committees. So, also, in the case of the accreditation of law schools by the State Bar and a continuing investigation into the matter of bar examinations has been conducted. In later sections of this report reference will be made to the present status of the studies upon all of these continuing investigations. 

WORK OF THE PREDECESSOR COMMITTEES 

The committee believes that it may be of interest to the bench and the bar to recapitulate a list of the code sections which the Judiciary Committees of the Senate have been called upon to consider for legislative change, beginning at the legislative session in 1949, at which session the predecessor Senate Interim Judiciary Committee was first created. It is suggested that this list, which is attached as Appendix "B" to this report, not only gives some indication of the volume of work, but also of the wide diversity of legal problems which are submitted to these committees of the Legislature for determination. B. EXPLANATIONS OF BILLS SPONSORED BY THE STATE BAR OF CALIFORNIA 

The committee heard the various proposals for statutory changes recommended by the State Bar. The committee believes that explanations of the effect of these proposed enactments and the reasons each is deemed desirable by the State Bar will be of assistance to members of the bench and bar, as well as to members of the Legislature, and, accordingly, there is hereinafter set forth the official explanation presented with each of the measures by representatives of the State Bar: 

PROBATE CODE SECTIONS 640, 642, 645 AND 646 Assembly Bill 12 (9) Setting Aside Small Estates. This bill amends the provisions for summary probate of estates so as to increase the monetary amount from $2,500 to $5,000. The amount of "other" estate which would be permitted in the hands of the surviving spouse or minor children would be increased from $5,000 to $10,000. These amounts have not been increased since the provision was first enacted in 1929.

GOVERNMENT CODE SECTIONS 8201, 8203.1, 8203.3, 8205.1, 8209, 8211 Assembly Bill 86 (50) Notaries Public-Amendment of Law Relating Thereto. 

Assembly Bill 86 makes these principal changes: (1) It would permit a person to be appointed a notary public for two counties, i.e., the one in which he resides and the one in which he has his principal place of business or employment. Separate appointments are required. At present the appointee must reside in the county for which he is appointed. (2) There is now no provision in the Government Code expressly stating the limitation on the authority of the notary to acts performed within the county for which he is appointed. (Compare special provisions in Gov. C. 8203.2.) In Fairbanks Morse & Co. v. Getshell, 13 C. A. 

458 (1910) it was held that in the absence of statutory authority, a notary public cannot act outside of such county. 

Assembly Bill 86 incorporates this general rule, expressly recognizing, however, that a notary may hold two commissions, as above. Also, it expressly permits any notary to act as such in any county of the state in connection with the taking of depositions. In this limited respect, the notary's authority is made state-wide. (3) The purpose of modifying the language of Section 8211 is to avoid any confusion as to the acts which a notary may lawfully per-

06 1 ད་ form. Deleted language is sometimes urged as authorizing acts constituting the practice of law. (See B. and P. C. 6125.) (4) Assembly Bill 86 increases the residence requirement to 12 months, from six months. (5) Assembly Bill 86 requires a notary who is not reappointed within 30 days after his commission expires, to deliver his papers to the county clerk. The present law makes no provision for this situation. (6) An express requirement of "good moral character" is added. 

PENAL CODE SECTION 1241 (NEW) Assembly Bill 87 (22) Compensation of Counsel Assigned by Appellate Court to 

Represent Indigent Person in Criminal Matter. The purpose of Assembly Bill 87 is to fill what appears to be a gap in recent legislation in this State on the subject of compensated legal representation of indigent persons in criminal matters. 

As an officer of the court, a member of the Bar by tradition and statute is bound "never to reject, for any [personal] consideration *** the cause of the defenseless or oppressed. (B. & P. C., Sec. 

6068 (h).) This broad obligation is being honored by the profession as a whole in many ways. However, it is the view of many familiar with the subject that representation of a person without means in criminal matters should be by some form of compensated system. This appears to be the recent policy in this State. Thus, in 1949, the Legislature extensively revised the Public Defender Law enacted in 1921 and made it more workable and suitable for adoption by smaller counties. (See 

1949 Stats. Ch. 1288; Gov. C., Secs. 27700, 27703, 27711.) In 1951 the

Legislature deleted provisions of Penal Code Section 987a which permitted a nonpublic defender county, by affirmative action, to adopt its provisions. It is now mandatory under that section that the superior court allow "assigned counsel" a reasonable sum for compensation and expenses. 

Section 987a, however, refers only to "counsel *** assigned in the superior court to defend a person who is charged therein with crime" (with other exceptions not here material). It is silent as to the matter of an appeal. It has been the practice for appellate courts themselves to assign counsel in certain cases, presumably on the assumption that Section 987a applies only to representation in the trial court. (See e.g. 

People v. McKay, 37 C. (2d) 792, 793—mandatory death sentence conviction reversed.)

In 1953 Section 1240 was added to the Penal Code to provide that where an automatic appeal is taken (death sentence) and "counsel has been appointed by the court," the cost of preparing defendant's brief up to $100 shall be paid by the State. 

In addition, it is the practice of the Supreme Court and District Court of Appeal to assign counsel to represent imprisoned persons without means, upon the filing of writs of habeas corpus and in other criminal matters before the court. 

Assembly Bill 87 would add Penal Code 1241, to provide in substance that where counsel has been appointed by the Supreme Court or a District Court of Appeal to represent a party to any criminal appeal

or proceeding in a criminal matter, the court shall fix a reasonable fee for such counsel, to be paid from the General Fund of the State. The amendment thus carries out the principle which has previously been approved in the amendment of Penal Code Section 987a mentioned above.

It is to be noted that, unlike Section 987a, the Public Defender Law expressly authorizes the public defender to represent the accused upon appeal, as well as in the trial court. (Gov. C. Sec. 27706 (a).)

Assembly Bill 87 does not undertake to specify the circumstances under which counsel is to be appointed by the Supreme Court or a District Court of Appeal but leaves this to the court itself. 

CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE SECTION 690.24 Assembly Bill 111 (36) Motor Vehicle Exempt From Execution or Attachment. This amendment would raise the value of an automobile exempt from execution or attachment from $250 to $500. 

This increase in exempt valuation is in keeping with the general increase in exemption statutes in recent years. For example, house trailer exempt valuation in this same statute was increased from $500 to $2,500 in 1951. The valuation of an automobile was fixed at $100 in 1935, and last increased to $250 in 1949.

PROBATE CODE SECTION 772 Assembly Bill 112 (11) Posting Notice of Sale of Personal Property. It is presently required by Section 772 of the Probate Code that notice of sale of personal property in an estate in probate be posted in three public places in the county in which the proceedings are pending. This requirement appears to date back to 1851. (Probate Act of 1851, Sec. 153; former

Section 1526, Code of Civil Procedure, enacted 1872.) 

Three public postings have largely, if not wholly, been abolished in probate practice. In proceedings taken in the estate itself, the usual requirement, where notice is by posting, is that the notice shall be posted at the courthouse. (E.g., Sec. 1200, Prob. C.; also see Sec. 780, Prob. C., amended in 1939 to add provisions for the sale of certain real property upon posted notice, notice to be posted at the courthouse.) 

The purpose of Assembly Bill 112 therefore is to eliminate obsolete requirements which in reality serve no useful purpose and to bring the law into conformity with other provisions. The amendment proposed will also apply to sales by guardians under the general guardianship law, by virtue of the general terms of Section 1534, Probate Code. 

When the court is held at a place other than the county seat, posting at the building where the court is held is equivalent to posting at the courthouse of the county. Section 1210, Probate Code. 

PROBATE CODE SECTION 850 Assembly Bill 115 (12) Proceeding Authorizing or Directing Conveyance or Transfer Pursuant to Contract of Decedent. Sections 850-853 of the Probate 

Code now provide a procedure whereby the probate court, upon petition

1 1 and notice, may authorize and direct an executor or administrator to execute a conveyance of real property or a transfer of personal property, to carry out a written contract of the decedent. 

The procedure by its terms is applicable to cases where "the decedent, if living, might have been compelled to make the conveyance or transfer," i.e., cases in which specific performance would have been decreed in a plenary action. 

The procedure is not exclusive of the right to maintain an independent action for specific performance. (O'Donnell v. Lutter, 68 C. A. (2d) 

376, 385; also see Bewick v. Mechem, 26 C. 2d 92.)

The purpose of Assembly Bill 115 is to remove an ambiguity which exists in the present wording of Section 850. Thus, a strict application of the present wording of the section would limit its use to cases where the obligation to convey was in existence and enforceable as such before death. In contrast, in recent years the use of contracts for the disposition of stock in family corporations, of partnership interests and other business assets, at or after death, has become widespread. "Buy and sell" agreements and options are familiar examples.

In some instances trial courts have relied upon a broader interpretation of Section 850; in others, a petition for instructions or to compromise has been used, to cover these situations. 

To remove the uncertainty, Assembly Bill 115 would amend Section 850, to make its provisions expressly applicable when "a person binds himself or his executor or administrator in writing to convey any real property or to transfer any personal property upon or after his death and the contract is one which can be specifically enforced." 

PENAL CODE SECTION 1203 Assembly Bill 116 (21) Probation-Removing Restrictions Against. Under present law probation is absolutely forbidden where the defendant is convicted of such crimes as robbery or burglary, etc., if: (1) the accused was armed with a deadly weapon; or (2) he wilfully inflicted great bodily injury; or (3) he has twice been convicted of felony; or (4) he has previously been convicted of one of the crimes listed in said section. 

This bill eliminates the absolute prohibition against probation in such cases and permits the court to grant probation in appropriate instances. 

The use of probation is no longer a matter of experiment but is widely recognized as a fundamental and effective program for the protection of society and for rehabilitation of those who have offended against society. Therefore, in all cases the trial judge should be granted the discretion of granting or denying probation, depending upon the facts and circumstances of the specific case and the background of the defendant then before the court. Statutory rules restricting probation either according to offenses or type of offender cannot be universally applied without hardship and injustice. The solution of this problem is to place authority without statutory limitation in the hands of the trial court which will then be held accountable for the proper exercise of the authority so conferred.

Probation should not be considered purely as an act of mercy or leniency but rather as one of the firmly established methods of treat-

ment. In other words, the methods now outlined for treatment of violators of the law are either state prison, county jail or probation. One must have in mind many persons who should be placed on probation at first object very strenuously to receiving this treatment. They feel they would rather take their sentence and have the matter completely over with, whereas those interested in the welfare of society and rehabilitating law violators feel that watchfulness over a convicted person's conduct for a period of years will bring about habits of good citizenship, the habit of staying with steady employment and develop in him a certain degree of pride in his good name, and accomplishes much toward releasing him in society as a well oriented citizen for the future. 

CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE SECTION 540 Assembly Bill 117 (33) Attachment of Occupied House Trailer. This bill incorpo rates the procedure outlined in the preceding item (C. C. P. 517;

Assembly Bill 118) in Section 540, relating to attachment levies. The same considerations there mentioned are applicable.

It is, however, to be noted that in the case of an attachment or execution levy, consideration must be given to Section 690.24. That section in effect gives the defendant or judgment debtor a claim to exemption for a house trailer actually occupied by him and his family, of a value not exceeding $2,500.

CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE SECTION 517 Assembly Bill 118 

(30) Claim and Delivery-Execution of Writ in Case of Occupied House Trailer. The purpose of this measure is to provide a practical and equitable procedure whereby a levying officer may execute a writ directing him to take possession of a house trailer in a claim and delivery proceeding, when it is being used for living purposes. 

At present there is no statute on the specific subject. Both at common law and in this State it is the general rule that a levying officer may not break into a dwelling house to execute civil process. (Foley v. 

Martin, 142 Cal. 256.) At the same time, a house trailer is personal property capable of being taken into possession in claim and delivery proceedings.

The procedure proposed in patterned, in part, upon the attachment procedure when a going business is attached. (C. C. P. 540.) A keeper may be placed in charge for at least two days, if the defendant consents. At the end of the two-day period, the officer then is to take the house trailer into possession, with authority to evict any occupant. The suggested amendments are believed fair both to the plaintiff and the defendant.

GOVERNMENT CODE SECTIONS 1981 AND 2003 Assembly Bill 374 (49) Amendment of General Tort Claims Statutes. There exists in this State a wide variety of "claims statutes," so-called. These have given rise to extensive litigation, often inducing a division of judicial opinion. Existing procedural requirements for filing tort claims with

the public body or the public employee, as a condition to recovery, have been characterized as "a bramble patch of legislation which, in many cases, completely chokes off the substantive rights of an unwary litigant." Ward, Requirements for Filing Claims Against Governmen tal Units in California, 38 Cal. Law Rev. (June, 1950), p. 259-272, at p. 271; also see (majority) opinion in Stewart v. McCollister, 37 C. (2d) 203, 207 (1951).

In addition to tort claims, the law is diverse on the necessity for, and manner of presentation of contract claims and claims arising out of the taking of property for public use without condemnation-socalled "inverse condemnation" claims.

Assembly Bill 374 is not directed to this over-all problem of simplification and uniformity but to immediate procedural reform in particular situations.

The two changes made by Assembly Bill 374 in substance are: first, wording is added to Government Code 1981, a general tort claims statute, to provide, with limitations, that where the fact of public employment is not known to the claimant, the time for presenting the claim required by the section is extended (based upon the principle of 

Stewart v. McCollister, 37 C. (2d) 203). An amendment for the same purpose is also made to Section 2003. Second, Section 1981 is amended by deleting present requirements for presentation of a claim to the employee, so as to provide, in effect, that filing of a claim with the public body will be sufficient notice for suit against the officer or employee individually. 

(1) Lack of Knowledge of Public Employment. In present form, neither Government Code Section 1981 nor Section 2003 (the latter enacted by 1951 Stats. Ch. 1630) makes any provision for an extension of time for filing a claim when the claimant does not know that the individual alleged to have committed the wrong is a public employee. The claimant is therefore not upon constructive notice of any special requirements for presenting a claim to the adverse party, or to a public body, within a limited time. 

The position, however, has been taken that even the claimant's common law right of action against the individual is so far restricted and qualified that he must at all events comply with the claims statute, or lose his right of action. This contention was emphatically rejected in 

Stewart v. McCollister, 37 C. (2d) at p. 207 (1951), where the majority of the court held that an action could be maintained upon the common law liability of an individual, notwithstanding his offer to prove that at the time he was driving his own automobile, he was in fact acting in the course of scope of public employment. 

Assembly Bill 374 is designed to incorporate the basic principle of the Stewart case (i.e., lack of knowledge of public employment) and at the same time provide for diligence and over-all time limit within. which a claimant is required to discover the fact of public employment. 

As to Section 1981, the 90-day period would not commence until "claimant knows, or in the exercise of due diligence should know, of the fact of public employment." However, the claim would be required to be filed, at all events, within one year after the accident occurred.

The precise form of amendment of Section 2003 has not been finally determined. The amendments will extend the time both for any required claim against the public body, and the claim against the indi vidual, under Section 1981. 

(2) Presentment of Claim to Employee. As indicated above, the other purpose of Assembly Bill 374 is to eliminate the requirement in Section 1981 for presentment of a claim to the individual officer or employee. Instead it is intended (by nondeletion of other wording) to substitute the requirement that any claim (that is necessary under the law) be filed with the governmental body only, as a condition to suit against the individual. The governmental body is enjoined with the duty of defending suits against individuals on claims of the character specified in Section 1981. (Goverment Code 2000; also see Government Code 2001, 2002.) As to such suits, notice of the public body is in effect notice to the real party in interest. In other cases, as where the injury was not done in the course and scope of public employment, the officer or employee should be in no different position than any other individual. Moreover, notice to the governmental body now appears the rule prescribed by Government Code 2003. This recent statute in terms applies to causes of action against employees of a district, county, city or city and county, for damages resulting from any negligence on the part of such employee while acting in the course and scope of his employment, and in terms requires presentment of claims to the public agency.

Claims statutes, so far as actions against individuals are concerned, are a modification of the common law liability of the individual. (See 

Veriddo v. Renaud, 35 C. 2d 263, 265; Stewart v. McCollister, 37 C. 2d 

203, 206). It is submitted, however, that they should not be carried to a point where compliance with the requirements is both cumbersome and unnecessary and where the formalities and time limits are such that the claimant may be deprived of his cause of action or day in court.

It is to be expressly noted that in certain situations at least Section 1981 has been interpreted as not requiring the presentment of a claim to the designated officer of a city when the action is directly against the public body. (Ansell v. City of San Diego, 35 C. 2d 76; Holm v. City of San Diego, 35 C. 2d 399.) It is not the purpose of the amendments here proposed to affect existing or future court interpretation of Section 1981, as to the necessity for present action or nonpresentation of claims, as a condition precedent to suit against the public body. 

CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE SECTION 682.1 Assembly Bill 375 

(35) Judgments-Interest Accrued After-Execution. The amendment proposed to Section 682.1 of the Code of Civil Procedure (prescribing the form of writ of execution) provides for a recital that interest has accrued on the judgment at the rate of 7 percent to the date of the execution, and for insertion of a specified sum in a blank left for that purpose.

•. 

These provisions are in substitution for wording in the present statutory form that "according to a cost bill after judgment filed herein" further sums have accrued since the entry of judgment, including "$(amount) accrued interest."

The purpose of the amendment is to permit the clerk to enter the amount of accrued interest after judgment.

While the present form in Section 682.1 refers to this item as one shown by a cost bill after judgment, Section 1033.7, relating to costs after judgment, does not expressly include the item of accrued interest. 

In one or more counties, new forms of cost bill in use do not include the accrued interest item under this heading. 

Interest on judgments is provided for by law. This item is not in the same category as expenses incurred after judgment, such as those for recording abstract of judgment, or sheriff's fees in prior levies, all of which are now covered by Section 1033.7. Hence, entry of interest by the clerk is appropriate.

PROBATE CODE SECTIONS 630 AND 630.5 Assembly Bill 377 (8) Probate Summary Proceedings. Probate Code Section 630 

presently provides a procedure whereby, upon affidavit only and without probate proceedings, the surviving spouse and certain designated relatives of the decedent may collect money due the decedent and receive other designated assets. In general, this procedure may be availed of where the decedent leaves no real property in this State, and the total value of his property in this State does not exceed $1,000. 

It is proposed to increase this amount to $2,000. This increase is advocated on the ground that the larger amount is more in keeping with present monetary values. The present monetary limit of $1,000 appears to date back to 1915 (see former C. C. P. Section 1454; 1915 

Stats. p. 532), although the procedure has been enlarged in other respects. Section 630 would also be amended by referring to the persons who may take advantage of its provisions in the plural as well as in the singular, so that if all interested persons join, the procedure will be available to them. A further amendment would add children of deceased brothers and sisters of the decedent to the class of designated heirs.

Probate Code Section 630.5 presently authorizes payment of a sum not exceeding $500 to the surviving spouse from money of the decedent on deposit in a bank. The provisions of this section are applicable regardless of the character of the estate left by the decedent, but is limited to cases where the value of the estate does not exceed $5,000. 

The bill under consideration would increase the amount of permitted estate to $10,000. It would also extend the right of withdrawal on affidavit of money in a savings and loan association and money evidenced by Postal Savings Certificates or United States savings bonds.

The amount which may be withdrawn upon affidavit would not be increased, however, but would be left at $500.

CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE SECTION 515 Assembly Bill 560 (28) Claim and Delivery-Exception to Sureties Upon Redelivery 

Bond. The purpose of this bill is to require the making of an exception to sureties when a redelivery bond is given by defendant in a claim and delivery proceeding if it is desired that the sureties be required to justify. Under present law, after the defendant has filed the bond, his sureties are required to justify, whether or not the plaintiff has excepted to them. Bailey v. Baker (1915) 28 C. A. 537; Bailey v. 

Security Trust Co., 34 C. A. 348, 353 (1917). See Prescott v. Farguhar, 135 C. A. 469; 12 C. A. 2d 286 (1933, 1936).

Present Section 513 allows the defendant two days after service of a copy of the affidavit of plaintiff and plaintiff's undertaking within which to give notice that he excepts to the sufficiency of the sureties. 

Otherwise, he is deemed to have waived all objections to them. 

Assembly Bill 560 makes this same procedure applicable to the defendant's redelivery bond provided for in Sections 514 and 515. 

VEHICLE CODE SECTION 420 Assembly Bill 561 (57) Financial Responsibility Law. Section 420 of the Vehicle 

Code provides that the Motor Vehicle Department shall not require a deposit of security for the benefit of any person in connection with a driver involved in an accident when "evidence has not been submitted by such person or on his behalf as to the extent of his injuries or the damage to his property within fifty (50) days following the date of accident."

In some cases this 50-day period is not sufficient for the injured person to submit such evidence. The bill would extend this period to 

90 days.

CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE SECTIONS 395, 397, 399 Assembly Bill 837 (26) Divorce-Nontransfer of Action to County of Defendant's 

Residence Place of Trial. An action for divorce is now required to be filed in the county in which plaintiff has resided for three months immediately preceding the commencement of the action. (C. C. Sec. 

128.) Yet it was early held, under the general statutes prescribing the place of trial of civil actions, that a divorce action is removable to the county of defendant's residence, upon timely motion. (Warner v. 

Warner, 100 Cal. 11-1893.) In divorce actions it is almost a "rule of thumb" for defendant to make a motion for such change of venue at the outset. Though the court has authority at this time to make orders for temporary alimony, etc. (C. C. P. Sec. 396b), it then has no alternative but to transfer the cause, as to all further proceedings, to the court of the county of defendant's residence. (See Ross v. Ross, 48 

C. A. 2d 72, 77.)

In contrast, present Section 396b of the Code of Civil Procedure provides that actions for personal injuries or injuries to property may be maintained in the county where the injury occurred; likewise, an action upon a contract for performance of an obligation in a particular

county may be maintained in the county where the contract was entered into or where it is to be performed. (C. C. P. Sec. 396b.) 

The result of present statutory law as construed in the Warner and subsequent cases is that in the majority of situations all but preliminary proceedings in the divorce action are required to be had in the county of defendant's residence. Generally, this removes the action from the locality where the parties maintained their matrimonial domicile and where witnesses and persons having knowledge of facts bearing upon various issues are apt to be found.

Assembly Bill 837 is designed to remedy this situation. In substance it provides that the county in which the divorce action is properly filed is a ("the") proper county for the trial of the action, thereby removing the defendant's absolute right to have venue changed to his county of residence. (C. C. P. Sec. 395 as proposed.) However, the trial court of the original county would be vested with discretion, upon proper showing, to order the cause transferred to the county of defendant's residence. Proposed subsection (5) of Section 397 provides that the court may order such transfer "where the ends of justice would be promoted by such change." This language, it is believed, empowers the trial court to consider all pertinent factors and not merely the "convenience of witnesses" as is now provided for actions generally, by subsection (3) of Section 397. If defendant makes a motion for transfer under proposed subsection (5), the trial court is nevertheless expressly empowered to make temporary orders for support, etc., as is now true under Section 396b.

Obviously, no rule can be framed that will provide satisfactorily for every factual situation bearing upon the most suitable place for conduct of divorce actions. An example sometimes cited is the case where the plaintiff moves to a distant county a comparatively short time before bringing the action. This, it is believed, is covered by the provisions of proposed Section 397 (5).

It is submitted that the amendments proposed correct the present defect in the law and yet give sufficient flexibility to permit trial courts to take care of the case where, under all the circumstances, further proceedings should be had in the county of defendant's residence, rather than in the county of plaintiff's residence. This solution is considered preferable to permitting the court in each instance to entertain a counter motion to retain the case in the county of commencement.

A minor amendment would be made to Code of Civil Procedure Section 399 to provide that the defendant shall pay the filing fee if transfer be ordered under proposed subsection (5) of Section 397.

CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE SECTIONS 681, 685 Assembly Bill 838 (34) Time for Issuance of Writ of Execution and Other Writs and 

Orders for Enforcement of Judgment. In 1953 the time for commencement of an action on a judgment was raised from 5 to 10 years. (C. C. P. Sec. 337.5, subpar. 3.)

One purpose of Assembly Bill 838 is to harmonize Sections 681 and 

685 of the Code of Civil Procedure with this change. Section 681, relating to the time when execution may issue as a matter of right, and

Section 685, relating to the issuance of execution after the lapse of the normal time, upon showing and order of court, would be amended by substituting 10 years for five years.

Assembly Bill 838 also changes the wording of Section 681 for the purpose of making clear that the time limitations apply to all orders and writs for enforcement of the judgment, not merely writs of execution as such. (See, e.g., orders specified in Section 684.) Under present wording it may be argued that Section 681 is not applicable to enforcement of a decree foreclosing a mortgage. (See, generally, Dorland v. Hansen, 81 Cal. 202; Laubitsch v. Roberdo, 43 A. C. 722, 729; and 

Knapp v. Rose, 32 C. 2d 530, to contrary.)

It is not intended to change the interpretation of Section 681 in its application to judgments or orders for payments of money in installments.

Section 674, providing for a five-year duration of a lien on real property created by recording an abstract of judgment, is unchanged. 

Such liens are drastic in nature, affecting many titles by reason of their general character. Considerations of policy therefor dictate that they should not remain unenforced or in effect for more than five years. 

CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE SECTIONS 956 AND 988h Assembly Bill 1225 

(43) Review for Prejudicial Error at Request of Respondent or

Other Person in Whose Favor Judgment Was Given. The amend ments to Code of Civil Procedure Sections 956 and 988h provide that on appeal a respondent or person in whose favor a judgment is given may ask for a review of the proceedings below to determine whether or not errors complained of by the appellant were prejudicial. This the respondent may not do now. The judgment being in his favor, he has nothing to appeal from and cannot have errors committed by the trial court reviewed on the appeal taken by his adversary. 

The problem arises particularly in nonjury cases, although the proposed statute is not so limited. For example: In an action on an alleged oral contract the defendant denies that there was a contract and pleads the statute of limitations. The court makes findings that there was a contract but that the action is barred by limitations. On appeal, error is found concerning the trial court's findings as to the statute of limitations yet the record shows that the findings on the merits, i.e., the existence of the contract, are unsupported. Application of the rule of nonreview of errors alleged by respondent requires the appellate court to reverse and the parties are thereby remitted to an unnecessary new trial. 

Another example is the litigation in Hudgins v. Standard Oil Co., 136 Cal. App. 44 (1933) and 5 Cal. App. 2d 618 (1935). In the first case the jury returned general verdicts for damages in favor of plaintiffs. It also returned special verdicts that plaintiff had executed a release in favor of defendant. The trial court set aside the general verdicts upon the ground that the special verdicts were inconsistent with the general verdicts and entered judgment for defendant. On appeal, this was held to be error and the trial court was directed to enter judgment for plaintiffs. On this first appeal, respondent (defendant) had urged that plaintiffs were guilty of contributory negligence, however,

Le 

CI el ex the appellate court declined to consider this contention, on the ground that respondent may not complain of errors against him. On the second appeal, the defendant now being an appellant on the same record, the contention as to contributory negligence was made again and the appellate court held that plaintiffs were guilty of such negligence as a matter of law. Judgment for plaintiff was therefore reversed. 

The purpose of the amendments proposed by Assembly Bill 1225 is to avoid such results to the end that litigation may be disposed of expeditiously.

The amendment of Code of Civil Procedure Section 956 applies to cases in the superior court while the amendment of Code of Civil Procedure Section 988h is applicable to municipal court appeals. 

Provision is made in the amendment of Code of Civil Procedure Section 956 to cover the situation where there is a partial record. In such cases the purpose of the proposed amendment might not be attained if the record did not contain matter necessary to make effective the review provided for. The language used is based upon Rule 4(b) of the Rules on Appeal. As this phase of the proposal would properly be covered by the Rules on Appeal, rather than by statute, it is provided that the statutory requirements pertaining to the record shall be effective until such time as the Judicial Council prescribes by rule the practice and procedure relating to the review.

Code of Civil Procedure Section 988h is amended so as to give respondents the same privilege in appeals from municipal courts. The practice and procedure in such appeals pending the promulgation of appropriate rules by the Judicial Council, would be that prescribed by Code of Civil Procedure Section 956, incorporated by general reference in Section 988h.

CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE SECTIONS 553 AND 946 

Assembly Bill 1289 

(31) Attachment-Release Effect of Motion for New Trial and 

Other Motions. Present statutes provide in substance that “if the defendant recovers judgment against the plaintiff," the attachment is released (C. C. P. Sec. 553); however, perfecting of an appeal and filing of an undertaking in the manner provided by Code of Civil Procedure Section 946 will continue the levy in force (C. C. P. Secs. 553, 946). Yet in the case of motions directed against the judgment, e.g., motion for a new trial or for vacation of the judgment, or for judgment notwithstanding the verdict, the levy is not continued in force. The matter is brought into focus by the comparatively recent decision of a district court of appeal in Henderson v. Drake, 255 P. 2d 875 (April 17, 1953), hearing granted; decision of Supreme Court reported in 42 C. 

2d 1 (Dec. 31, 1953). In that case a timely motion for a new trial was made and later granted but no appeal was taken. The ultimate decision in the case held that the attachment had not been dissolved, but upon the narrow ground that defendant had failed to give notice of entry of judgment.

The proposed amendments to Code of Civil Procedure Sections 553 

and 946 put the motions for a new trial, for vacation of the judgment, and for judgment notwithstanding the verdict, for the purpose of preserving the attachment, largely on a parity with an appeal. This is done

upon the ground that, on principle, there is no basic reason for applying a different rule to the situation where an appeal is taken, from that where a new trial, for example, is sought. 

If the levy is to be continued in these additional situations, then the question arises whether the undertaking filed by the moving party (plaintiff) to obtain the writ of attachment should suffice or whether a new undertaking should be required. The amendment continues such original undertaking but the trial court is empowered, upon motion of the defendant, to order an increase thereof in such amount as is justified by the detriment reasonably to be anticipated by continuing the attachment. If such an order increasing the undertaking is made, then the amount of the undertaking on ensuing appeal is to be the same; however, the trial court would be authorized, upon motion made within 60 days, to increase the undertaking on appeal, upon the same formula, i.e., the detriment reasonably to be anticipated by continuing the attachment. Where not so fixed, the amount of the undertaking on appeal would be twice the amount of the debt claimed, as at present. 

Provision is also made for justification, for discharge of the attachment upon failure to file undertakings, and for failure to justify. 

The bill is not necessarily in final form. 

CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE SECTION 690.26 Assembly Bill 1290 (37) Release of Earnings Held Exempt by Trial Court. Section 

690.11 of the Code of Civil Procedure now provides for entire, or onehalf, exemption of "earnings" of a defendant or judgment debtor, from levy of attachment or execution. The exemption depends upon various circumstances. For example, if the debt sued upon is not one incurred for the common necessaries of life, and the earnings are necessary for the use of the debtor's family residing in this State, all the earnings may usually be claimed as exempt. Even if the debt is for necessaries, one-half of the debtor's earnings may be claimed as exempt. 

For the purposes of Section 690.11, "earnings" are defined as those received for personal services rendered within 30 days prior to the levy.

A formal claim for exemption, however, must be made. (C. C. P. Sec. 

690.26(1); see Tinsley v. Bauer, 125 C. A. 2d 724, 732.) Section 690.26 of the Code of Civil Procedure provides the procedure for making, contesting and determining claims to exemption.

While Section 690.26 provides for prompt hearing of contested claims, present wording of the section requires the levying officer to hold the property levied upon until the time for appeal has expired and the judgment thus has become final (unless the right of appeal is waived by the plaintiff or judgment creditor). The normal time to appeal from a superior court judgment is 60 days (Rules on Appeal, Rule 2(a)), and from a municipal court judgment, 30 days (Rules on Appeal from Municipal Courts, Rule 2(a)). Appeals in this type of proceeding are comparatively rare. In consequence, earnings of a debtor may be unavailable to him for a period as long as 60 days, even though he has obtained a judgment of the court hearing the matter, that they are exempt under the law.

To obviate this hardship, Assembly Bill 1290 amends subsection 11 of Section 690.26, to provide that if the claim to exemption under Section 690.26 be allowed by judgment of the trial court, the earnings so exempted shall be released at the end of three days, unless: (a) otherwise ordered by the (trial) court; or (b) the levying officer shall have been served with a copy of notice of appeal. 

It may be noted subsection 2 of Section 690.26 now provides that when an affidavit of exemption is filed, averring earnings to be exempt under Section 690.11, the defendant or judgment debtor is entitled to an immediate release of one-half such earnings. The creditor is not permitted to delay such release by a contest. The amendment proposed, therefore, will be applicable to the remaining one-half earnings, in the particular circumstances here described. 

It may also be noted that Section 690.27 of the Code of Civil Procedure, enacted in 1953, incorporates the provisions of Section 690.26 and other exemption laws by general reference, where certain "tax levies" are made. It seems therefore the amendment proposed herein will also be applicable where a judgment exempting earnings of a “tax debtor" is rendered.

VEHICLE CODE SECTIONS 404.1, 404.2, 404.3 AND 404.4 Assembly Bill 1357 (56) Service of Process Outside State-Owners and Operators of Motor Vehicles. Section 404 of the Vehicle Code provides that the acceptance by a nonresident of the rights and privileges conferred upon him by the Vehicle Code or any use of the highways of the State is equivalent to the appointment by the nonresident of the Director of Motor Vehicles as his agent for the service of process in an action or proceeding against the nonresident growing out of an accident or collision resulting from the operation of a motor vehicle upon highways of this State. Yet the Vehicle Code does not extend such means for effectuating service of process in the case of the person who is a resident at the time a motor vehicle accident occurs but who becomes a nonresident prior to the service of process. This may lead to the precipitate filing of an action and the service of process issued thereon or the loss of the opportunity to make service while the party is within the State. 

The purpose of Assembly Bill 1357 is to put the person who is a resident at the time of the motor vehicle accident in the same position as if he had been a nonresident. It is based upon the same foundation as Section 404, i.e., that the State may attach reasonable conditions to the privileges granted by the Vehicle Code. The bill creates no substantive rights; it is procedural in character. 

Four new Vehicle Code sections are proposed: (1) Section 404.1 re lates to service of process on a resident who accepts a certificate of ownership or registration. It is provided that this constitutes consent that personal service may be made upon him at any place where he may be found whether or not he is then a resident of the State in any action brought in this State upon a cause of action arising in this State out of his ownership or operation of the vehicle. (2) Section 404.2 relates to acceptance of an operator's license and applies a corresponding consent subject to the same limitations: in an action brought in California upon a cause of action arising in this State out of his driving a motor vehicle

upon any public road or highway in California. (3) Section 404.3 prescribes the manner of service and the time to appear, the latter being 

60 days in case of service outside the State. (4) Section 404.4 expressly provides that the time the nonresident is out of the State is not to be excluded (as in cases under C. C. P. 351) in computing the time limited for commencement of the action except where the nonresident's place of residence outside the State is unknown to plaintiff and cannot be ascertained through the exercise of due diligence. Note: the form of 

Section 404.4, relating to the running of the statute of limitations, is not necessarily final.

CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE SECTIONS 2051 AND 2065 Assembly Bill 1358 (46) Impeachment of a Witness by Reason of Prior Conviction of Felony-Change in Rule and Procedure. Assembly Bill 1358 pro poses a fundamental change in the rule which permits a witness in a civil or criminal case to be impeached by showing that he has been previously convicted of a felony. The rule, to which certain exceptions have been made by statute or statutory construction, has been part of our written law since 1872.

In addition, Assembly Bill 1358 proposes a procedural reform in this subject matter.

Both changes are advocated as important improvements in the administration of justice. The reasons and amendments in brief, are: 

Fairness of the Impeachment Rule Generally

There can be little doubt that disclosure of the fact that a witness has been previously convicted of a felony is usually highly prejudicial. 

Particularly is this true in the case of a person accused of crime who takes the stand in his own defense. (See infra.) In other situations, for example, the witness in a criminal case, or a witness or party-witness in a civil case, disclosure of that fact is not only prejudicial but often introduces collateral issues that impede, rather than aid, the search for the truth.

The basic weakness in the rule itself and the vast increase in the number and type of offenses declared to be felonies point to the need for reform.

Many crimes, in fact those which probably would create the most prejudice against a party or witness, do not at all indicate any lack of probative untrustworthiness. Typical are crimes of violence and passion, for example, murder, manslaughter and assault. Certainly, without a knowledge of the circumstances surrounding the commission of such crimes and the motives which led to them, it cannot be said with any assurance that the criminal involved is in all likelihood a liar. 

Creation of numerous new statutory offenses, to meet the demands of a complex society accentuates the problem. 

It is also well known that the present impeachment rule itself tends to the suppression of information and testimony, for its very existence acts as a deterrent to witnesses and potential witnesses. 

For these and other reasons, it is proper that impeachment by felony conviction should be carefully limited.

Impeachment of a Defendant in a Criminal Case 

In a criminal case, disclosure of the accused's previous conviction of a felony, for obvious reasons, is unusually prejudicial to his defense. 

He is, however, also highly prejudiced if, in order to avoid that disclosure, he does not take the stand, thus subjecting himself, under the law of this State, to adverse comment from the prosecutor and the court. An accused should not be placed in that drastic dilemma. It is to be observed that even if the impeaching question may not be asked, nor proof introduced, adequate criteria still exist for determining whether the accused is testifying truthfully. Demeanor on the stand, the nature, consistency and probability of his testimony, the accused's responses to other types and phases of cross examination and the like may be considered. In such situation, i. e., the testimony of an accused, the value of the "felony-impeachment" is clearly outweighed by the prejudice resulting from its use. Assembly Bill 1358, therefore, excludes a defendant in a criminal case from this type of impeachment. 

It is not proposed, however, that any change be made in the present law or procedure relating to proof of prior convictions for the purpose of fixing the degree of crime or the nature of the punishment therefor. 

Amendments Proposed 

First, the class of felonies conviction of which is a ground of impeachment, is limited to those which have a direct bearing upon trustworthiness in testimony. Thus, it is provided in the revised wording of Section 2051 that "an intent to defraud, cheat, deceive, lie, steal or embezzle" must be an essential "element of the felony" as defined by the law of the jurisdiction in which it was committed. 

Second, the conviction of the felony (of the class described) may not be shown until the party proposing to examine the witness or offering the record of conviction has satisfied the court in proceedings had outside the presence of the jury (1) that the felony was of the class described; and (2) that competent evidence of the record of conviction is available for admission in evidence. 2-L-1691 It is recognized that it is frequently difficult, in the short time usually available, to determine if a witness has a criminal record of the type material. It is nevertheless believed unwise to permit the impeaching question to be asked unless the questioner is prepared to prove the fact. 

The mere asking of the question can, and usually will, be most prejudicial; and the prejudice so induced is of a kind which no instruction or admonition can effectively or completely remove. Furthermore, if impeachment is limited only to certain kinds of felonies, the asking of the question may produce an answer admitting one of the excluded felonies, thus defeating the beneficial purpose of the revised law. 

Third, as noted, impeachment of an accused in a criminal case, upon this particular ground, would be prohibited. 

Fourth, Sections 2051 and 2065 would be amended in a respect not directly related to the changes above advocated by deleting the words "based upon a certificate of rehabilitation" following the words "full and unconditional pardon." The deletion of these words would place full and unconditional pardons in a single category, for purposes of

the sections. Many states do not have a procedure for obtaining a certificate of rehabilitation when such a pardon is granted. In other instances, such a pardon may be granted upon a finding of innocence. On balance, it is believed the act of executive clemency should be sufficient to prevent impeachment, provided only the pardon is "full and unconditional."

CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE SECTION 949a Assembly Bill 1617 (42) Minors-Orders Awarding or Affecting Custody-Permission to Leave Jurisdiction-Effect of Appeal. Under present law, and by force of general statutory provisions, an appeal from an order awarding custody of a minor child automatically stays further proceedings in the trial court. (Sec. 946 and 949, C. C. P.; In re Barr, 39 C. 2d 25; Lerner v. Superior Court, 38 C. 2d 676; Gantner v. Gantner, 38 C. 2d 691.)

This statutory rule has been substantially criticized. Thus, if there is a modification of a custody order previously entered, the very reason for the modification in nearly every case will be that the trial judge has determined that the welfare of the child demands a change. Yet the mere perfecting of an appeal by the losing party will delay execution. of the order, sometimes for very substantial periods. As a result the child is subjected to a continuance of the same conditions which brought about the change ordered. The rule is conducive to appeals for considerations other than those relating strictly to the merits of the appeal. 

In actions for divorce and separate maintenance, the statutes expressly empower the court to make, vacate and modify orders for custody, guided by what appears to be for the best interests of the child. 

Civ. C., Sec. 138). However, this discretionary authority comes to an abrupt end, under present law, upon an appeal being taken. 

During the period of the appeal, the appellate court has power to transfer custody pending the appeal. The power, it is believed, is not one that in the nature of things can be expected to be exercised with frequency or in any but extreme cases, under the present legislative mandate.

The solution which has been suggested in some earlier decisions, that of making the child a ward of the juvenile court pending the appeal, does not appear practical nor in the interests of the child. 

While the trial court's findings and order may upon occasion be erroneous, and a reversal therefore required, in the vast majority of cases, it is believed, the converse is true. 

Assembly Bill 1617 in proposed Section 949a in effect gives presumptive validity to the order of the trial court, so far as immediate execution of the custody order is concerned. The burden is placed upon the appellant for this limited purpose. 

The section proposed, however, provides that the trial court, in its discretion may stay execution. Moreover, the appellate court's power to issue an appropriate writ or order (including an injunction) is expressly recognized.

The proposed new section also deals specifically with provisions of an order or judgment permitting the child to be removed from the jurisdiction. These would be stayed for a period of 30 days by operation of law. Likewise this matter is subject to power of the appellate

court to act in aid of its jurisdiction. When an appeal has been taken or is to be taken, removal from the jurisdiction seriously affects both the appellate court's jurisdiction and the right of appeal. (See e.g., Lerner v. Superior Court, 38 C. 2d 676; Gantner v. Superior Court, 38 C. 2d 688.)

It is expressly to be noted that the new section is not limited to custody orders in domestic relations cases but would apply "in any civil action, action filed under the Juvenile Court Law, or special proceeding."

CIVIL CODE SECTION 25 Assembly Bill 1618 (1) Domestic Relations Litigation; Maintenance by Wife 18 Years of Age or Over. In 1953 the Legislature amended Section 25, Civil 

Code, to provide, in substance, that "for the purpose of maintaining or defending an action affecting her marital status," a lawfully married female of 18 or over is deemed an adult. 

While the bills to this effect (S. B. 939 and A. B. 1619) were not 

State Bar bills, they had the support of the State Bar. 

Subsequently, questions have been suggested as to the application of the 1953 amendment in various situations. Orders for child custody and for support are but two examples. Are they within the expression "action affecting * * * marital status'?

The purpose of Assembly Bill 1618 is to add more specific language to clarify the 1953 amendment and carry out what is believed to have been its general purpose, namely, to make a lawfully married female of 18 or over an adult, for all purposes, in domestic relations actions and proceedings brought by or against her.

If the words, "affecting her marital status, were to be given a restricted or narrow meaning, an anomalous situation would often arise. 

One part of the litigation would be controlled by the wife herself. The other part would be subject to the control of a guardian appointed for her. Such a situation would tend to result in undesirable conflicts and uncertainty and should be avoided.

The amendment proposed adds to Section 25 after "action affecting her marital status": 

"including therein any action or proceeding involving her support or custody or the support or custody of children of the marriage, or determination of property rights.' 

PENAL CODE SECTIONS 647ɑ AND 653 (NEW) Assembly Bill 1620 (18) Vagrancy-Loitering About Schools. Penal Code Section 

647a presently provides that any person who loiters about a school or public place at or near which school children attend is a vagrant and is punishable by fine of not more than $500 or by imprisonment in the county jail for not exceeding six months. Penal Code Section 290 requires any person convicted under Section 647a to register with the sheriff or chief of police in the same way sex deviates are required to register.

It is desired to amend the present law in such a way that offenders hanging around a school, who are not sex deviates or in any way involved in sex activities, may be prosecuted but will not be classified with those mentioned in Section 290 of the Penal Code. Many of these loiterers are merely former students who are waiting for a girl friend or for school hours to close so that they can go upon the playground. 

In no way are they sexual perverts. 

Assembly Bill 1620 will amend the law so that persons who are not sex deviates but who loiter about any school or public place at or near which school children attend may be prosecuted as vagrants but will not be subject to registration under Section 290 of the Penal Code as sex deviates.

LABOR CODE SECTIONS 3860 AND 3861 Assembly Bill 1626 (52) Workmen's Compensation-Attorneys' Fees. In the case of 

R. E. Spriggs, Inc. v. Industrial Accident Commission, 43 C. 2d 785 (May, 1954) an employee was injured in the course of his employment owing to the negligence of a third party. Without filing suit against the third party or notifying his employer or its insurance carrier, the injured employee made a settlement with the third party and executed a full release of all claims against it for $2,100, paying his attorney $700 for his services. In proceedings before the Industrial Accident 

Commission the commission credited the employer with $1,400 against a compensation award, on the theory that the employer was not entitled to credit for the amount of the fee paid to the employee's attorney which benefited both employer and employee, because the 1949 amendments to Sections 3856 and 3861 of the Labor Code, applying to third party actions that proceed to judgment, should be deemed to repeal Labor Code Section 3860 (which applies to settlements before judgment) by implication. The Supreme Court rejected this contention and directed the commission to credit the employer with the full $2,100, declaring that "when the third party's liability has been settled, either by the employee or the employer there is no provision for reimbursement to either of them for his legal expenses." The court, moreover, suggested, quoting from Dobbs v. Stellar (30 C. 2d 496): "if there is to be any change in these statutory provisions defining the rights of the parties, the suggestion for such change should be addressed to the 

Legislature rather than to the courts. 

The present bill is responsive to that suggestion and is intended to carry out the principle that each party who recovers in the personal injury action should bear his proportionate share of the fees of the attorney whose advocacy made the recovery possible, and that neither party should be required to pay for work done for the other's benefit. 

The amendment to Section 3860 also removes therefrom language which at one point makes the section applicable only to releases and settlements "after action and before judgment" and yet later refers to "settlement without suit." The application of the sections is clarified by making it clear that it applies to any release or settlement under the chapter.

LABOR CODE SECTIONS 4903, 5002, AND 5003; UNEMPLOYMENT INSURANCE CODE SECTION 2417 Assembly Bill 1627 (53) Workmen's Compensation-Unemployment Compensation Dis ability Benefits-Attorney Fees. The general purpose of these amendments is to clarify the present provisions in Labor Code 4903 concerning the extent of the lien given by subdivision (f) of that section; to facilitate legal representation in cases where unemployment disability payments have been made for disabilities which the Industrial Accident Commission later finds to have an industrial causation by adjusting the lien priority under that section; to require a lien claimant under subdivision (f) of the section to file in the workmen's compensation proceedings all medical reports relating to the person to whom unemployment compensation disability benefits were paid; and to provide that a lien claimant under that and other sections of the Labor Code may compromise its claim and to provide a procedure therefor.

Unemployment disability benefits may be paid to an employee in cases where it is uncertain whether the claim is one properly within the provisions for unemployment disability, or, as an industrially connected disability, is one payable under workmen's compensation. Labor 

Code Section 4903 provides, however, that the Industrial Accident Commission may allow as a lien against any amount awarded as workmen's compensation (subd. (f)) "The amount of unemployment compensation disability benefits which have been paid under or pursuant to the Unemployment Insurance Act in those cases where, pending a determination under Division 4 (Workmen's Compensation) * * * 

there was uncertainty whether such benefits were payable under that act or payable hereunder.” 2 Assembly Bill 1627 provides that "such lien shall not be allowed against permanent disability compensation benefits for unemployment compensation disability benefits paid for any period of unemployment due primarily to a disability which the commission finds was not due to an injury or disease arising out of and in the course of the employment." This provision, then, is intended to protect payments for industrially incurred permanent disability against any lien on account of unemployment disability payments for nonindustrial disability coinciding with the period during which such permanent disability compensation payments fall due.

In order to assure representation and the prosecution of claims having an industrial causation, the bill provides that lien claims for unemployment disability benefits paid shall be subordinate to the lien for attorney's fees and disbursements. Where the Industrial Accident Commission finds there is an industrially caused disability and makes an award therefor upon which a lien accrues for unemployment disability benefits which have been paid, the award may be so far consumed by the lien that the attorney who has prosecuted the claim receives no reimbursement. In such cases he has performed a substantial service for the lien claimant (the Department of Employment) who has not cooperated in the action. This situation is aggravated by the fact that the cases frequently are difficult, as might be supposed from the language of Labor Code itself: "uncertainty whether such benefits were

payable under that act (unemployment) or payable hereunder (workmen's compensation)." The purpose of the amendment is to ameliorate this condition.

In order to make a determination on all the facts subdivision (f) of Section 4903 is further amended to provide that the lien claimant shall serve and file all medical reports in its possession relating to the person to whom unemployment compensation benefits were paid. Unemployment Insurance Code Section 2714 is amended to conform. 

Section 5002 Labor Code, relating to releases and compromises and procedure thereon is revised to cover the situation where any lien is claimed and any lien claimant has failed to join in and sign the release or compromise. Procedure is established for service and objection and for approval by the Industrial Accident Commission; if the lien claimant objects to release or compromise the commission may approve the release or compromise except its provision for the lien claimant, whose rights by way of subrogation are preserved. 

PROBATE CODE SECTION 951.1 Assembly Bill 1628 (14) Funeral Expenses and Expenses of Last Illness. This bill, in the form introduced, establishes the rule that funeral expenses and expenses of last illness are primarily payable from the estate of a deceased spouse. It brings the law into conformity with the Inheritance 

Tax Act, which allows a deduction thereof, regardless of the ability of the surviving spouse to pay such expenses. (See: Rev. and Tax. C., Section 13986.)

At common law, the expenses are the obligation of the husband. (In re Weringer's Estate, 100 Cal. 346.) However, certain decisions established the rule that if the wife's (separate) estate is ample and the financial ability of the husband limited, the probate court in its discretion may make an allowance for the husband from the wife's estate. (Estate of Mathewson, 181 Cal. 452; Brezzo v. Brangero, 51 C. A. 79.)

It was indicated a similar discretionary power existed in the probate court as to expenses of last illness ordinarily payable by the husband. (Medros v. Kohn, 68 C. A. 367, 370.)

A recent decision of a District Court of Appeal construes Section 

950, Probate Code, and Sections 7700-7701, Health and Safety Code, to indicate a legislative intent to abrogate the common law rule as to funeral expenses and make them primarily payable out of the wife's estates. (Estate of Dennis, 110 C. A. (2d) 667 (1952); also see Estate of Kemmerer, 114 C. A. 2d 810 (1952)). In the form introduced, the bill is limited to unpaid expenses. It is not intended to change the rule requiring the presentation of claims.

NOTE: As of the time this report was prepared, the Board of Governors had not finally acted upon this measure. 

CORPORATIONS CODE SECTION 1904 Assembly Bill 1731 (47) Domestic Corporations-Shares Outstanding When Reduction 

Surplus Is Created. Section 1904 provides that the stated capital of a corporation shall not be reduced to an amount less than the aggregate par value of all par value shares without liquidation preference to

remain outstanding after such reduction and the aggregate amount of the liquidation preferences upon involuntary liquidation of preferred shares with or without par value to remain outstanding after such reduction. Section 1905 provides that the adjustment of outstanding par value shares to correspond with the stated capital as reduced may be effected by specified means, including redemption, purchase or retirement of shares. Section 1906 provides that surplus arising from reduction of stated capital may be used to purchase outstanding shares, where only one class of shares is outstanding, if such purchase is authorized by the holders of two-thirds of the outstanding shares other than those to be purchased. Thus it appears from the three sections that it is intended to permit a corporation to apply reduction surplus to the purchase of outstanding shares under the conditions specified, but if Section 1904 is interpreted literally, it is impossible to create the reduction surplus which is to be applied to the purchase of the outstanding shares, since the shares to be purchased and retired are necessarily outstanding at the time of the reduction of stated capital. 

The proposed amendment of Section 1904 merely clarifies the language of the section to exclude from outstanding shares those shares which are to be purchased and retired by the application of the reduction surplus to be created by the reduction of stated capital and thus gives effect to the legislative intent. It is believed that no substantive change in the law will result from this proposed amendment. 

CORPORATIONS CODE SECTION 25153 Assembly Bill 1732 (48) Corporate Securities Act-Conditional Subscriptions. Sub ject to statutory safeguards, subscriptions may be taken for shares of a corporation prior to incorporation. Corporations Code Section 25153 expressly provides that no provision of the Corporate Securities Act prohibits such a transaction. But there is no provision expressly exempting from the act post-incorporation subscriptions for shares made prior to application for a permit. Many times informal arrangements take place in advance of a permit, and necessarily so. In other cases, agreements are made between two or more parties of a conditional nature expressly subject to obtaining approval of the Corporation Commissioner. The breadth of the Corporate Securities Act, particularly the scope of the definition of sale (Corp. C. 25009) and provisions governing the necessity for a permit (Corp. C. 25500), appear to leave a wide area for later civil litigation, even on largely technical grounds, in respect to such frequent preliminary activities. The amendment of 

Corporations Code 25153 proposed by this bill puts the post-incorporation and the preincorporation subscription on a parity, both being subject to the same safeguards, for the purpose of removing those preliminary activities from their present ambiguous position. 

The bill thus removes the present artificial distinction between preincorporation and post-incorporation subscription agreements; noncorporate issuers are placed on the same basis as corporate issuers; a definite period, 90 days after acceptance of a subscription, is specified for making application for and obtaining a permit.

The bill strengthens and makes more particular the present prohibition against the payment of consideration prior to issuance of a permit, the amendment reading "No portion of the consideration shall be paid or transferred to or deposited with anyone unless and until a permit has been granted by the commissioner authorizing the sale and issuance of such securities. This would preclude payment escrows, and good faith deposits." Another new clause requires the subscription to state on its face the provision just quoted concerniing payment of consideration. 99 "C 

REVENUE AND TAXATION CODE SECTION 13985 Assembly Bill 1733 (59) Inheritance Tax Act-Deductible Items Paid or Established After Order Fixing Inheritance Tax-Modification of Order. Present Section 13985 of the Revenue and Taxation Code (Inheritance Tax 

Act) now provides that after an order fixing inheritance tax has been made, the order on petition, after notice to the State Controller, may be modified equitably, if a "debt" is thereafter established. 

This measure makes clear that, for the purposes of the statute, the word "debt" is not to be applied in a restrictive sense, but applies to any item which is deductible in computing the proper inheritance tax. 

Estate taxes payable to the Federal Government are one example. In this situation, particularly, the final amount of estate tax liability may not be determined until after an order fixing inheritance tax has been made. No reason appears why this and other deductions allowable under the State Inheritance Tax Act should not be allowed in full, even though not a debt" in the technical sense. Otherwise, the full deductions provided for in other parts of the Inheritance Tax Act may be lost.
