66 The measure provides, in the first sentence of Section 13985, that if an item which is allowable as a deduction in computing the tax * * * is established or paid” after the order fixing inheritance tax, the order may on petition after notice to the Controller be modified equitably. Present wording reads: "if a debt is established," etc.

The second sentence of the present section now provides that there is no limitation within which "a debt may be established," for the purposes of the act, but that any petition for modification of the order after "the establishment of the debt" must be filed within one year after the "debt is established." There would be substituted wording that there is no limitation within which a deductible item may be "established or paid" but that any petition for modification "after establishment or payment" of the item shall be filed within one year after "such establishment or payment, whichever is later.” 

The words "whichever is later" are intended to eliminate uncertainty as to when the statute of limitations commences to run. In some situations, particularly determination of federal estate tax liability, an "item" may be established in advance of payment. When this occurs may be a matter of uncertainty. For this reason, and also in the interest of making the full deductions available, the longer period, of the two possible alternatives, has been adopted.

PROBATE CODE SECTION 541.1 Assembly Bill 1736 

(7) Bond-Deposit of Money or Securities in Bank or Trust Com pany Prior to Appointment of Personal Representative. This bill proposes a clarifying amendment to Probate Code Section 541.1. That section provides in substance that, in determining the amount of a bond to be required of an executor or administrator, the probate court may take into consideration money or securities which have been or will be deposited in a bank or trust company in this State, upon the condition that they may not be withdrawn except upon court order. The court in its discretion may make an order to that effect. 

Such a deposit and order usually results in a smaller bond, since the property so deposited is subject to withdrawal only upon court order. 

Unnecessary expense in administration of the estate is thereby avoided. 

A question, however, has been raised in some quarters of the authority of a bank or trust company to accept such deposit of money or securities and to make the nonwithdrawal agreement, before a personal representative is appointed and has qualified. 

The purpose of Assembly Bill 1736 is to eliminate any hiatus in the law in this respect and expressly to authorize banks and trust companies to accept such deposit and make such agreement in advance of appointment of the personal representative. This will avoid unnecessary steps and expense, since the probate court at the time the amount of the bond is to be fixed, will have before it the fact of such deposit and agreement. 

If the technical interpretation above mentioned should be adopted, under present law the bond would have to be fixed at a higher amount, contrary to the purposes of the section, subject to a reduction thereafter upon petition; or various informal arrangements would have to be made if the personal representative is to qualify promptly under a bond that takes into consideration the money or securities already deposited upon the conditions stated.

The purpose of the amendment is to avoid these difficulties. 

It is to be noted that Financial Code Section 1586 contains general authority for the court to credit such a deposit by a fiduciary and to make an order reducing bond.

PENAL CODE SECTIONS 1192.1 AND 1192.2 (BOTH NEW) Assembly Bill 1797 (20) Pleas of Guilty-Specifying Degree of Crime. This bill would permit a defendant, in pleading guilty to a crime, to specify the degree of the crime to which he pleads guilty if the crime is one divided into degrees. Such a plea, to become effective, would have to be consented to by the district attorney and approved by the court. 

The suggested change in the law is recommended for the reason that where a crime is divided into degrees it is now impossible to secure one of the chief objectives of a plea of guilty: certainly on both sides that the defendant will be convicted of a lesser offense. This is especially inconvenient in homicide cases where murder has three subdivisions: (1) first degree with death penalty; (2) first degree with life imprisonment; (3) second degree. It is impossible to fix either the second or the third on a plea of guilty. (On the other hand, a plea of guilty to

manslaughter would be binding.) The result is first that a substantial number of cases are tried when the degree and/or punishment are the only real issues, and which could otherwise be disposed of on a plea of guilty; and second there are a certain number of time consuming coram nobis proceedings where the defendant claims he was assured of a lesser penalty but received a higher one. These could also be avoided if the defendant could make a binding plea to the degree and/or punishment. Capital cases and statutory rape are now the only crimes in which the jury makes a binding recommendation on punishment. 

PENAL CODE SECTION 3045 Assembly Bill 1798 (23) Paroles Effect of Pardon Upon Previous Conviction. Penal 

Code Section 3045 presently provides that any sentence based on a conviction of which the person has previously been pardoned "on the express ground that he was not guilty" shall not be counted as a previous conviction. This bill would strike the portion quoted so that a pardon on any ground would have such effect. 

This amendment was proposed because of inconsistent effects given to certain pardons. For instance, where a person has received full and unconditional pardon on the ground that he was innocent of the crime of which he was convicted, such conviction cannot be used subsequently against him to increase the minimum punishment as set forth in Section 3024 of the Penal Code. On the other hand, this same individual could be impeached if he ever testified as a witness under Section 2051 of the Code of Civil Procedure. This was so held by the 

Supreme Court. Section 2051 of the Code of Civil Procedure provides that a person who has been previously convicted and thereafter institutes proceedings for rehabilitation and is granted a full and unconditional pardon by a judgment of rehabilitation, cannot be impeached as a witness by virtue of the prior conviction. But it does not relieve him of the burden of mandatory increased punishment for a later offense. It is felt that a full and unconditional pardon should have the same effect, both as to subsequent punishment and as it relates to testifying as a witness. Relieving a person of the burden of having to carry a mandatory increase in minimum punishment for a subsequent offense does not prevent the Adult Authority from taking into consideration the prior crime in assessing the penalty for the second offense. It often happens that there is no similarity between these offenses. For instance, suppose the original offense was for checks without sufficient funds and the subsequent offense was manslaughter emanating out of an accident in which some negligence was attributed to the defendant. Here there would be no rational reason why he should suffer a minimum sentence of five years because of the first offense. 

CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE SECTION 714 Assembly Bill 1799 (38) Examination of Judgment Debtors Nulla Bona Returns. 

The purpose of this legislation is to remove the necessity of waiting 10 days for a nulla bona return in order to institute supplemental proceedings to examine the judgment debtor.

For an order of examination to be issused, it is presently required by Code of Civil Procedure Section 714 that an execution against the property of the judgment debtor be returned unsatisfied. Code of Civil 

Procedure Section 683 provides that the writ of execution may be made returnable not less than 10 days after its receipt by the officer to whom it is directed.

The proposed legislation provides that when no property is found, the officer may make his return to that effect forthwith. This will enable the creditor to obtain an order for supplemental proceedings without awaiting the expiration of 10 days.

CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE SECTION 763 Assembly Bill 1802 (39) Actions for Partitions-Property Held in Trust. The pur pose of this bill is to clarify the provisions of Sections 763 and 781 of the Code of Civil Procedure in their application to trusts. At present, these sections provide that where the property involved in a partition action is subject to a life estate and the remainder is a contingent remainder, the court "must order the sale thereof," and may direct the proceeds of the sale to be paid to a trustee appointed by the court. 

The view is taken by some persons that these statutory provisions control in the case of a trust with a life beneficiary and contingent remainder. As a consequence, they say, the original trust is terminated by a partition action as to that particular asset. There is no reason, however, why this arbitrary result should follow when the undivided. interest (or the proceeds, if a sale is necessary for other reasons) can be administered in the original trust. 

The bill provides that where property or an interest therein is subject to an express trust, the court may, but shall not be required to, order a sale thereof; and in the event of either a partition or sale, the property or proceeds allotted to the trustee of such express trust shall be held by him upon the same trust. 

PENAL CODE SECTIONS 4852.01, 4852.02, 4852.03, 4852.06 AND 4852.18 Assembly Bill 1803 (24) Pardons Procedure for Obtaining-Rehabilitation. The pro posed amendment will change the law in two respects, namely: (1) permit the petitioner to file his application for rehabilitation either in the county of his residence or in the county in which the conviction was had (if he is not residing there at the time he avails himself of this proceeding); and (2) it will clarify the period when rehabilitation starts.

Under the present law, a person who has been convicted and sentenced to the state penitentiary may, upon his release, file his notice of intention that he is going to apply for a petition of rehabilitation at a later date. Before he can file his petition, however, he must have been discharged from parole and he must have been a resident of the county in which he files his petition for at least three years. 

The Judicial Council fixes the period of rehabilitation, which ranges from three years to seven years and two months. A person who has been released from prison and has moved to a different county from

that in which he was convicted frequently finds, after the long waiting period required by law, that the attending disgrace of a conviction has more or less subsided and it is very embarrassing to initiate proceedings, particularly in a county where he has built up a good reputation for himself, perhaps even in a business way. Many of the most deserving refuse to initiate proceedings when they are told they have to file a notice of intention with the county clerk in the county in which they reside, saying the harm ensuing more than offsets many benefits.

Permitting a person to file his petition in the convicting county even though he is not residing there would in a large way offset this. Under the proposed amendment he would still have to reside in a county for three years before filing a petition and give full notice to the police officer or sheriff of the resident county, but the court proceedings would be held in the convicting county. No one can be misled by this because all authorities will have full notice and just as thorough an investigation is available. Experience has shown that some of the most worthy persons of this class refuse to initiate any proceedings if they are compelled to file a petition in the county of their residence, particularly if they are business men, but they would file a petition if permitted to file in the convicting county.

As to the other phase, under the present law the Attorney General has ruled that the period of rehabilitation does not commence until the notice of intention is filed. There are two evils to this ruling: (1) in a large class of crimes it is required that the proceedings be kept open for an unreasonable length of time, seven years and two months, and no possible good can be gained in keeping these proceedings open so long; (2) a person may have been off parole ten years or even longer and led a model life. To require him to wait longer, seven years and two months, is unreasonable and often serves as a barrier toward initiating proceedings. Experience has shown the record of his conduct is surveyed very thoroughly anyway from the date of his release from the penitentiary up until the time of the court hearing, whether his notice of intention was filed three years or seven years and two months earlier.

In other words, the period between notice of intention and filing the petition is not the only period covered by the investigation. It seems that three years' notice would be ample time for the officers to observe the present conduct of a person, as well as to give them ample time to run down the record of his conduct for preceding years. Three years seems ample time to keep a proceeding of this sort open. The proposed amendments would clarify the law on this point. 

PENAL CODE SECTION 987a Assembly Bill 1804 (19) Attorneys' Fees-Criminal Trials. Penal Code Section 987a presently provides that in any case in which counsel is assigned to defend a person charged with crime, such counsel, except in a county where there is a public defender, shall receive a reasonable attorney's fee which shall be determined by the court. In some cases, however, where there is a public defender, the public defender cannot properly represent the person accused because of conflict of interest or other reasons. The bill provides that in such case the court may assign other

counsel to defend the accused and that such counsel shall be entitled to receive a reasonable attorney's fee fixed by the court, which fee shall be paid out of the general funds of the county. 

PROBATE CODE, NEW DIVISION 5 (SECTIONS 1701-2207) Assembly Bill 1887 

(17) Conservatorship. Assembly Bill 1887 would add an entirely new division (Division 5) to the Probate Code, creating and defining the office of conservator and the concept of conservatorship. Conservatorships would be an alternative to guardianships in certain instances and would provide the benefits of a conservator in certain others wherein guardianships are not now permissible. (1) Purpose A great many elderly or physically or mentally ill persons are reluctant to ask that a guardian be appointed to conduct their affairs because of the label of "incompetent" which attaches to them under the present law. Often where the person affected is himself willing to have a guardian appointed, his relatives resist the suggestion for the same reason. For these reasons, there are in California today a great many people whose affairs should be given the protection of a guardianship but for whom such protection has not been sought. The proposed legislation, by calling the person requiring assistance a "conservatee, and the person appointed by the court to assist him a 'conservator,' and by eliminating all reference to "incompetency" seeks to overcome the reluctance to use the protection which should be available under the law. (6 

(2) Initiation of Proceedings 

Under the present law, a person may not petition to have a guardian appointed for himself. The petition must be filed by a "relative or friend" (Prob. C. Sec. 1461). Under the proposed legislation, a person may himself file a petition to have a conservator appointed (Sec. 1754), although a friend or relative is also given the right to do so. To avoid abuses, the proposal requires that a person requesting the appointment of a conservator for himself establish good cause therefor (Sec. 1751). 

Furthermore, a person is given the right to nominate his own conservator in advance of the need or, if he has sufficient capacity to form an intelligent preference, to nominate him in his petition (Sec. 1752).

The court is bound to give preference to such a nominee, although the final selection rests in the discretion of the court (Sec. 1753). Under the present law, an elderly or infirm person, even though he anticipates the possible future need of a guardian, may not nominate him. The proposal permits the appointment of a conservator of the person or property or both "of any adult person who by reason of advanced age, illness, injury, mental weakness, intemperance, addiction to drugs or other disability, or other cause is unable properly to care for himself or for his property, or who for said causes or for any other cause is likely to be deceived or imposed upon by artful or designing persons, or for whom a guardian could be appointed under Division

4 of this code, or who voluntarily requests the same and to the satisfaction of the court establishes good cause therefor.” (3) Powers of Conservators

Under present law, a guardian must petition the court for instructions or for authority to make sales or investments at practically every turn. In a great many cases, this burdens the courts with perfunctory review of acts which an experienced and qualified guardian is perfectly competent to undertake without court approval. The proposal gives to a conservator the powers of a guardian under present law. In addition, "additional powers" may be granted to the conservator upon his application if the court in its discretion decides to grant them (Section 1853 of the proposal). The grant of additional powers is set forth in the letters of the conservator. It is thus possible for an experienced and qualified conservator, upon satisfying the court that he is entitled to additional powers, to conduct the conservatee's affairs without constantly addressing petitions to the court. It is believed that this arrangement will greatly facilitate the management of the affairs of the conservatee, while preserving adequate safeguards through the requirements of accountings mentioned below. 

(4) Accounts 

Under the present law, no period is fixed for the filing of accounts by a guardian, except for the filing of the first annual account (Prob. 

C. Sec. 1553). It is believed that most of the cases involving misappropriation of guardianship funds could be prevented if the fiduciary were required to file regular periodic accounts with the court. The present proposal (Sec. 1904) requires the filing of a first annual account and thereafter requires the conservator to file accounts "not less frequently than biennially unless otherwise ordered by the court." This fixes a normal accounting period, but permits shortening or lengthening of the interval in special cases. 

(5) Summary Proceedings 

Under the present law, there is no provision for the appointment of a temporary guardian of person or estate. The proposal sets up a procedure for the appointment of a "temporary conservator" of person or estate in circumstances requiring urgency. (Sec. 2201 and following.) The foregoing is a very brief summary of the salient features of the conservatorship proposal. It is not intended to be a comprehensive explanation.

CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE SECTION 553.5 (NEW) Assembly Bill 1889 (32) Attachments-Release of by Filing Undertaking on Appeal. 

Assembly Bill 1889 provides in substance that when a defendant appeals and the enforcement of the judgment against him is stayed by the filing of a sufficient undertaking, all property of the defendant which has been attached shall be released from attachment levy. The Code of 

Civil Procedure presently provides that such an undertaking on appeal

releases property levied under execution. Code of Civil Procedure Sections 946, 979, 986. Such an undertaking also releases real property under attachment. Code of Civil Procedure Section 674. The latter section, however, has been held inapplicable to the release of an attachment lien upon personal property. Balzano v. Traeger, 93 Cal. App. 640, 270 Pac. 249.

The proposed legislation would relieve defendants from the costly and purposeless burden of obtaining an additional undertaking for the release of an attachment where a sufficient undertaking staying execution has already been filed. 

PROBATE CODE SECTION 1080 Assembly Bill 1890 

(15) Petition to Determine Heirship; Time for Filing. The sole purpose of Assembly Bill 1890 is to advance the time when a petition to determine heirship may properly be filed in a probate proceeding. 

At present, such a petition may be filed by the personal representative or by a person claiming to be an heir or entitled to distribution, when the time to present claims against the estate has expired. This does not occur until six months after the first publication of the notice. 

(Prob. Code Section 700.) 

Assembly Bill 1890 substitutes as the earliest date for filing the petition: "when four months have elapsed after the first publication of notice to creditors." This is the limitation now prescribed for the filing of a petition for preliminary distribution of the estate or some part thereof.

Section 1080, as proposed to be amended, would retain the present additional limitation that a petition for final distribution shall not have been filed. (Prob. Code Section 1000.) 

Advancement of the time when a petition to determine heirship may be filed will tend to expedite the administration of the estate. At the same time, a reasonable waiting period is retained to allow time for the affairs of the estate to take shape and for persons having claims as heirs and beneficiaries to be identified. 

The amendment proposed does not attempt to provide for priority where both a petition for preliminary distribution and a petition to determine heirship are filed. This situation, it is believed, can be resolved by the court as particular cases arise. 

CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE SECTIONS 417 AND 351 Assembly Bill 2612 

(27) Code of Civil Procedure Section 417 now provides for a personal judgment, upon personal service outside the State, where the defendant was a resident of the State at the time of the commencement of the action or at the time of service. (See Allen v. Superior Court, 41 

C. 2d 306.) Its provisions do not apply to cases where a resident leaves the State after the cause of action arises but before action is commenced against him and does not return. The amendment proposed to Code of 

Civil Procedure Section 417 extends the section to cover that contingency so that the section would be applicable where the defendant is a resident at any one of three successive times: (1) when the cause of

action arose, (2) when the action was commenced, or (3) at time of service.

It is also proposed, as part of this measure, to incorporate provisions relating to the running of the statute of limitations when the defendant, who is thus subject to suit, is absent from this State. (Compare present C. C. P. 351.) The precise form of the provisions on this phase have not as yet been determined.

CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE SECTION 1013α Assembly Bill 2651 

(45) Proof of Service by Mail. The principal purpose of this bill is to permit a member of the State Bar to furnish proof of service by mail by a certificate rather than by an affidavit. This will make such proof simpler and less expensive. Proof of service by mail by such method is permitted in the federal courts in this State. Rule 5(b) of the Rules of the United States District Court for the Southern District provides for such proof. 8 Fed. Rules Serv. p. 960. Rule 4(b) of the 

Rules of the United States District Court for the Northern District provides that proof of service by mail may be made by affidavit, acknowledgment, "or by any other proof satisfactory to the court. ***"' 

8 Fed. Rules Serv. p. 945.

Persons who are not members of the Bar would still be required to prove such service by affidavit. This difference in method is justified by the fact that members of the Bar are officers of the court and would be subject to disciplinary action by both the court and by the State Bar for false statements, but such sanction would not apply to nonmembers of the Bar. This difference in method of proof conforms to the practice in several states as well as to the practice in the United States 

Supreme Court. See Rule 33(3), Rev. Rules of the U. S. Supreme Court, adopted April 12, 1954; 2 New Mexico Statutes, Sec. 19-101 (5) (f), p. 

104; 1 Ore. Rev. Statutes, Sec. 16.780, p. 107.

This bill would also delete from Code of Civil Procedure Section 1013a the requirement that the affidavit (or certificate) aver that there is delivery service by United States mail at the place addressed or that there is regular communication by mail between the place of mailing and the place addressed. This averment appears obsolete and superfluous today.

CIVIL CODE SECTION 224q, WELFARE AND INSTITUTIONS CODE SECTION 1620, AND BUSINESS AND PROFESSIONS CODE SECTION 9000 Assembly Bill 3374 (2) Independent Adoptions. Section 224q of the Civil Code pro vides that any person other than a parent or a licensed agency who places any child for adoption is guilty of a misdemeanor. When this section was enacted in 1945, there was no thought on the part of the legal profession that this section would impair or curtail the practice of law or the practice of medicine with respect to clients who might retain the professional services of a lawyer or physician for advice, counsel or assistance in an adoption matter. During that year, 83 percent of children placed for adpotion (other than stepparent adoptions) were placed independently, with the only professional assistance being

from lawyers and physicians. This has been the case in a substantial majority of all adoption placements from the enactment of the first Adoption Law in California in 1872 down to the present time. An opinion (53-228) of the Attorney General obtained by the Department of Social Welfare in January, 1954, has for the first time interpreted this section as being a limitation on the professional activities of lawyers and physicians, and raised a question as to the legality of their traditional services. 

The State Bar's position on the subject of maintaining independent adoptions has been stated as follows: "The Board of Governors believes that the State Bar should stand in accordance with the apparently overwhelming weight of opinion among the members of the Bar, i.e., that independent adoptions should be supported; that lawyers have a legitimate and useful place in this field and that the field should not be taken over by social welfare agencies. The State Bar, needless to say, will gladly work with the State Department of Social Welfare, the Attorney General and others legitimately interested, for the correction of any abuses which may exist in this field and for constructive clarifying legislation on the problem as a whole." May-June, 1954, Journal of the State Bar, p. 172.

In order to clarify and make clear the role which attorneys and physicians may legitimately play in the placement of children for adoption it is proposed to add a provision to Section 224q of the Civil Code to the effect that nothing therein shall be deemed applicable to an attorney-at-law or to a physician in connection with this practice. 

Corresponding amendments would be made to Section 1620 of the Welfare and Institutions Code and Section 9000 of the Business and Professions Code.

Similar provisions with respect to the rights of attorneys where the work of other licensed professional persons impinges on the traditional field of law are to be found in Business and Professions Code Section 5062 (relating to public accountants), Section 6854 (relating to collection agencies), Section 10133 (relating to real estate brokers), and 

Insurance Code Section 1850.2 (relating to advisory organizations). 

Similar provisions relating to physicians are to be found in Business and Professions Code Section 4031 (relating to pharmacists), and Sec tion 1626 (relating to dentists).

PROBATE CODE SECTIONS 640.1 (NEW), 641, 642, 644 AND 645.1 (NEW) Assembly Bill 3431 (10) Administration of Estates Setting Aside $2,500 to Surviving 

Spouse. Under the provisions of Probate Code Sections 640, et seq., if the value of an estate is $2,500 or less, all of the estate is set aside to the surviving spouse or minor children, provided they do not have other estate of $5,000 or more, subject only to the payment of the expenses of last illness, funeral expenses and expenses of administration, thus excluding general creditors of the estate. If, on the other hand, the estate exceeds the value of $2,500, the spouse or minor children take nothing under the provisions of these sections. The claims of general creditors, if the estate be insolvent, will consume the whole

of the estate. It appears to be an anomaly, therefore, that the spouse should take the whole estate free from creditors' claims where the estate is $2,500 or less and gets nothing where the estate is more valuable, even if only one dollar more valuable.

The proposed amendments permit the surviving spouse or minor children to select property up to $2,500 in value, which property will be set aside to them. The proposed legislation requires the surviving spouse to pay the expenses of last illness, funeral expenses and expenses of administration only if there are no other funds from which such expenses can be paid. The result of the proposed amendment will provide the surviving spouse with a claim for $2,500 second only in priority to possible claims for expenses of last illness, funeral expenses and administration costs.

CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE SECTION 170.5 Senate Bill 89; Assembly Bill 1281 (25) Disqualification of Judge by Ex Parte Affidavit. Present 

Section 170.5 of the Code of Civil Procedure was held unconstitutional in Austin v. Lambert, 11 C. (2d) 73 (1938).

Senate Bill 89 and Assembly Bill 1281 would enact a new Section 

170.5 to permit the disqualification of a judge and require assignment of a new judge to try a case or special proceeding, upon ex parte affidavit procedure, similar to that in force in some other states. The provisions would be available to all parties, including the State in criminal cases. However, only one such motion could be made by each side. 

Passage of the measure is advocated on the ground that one change of judges is warranted when a party or his attorney makes an affidavit in the form required, averring that the judge assigned or scheduled to try the cause is prejudiced against the party or his attorney so that the party or attorney cannot or believes he cannot have a fair and impartial trial.

The new section is intended to meet certain objections raised on constitutional grounds to present Section 170.5 in Austin v. Lambert, supra. It was said in that case, distinguishing present Section 170.5 from laws of certain other states providing for the ex parte affidavit procedure: "Such an ex parte proceeding has been upheld on the ground that the charge of bias and prejudice under oath is at least an imputation of such disqualification sufficient to save the statute from attack on constitutional grounds." (11 C. 2d at p. 261.)

Measures similar to Senate Bill 89 and Assembly Bill 1281 have met with approval of past Legislatures but have not been successful as to executive approval. (E.g., A. B. 479, 1951 General Session; S. B. 392, 1953 General Session.) 

The present measure differs in form from previous ones principally in two respects.

First, more explicit provisions have been added for courts having a "master calendar system" where a presiding or other judge assigns a case to a particular department or judge on the day set for trial. The motion must be then made to such presiding or other supervising

judge, rather than to the particular judge after the case is assigned. Also, the case is thereupon to be continued on the master calendar from day to day. These provisions are intended to conserve the court's time, since the presiding or other judge in the calendar department can then assign another case to the particular judge without delay. Moreover, they make clear the general intent that the procedure is not to be used for purposes of delay.

Second, the wording of the proposed section has been revised to eliminate the word "hearing" and other matter which might afford a basis for applying the section to matters such as demurrers, motions for preliminary injunctions and the like. The section is now generally stated in terms of "trial" of the action or proceeding, as this is the situation in which freedom from imputation of prejudice is most important.

The new section expressly states that it is cumulative to other sections on motions for disqualification.

It may be noted that a similar principle, that of obtaining a change of place of trial in civil actions in justice's courts upon simple affidavit of prejudice, appears formerly to have been upheld. (See People v. Compton, 123 Cal. 403; Ritzman v. Burnham, 114 Cal. 522, 524; former C. C. P. 883(5) and present C. C. P. 170(5).) In respect of an affidavit filed by a defendant in a criminal action under Penal Code Section 1431, it was held, however, that the affidavit was required to state "reasons" and that the sufficiency thereof was for the trial court to determine. (See Lowrey v. Hague, 85 C. 500; Miles v. Justice Court, 13 C. A. 454.)

PROBATE CODE SECTION 901 Senate Bill 320; Assembly Bill 114 

(13) Probate Fee Schedule. The commissions paid to the executor of a will, when no compensation is provided by the will or he renounces all claim thereto, or to an administrator, are fixed by Probate Code 901. 

The purpose of this measure is to adjust those commissions in respect to estates where the amount accounted for is between $20,000 and $500,000.

The present fees for estates under $20,000 are unchanged, i.e., 7 percent on the first $1000; 4 percent for the next $9,000; and 3 percent on the next $10,000. Thereafter the changes effected by the amendment are: 

From $20,000 to $50,000, From $50,000 to $150,000, From $150,000 to $500,000, Above $500,000, no change. increased from 2 percent to 3 percent increased from 1 percent to 2 percent increased from 1 percent to 1 percent

Under the provisions of Probate Code Section 910 the changes made in Probate Code Section 901 would be applicable to the regular compensation of attorneys.

The probate fee schedule as now provided for in Probate Code Section 901 has remained unchanged since 1909, except that in 1925 the fee for estates of over $100,000 was increased by one-half of 1 percent.

CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE SECTION 1001 (NEW) Senate Bill 356; Assembly Bill 836 

(44) Disclosure of Insurance Coverage. These bills provide that in an action for bodily injury, death or property damage, the defendant, upon written demand from the plaintiff, shall furnish to plaintiff a copy of any insurance policy issued to the defendant insuring against claims of the general character sued upon. The proposed new code section will expressly provide, however, that nothing therein shall be deemed to make admissible in evidence any information required to be furnished.

The enactment of this legislation is urged upon the ground that since this information can presently be obtained in a proceeding to perpetuate testimony (Superior Insurance Company v. Superior Court, 37 C. 

2d 749; Demaree v. Superior Court, 10 C. 2d 99), a direct statutory method should be provided, instead of compelling resort to the more cumbersome and expensive method of a special proceeding. A statutory right to obtain this information, it is said, will expedite the obtaining of such information.

Present statutory law (Ins. C. Sec. 11581) provides that in any proceeding supplementary to execution, the judgment debtor may be required to exhibit any policy carried by him, insuring against liability for the loss or damage for which judgment was obtained. 

A federal court has allowed discovery of insurance policies under. 

Federal Rules of Civil Procedure. Brackett v. Woodall Food Products, 12 F. R. Dec. 4. Also see Kingdom of The Netherlands v. Ferrary Marine Repairs Inc., 18 F. R. Serv. 34.41, Case 3; 14 F. R. Dec. 14 (Ad miralty case). Contra: McClure v. Boerger, 17 F. R. Serv. 34.41, Case 2. 

See 5 Stanford Law Review (Feb. 1953), pages 322-331.

CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE SECTIONS 875-880 (NEW) Senate Bill 412 

(40) Contribution Among Joint Tortfeasors. Under the common law there is no contribution between joint tortfeasors. One of several joint tortfeasors may be forced to pay the whole claim for the damages caused by them yet he may not recover from the others their pro rata share of the claim. California follows this rule. (Dow v. Sunset Tel. & 

Tel. Co. (1921) 162 Cal. 136 and cases there cited; see Tuft, Contribution Between Joint Tortfeasors (1936) 24 Cal. Law Rev. 546.) The purpose of this bill is to lessen the harshness of that doctrine. 

The ancient basis of the rigid rule against contribution in this type of case is the policy that the law should deny assistance to tortfeasors in adjusting losses among themselves because they are wrongdoers and the law should not aid wrongdoers. But this overemphasizes the supposed penal character of liability in tort; it ignores the general aim of the law for equal distribution of common burdens and of the right of recovery of contribution in various situations, e.g., among co-sureties. It ignores also the fact that most tort liability results from inadvertently caused damage and leads to the punishment of one wrongdoer by permitting another wrongdoer to profit at his expense. 

Some American jurisdictions have recognized the right of contribution among joint tortfeasors without a statute: District of Columbia,

Minnesota, Pennsylvania (before statute), Tennessee (before statute), Wisconsin, and (possibly) Maine.

Eight states have statutes which, as in the proposed bill, are limited to contribution among joint judgment defendants: Delaware, Michigan, Mississippi, Missouri, New York, North Carolina, Texas, and West Virginia.

A number of states have adopted statutes which declare broadly the right to contribution while other states have adopted variations of the contribution act sponsored in 1939 by the National Conference of Commissioners on Uniform Laws but which has been withdrawn for further study. 

According to the Report of the Special Committee on Review of Uniform Joint Tortfeasors Act of the National Conference of Commissioners on Uniform Laws (1953 Handbook, p. 273) "contribution among joint tortfeasors is now in effect, in one form or another, in about half of the states."

The proposed statute applies only to the case where a money judgment has been rendered against two or more defendants jointly. Like the New York statute, it covers only contribution among judgment tortfeasors. In order for one judgment tortfeasor to enforce a right of contribution he must, by payment, discharge the joint judgment or pay more than his pro-rata share (Sec. 875 (c)). The pro-rata share is determined by dividing the entire judgment equally (Sec. 876(a)) but the measure also provides that "where one or more persons are held liable solely for the tort of another, as in the case of the liability of a master for the tort of his servant, they shall contribute a single pro-rata share, as to which there may be indemnity between them. (See Martindale v. Griffin, 233 App. Div. 517, 253 N. Y. S. 578 (1931), affirmed in 259 

N. Y. 530, 182 N. E. 167 (1932) applying the New York statute.) 

The right of contribution is denied the tortfeasor "who has intentionally injured the injured person." (Sec. 875 (d).) In this respect. the statute retains the original rule on contribution, the doctrine having its inception in a case involving intentional joint tortfeasors (Merryweather v. Nixan (1799) 8 Term Reports 186).

Certain problems inherent in the formulation of a statute on contribution, such as subrogation of liability insurers, indemnity, vicarious liability, and the application of equitable principles, are covered by the statute. Thus, Section 875 (e) expressly provides for subrogation where the liability insurer by payment has discharged the judgment in order to make it clear that the right of contribution is not limited to the insured. The right of indemnity is preserved and there is no right of contribution where one joint tortfeasor is entitled to indemnity from another (Sec. 875(f)). In the case where the master is liable for the tort of his servant under the doctrine of respondeat superior, the servant could have no justifiable claim to contribution from the master and the master has the greater right to full indemnity. 

The procedure applicable is provided by Section 877 which provides for entry of judgment for contribution by motion upon notice. 

In order to provide ample opportunity for knowledge of the statute and to make it applicable prospectively, it is provided that the measure "shall become effective as to causes of action accruing on and after

January 1, 1956" in lieu of the earlier normal effective date under constitutional provisions.

CIVIL CODE SECTION 683 Senate Bill 524 (3) Joint Tenancy; How Created in Certain Situations. At the common law it was essential for the existence of a joint tenancy that there be unity of time, title, interest and possession. In other words, it was necessary that all the concurrent owners acquire their interests at the same time and by the same conveying instrument. There is also the common law rule that an owner of land cannot grant to himself. 

Civil Code Section 683 presently provides for certain exceptions to the common law rules with respect to the creation of a joint tenancy. 

The purpose of Senate Bill 524 is to enlarge these exceptions. The bill would permit the creation of a joint tenancy by a single transfer from tenants in common or joint tenants to less than all of them or to less to all of them and others. This is not permissible under present law. The proposed legislation will avoid the necessity of making a conveyance through a dummy in these particular situations. 

PROBATE CODE SECTION 41.1 Senate Bill 319 (5) Wills Restrictions on Gifts to Charity. Probate Code Section 

41 now sets forth in the first part thereof, certain limitations on gifts to charity in wills. These general terms of Section 41 require the will to have been duly executed at least 30 days before the death of the maker ; even if so made, it is provided that the will is void as to bequests and devises to charity, to the extent they exceed one-third of the estate, as against certain designated heirs.

In 1943 the Legislature amended the section by adding the final paragraph. This, in general terms, precludes nonheirs named in the will from challenging the charitable bequest. It also in general terms curtails the right of the designated heirs to do so. 

As a result, it is contended, the first part of the section may now be completely avoided by a circuitous device in will provisions. By example: A, the maker of the will, has a son, S, and it is desired to will all A's estate to Z Charity. It appears that provisions in the will may be added leaving all of A's estate to F, a nonheir, in the event the bequest of the Estate of Z Charity is invalid. But the real purpose of making the provisions in favor of F, it is asserted, is to circumvent the general terms of Section 41, not to vest any property in F. In this situation, F, a person not mentioned in the protective provisions of the present law, may not void the charitable provisions, under the final paragraph. The presence of the will provisions in his (F's) favor also prevents S from asserting the benefit of the statute. Consequently, through this formula, the bequest to Z Charity becomes legally valid, no matter when made and in what amount. (See, on construction of present Section 41, Estate of Davis, 74 C. A. (2d) 357; Estate of 

Haines, 76 C. A. (2d) 673; Estate of Leymel, 103 C. A. (2d) 778.)

The present statute is criticized on several grounds: First, the permissible use of such a device invites disrespect for law and those who

apply and administer it. The formula involves the inclusion of purely specious and circuitous provisions in a last will and testament. Second, Section 41 in present form is not clear, direct and understandable. Its general tenor is to one effect, whereas if the device be used, the section provides for the opposite effect. Third, because of such uncertainty, the statute does not produce a uniform effect. It is said to impose restrictions upon wills where the maker, through natural hesitancy or lack of specific information, has not included the formula provisions, whereas wills which have been specifically drawn and executed to take advantage of such formula are freed of all limitations. 

Senate Bill 319 is introduced to remedy this situation. In terms it is applicable only to wills executed on or after July 1, 1956. As to such wills the provisions of new Section 41.1 would apply. Such provisions would eliminate the use of the formula and make effective, for purposes of charitable bequest limitations, contingent bequests or devices. to strangers. Thus, if the stated limitations on charitable bequests or devices were violated, the property so improperly willed would go in accordance with other provisions of the will, and if not disposed of by the will, then to the heirs at law in accordance with the laws of succession.

PROBATE CODE SECTION 1200.1 Senate Bill 558 

(16) Uniform Form of Probate Notice. While there are numerous sections of the Probate Code relating to notice of hearing to be issued by the clerk of court in various proceedings provided for by the Probate Code, there is no uniform pattern as to the form and wording of such notices.

To eliminate the waste in time and expense inherent in the above situation, and to meet a long-standing demand by court clerks and the bar, an intensive study of the subject was made by the State Bar Committee on Legal Forms, resulting in the development of the proposed uniform form of probate notice set out in Senate Bill 558. 

The proposed notice form will require no other changes in statutory law since it is designed to couch in standard phraseology and form requirements of existing law on the subject. The form is specifically prescribed for notice of hearing on petition for probate of will (Prob. 

C. Sec. 327), petition for letters of administration (Prob. C. Sec. 441) and all notices covered, specifically or generally, by Probate Code Section 1200. In addition, it is provided that the form shall be used "in all other cases in which notice is required by this code and no other type of notice is prescribed by law or by the court or judge." It is not intended that the form replace statutory requirements for additional or special matter. (See, e.g., Probate Code Section 1173-petition to establish fact of death in petition for probate of will; Probate Code Section 1935.14-special form, where community real property or homestead of incompetent spouse is involved.)

The proposed notice form will greatly reduce the number of forms now in use throughout the State and will also eliminate uncertainty as to how such notices should be drafted.

VEHICLE CODE SECTION 404 

Senate Bill 660 

(55) Service of Process Outside State-Executor or Administrator of Nonresident Motorist. The amendment to Vehicle Code Section 

404 proposed by Senate Bill 660 seeks to bridge a gap in the coverage of that section.

Statutes akin to Vehicle Code Section 404 exist in all the states; they provide a means for constructive or substituted service of process upon nonresident motorists involved in accidents on the highways. Thus Section 404 prescribes, in substance, that the acceptance by a nonresident of the privileges of use of California highways is equivalent to the appointment of the Director of Motor Vehicles as his agent upon whom service of process may be made, provision being made for filing, notice, mailing, etc. But Section 404 does not cover the situation where the nonresident motorist dies prior to the commencement of the action or after the action is commenced. This hiatus has been rectified in other states by amendments similar to that proposed here. (See especially 

New York Vehicle and Traffic Law, Section 52, McKinney's Consol. 

Laws, N. Y. Book 62A; and 2 Laws, Wis. 1953, ch. 648, sec. 8; Mich. 

Comp. Laws, 1948, Mason's Supp. 1952, sec. 257.403; Acts Ark. 1949, Act 199, p. 615; Texas Gen. Laws (Acts) 1953, ch. 53.)

The amendment provides: (1) Where a nonresident who is subject to Section 404 has died prior to the commencement of an action pursuant to the provisions of the section, an action may be brought in this State against his executor or administrator; process therein may be served upon the executor or administrator in the same manner as is provided for the decedent. (2) Where an action has been commenced under the section by service upon a nonresident who dies thereafter, the court must allow the action to be continued against his executor upon motion, with such notice as the court deems proper. 

It has been argued that such statutes providing for substituted service upon the personal representative are vulnerable on two grounds: (1) that any consent to be sued, either actual or fictional is revoked by the death of the nonresident, and (2) "that while substituted service may be justified, under the police power, in the action against the motorist himself, which is transitory and in personam, the same does not hold true as to an action against his estate through his personal representative, which is in the nature of an action in rem, creating a right against property wholly within another jurisdiction." (Note, 18 

A. L. R. 2d 544, 545.) The New York Court of Appeals in Leighton v. 

Roper (1950) 300 N. Y. 434, 91 N.E. 2d 876, 18 A. L. R. 2d 537 considered those arguments in relation to the New York statute and sustained the constitutionality of that enactment. It concluded that an action against a personal representative is not one purely in rem and may therefore be founded upon consent jurisdiction and that the agency created by the statute is one created for the benefit of third persons and not for the benefit of the principal and, moreover, the Legislature, by the statute has made the agency irrevocable, thus annulling any common law rule to the contrary. 

The New York court pointed out it was not considering the effect the courts of the motorist's residence would give to the judgment. "And so, in enforcing any right adjudicated here against the estate,

the plaintiff must employ the procedure made appropriate by the law of Indiana (decedent's residence)." The court suggested, however, that the judgment "might nevertheless be effective for some purposes as, for example, the Statute of Limitations *** and where the liability of the decedent is payable by an insurer or an indemnitor, an estate may never be called upon to pay the judgment that may be rendered." (91 N.E. 2d at 876, 18 A. L. R. 2d at 544.) 

For other cases see: Feinsinger v. Bard (C. A. 7th, 1952), 195 F. 2d 

45; Tarczynski v. Chi., M. St. P. & P. R. Co. (1952), 261 Wis. 149, 52 

N. W. 2d 396; Oviatt v. Garretson (1943), 205 Ark. 792, 171 S. W. 2d 

287; Plopa v. Du Pre (1950), 327 Mich. 660, 42 N. W. 2d 777; Chapa v. Allen (D. C. Tex. 1954) 119 F. Supp. 129. Contrast: Knoop v. An derson (D. C. Iowa, 1947), 71 F. Supp. 832. See Annotation, 18 A. L. R. 

2d 544.

PUBLIC UTILITIES CODE SECTION 315 Senate Bill 661 

(54) Public Utilities Commission Accident Reports. Under present law Section 315 of the Public Utilities Code requires a public utility to file accident reports with the Public Utilities Commission. These reports are entirely confidential. Section 488 of the Vehicle Code, on the other hand, provides that while all accident reports filed with the Motor Vehicle Department shall be for the confidential use of the department, nevertheless the names and addresses of persons involved in or witnesses to an accident, registration numbers and descriptions of vehicles involved, and the date, time and location of an accident, shall be furnished to any person who may have a proper interest therein. 

The purpose of the proposed legislation is to require the Public Utilities Commission to supply the same information to any interested person as that required to be furnished by the Vehicle Code section. 

Common carriers secure the names of witnesses whenever an accident occurs involving personal injuries. Frequently those injured are unable, or do not know enough, to secure names of witnesses on the scene and are thereby greatly handicapped in enforcing their rights. The proposed legislation would protect the interest of those injured by cars or busses of common carriers.

PROBATE CODE SECTIONS 422 AND 423 Senate Bill 662; Assembly Bill 839 

(6) Administrators-Appointment by Nonresident Heirs. At the present time Section 422 of the Probate Code sets forth the order of priority of persons entitled to letters of administration in an intestate estate. The one first entitled to priority by that section is the surviving spouse, the wording of subdivision (1) of that section being as follows: “(1) The surviving spouse, or some competent person whom he or she may request to have appointed."

In spite of the fact that Section 420 of the Probate Code says that no person is competent to serve as an administrator or administratrix who is not a bona fide resident of this State our courts have determined that a nonresident surviving spouse although not himself qualified to petition for letters because of nonresidence, may nonetheless nominate

a resident of this State to be administrator, and such nominee acquires the number one priority held by the nonresident spouse. Robie's Estate (1879) Myrick 226; Cotter's Estate (1880) 54 Cal. 215, 216; Stevenson's Estate (1887) 72 Cal. 164, 165; see Pardue's Estate, (1937) 

22 C.A. 178, 180.

The priorities following the surviving spouse as set forth in Section 422 of the Probate Code in subdivision (2) to (5), inclusive, comprise in that order the children, the grandchildren, the parents and the brothers and sisters of a decedent. Although Section 422 says nothing concerning the nominee of these relatives, Section 423, Probate Code, does permit the appointment of the nominees of such relatives, but says nothing about the question of the residence of the nonresident making the nomination. It could be argued that, therefore, the same rule should apply to the nominee of children, grandchildren, etc., as to the nominee of a spouse, but the courts in this State have held otherwise. In a number of cases it has been held that the nonresidence of a child, grandchild, etc., which disqualifies him from acting as administrator also disqualifies him from nominating a resident administrator. See Beeche's 

Estate (1883) 63 Cal. 458, 459; Pardue's Estate (1937) 22 C.A. 2d 

178, 180; Donovan's Estate (1894) 104 Cal. 623; Hyde's Estate (1883) 

64 Cal. 228.

The effect of this interpretation is that the public administrator has preference over the nominee of a nonresident child who may be the sole heir of the decedent.

The only purpose of these amendments to Probate Code Sections 422 and 423 is to remove from the classes of child, grandchild, parent, brother and sister this present disqualification to nominate when they are nonresidents. The priorities among the classes are not changed. 

The successors to a decedent's property who are closely related members of his family will, in the ordinary course of events, be sufficiently acquainted with, and are so directly interested in his property and affairs, that they should be entitled as a matter of right to select the person who is to deal with this property during administration. The law generally favors the control and management of property by its owners. Every principle of equity supports a policy under which the principal is granted the right to select his agent. These standard concepts of property law should be followed unless some important and overriding public policy justifies depriving the owner of control over his property.

The rights of local creditors, including taxing authorities, and the convenience of the court in which administration is pending, justify limiting administrators to residents of this State. It is beyond challenge that the courts are presumed to discharge their duties properly and will appoint only capable and qualified persons. Within the available qualified appointees no reason exists for preferring a government official over a private individual or trust company selected by the beneficial owner of the property. In all but exceptional circumstances the proportionate share of the beneficial owner in the estate substantially exceeds the shares of creditors, and taxing authorities. Any concept favoring the public official because of his impartiality and independence deprives the party most interested in the estate of control

over it. It would seem that the predominant ownership should be preferred while the court can still protect the creditors and taxing authorities without depriving the property of private, experienced and harmonious management appointed by the owner. 

CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE SECTIONS 416.1, 416.2, 416.3, 

442, 581a, 585, 586 AND 1014

Senate Bill 815 (29) Motions-Procedure Upon Filing Motion to Quash-Service of Process or Motion to Strike Allegations in Complaint-Motion Under Section 581a as a Special Appearance. Senate Bill 815 in two aspects relates to motions made at the outset of civil litigation. 

When a person who has been served with process desires to raise an objection to the jurisdiction of the court over him or to the service of process, it is necessary that he make a motion to quash the service. 

Such a proceeding must be carefully limited to an appearance for this purpose i.e., a special appearance. However, under present statutory law, the making of the motion does not extend the time to plead to the complaint. Consequently, it is possible for a default judgment to be entered against the litigant while he is contesting the process which is the very basis of the proceeding against him. Though relief may be obtained from such a default, the circumstances under which it will be granted are not clear and have resulted in a diversity of judicial opinion. (See Riskin v. Towers, 24 C. 2d 274.)

Senate Bill 815 provides, in essence, that no default can be taken while the question of jurisdiction over the person remains undetermined; that is, until a specified period (10 days) has elapsed after notice of denial of the motion or, if timely application has been made for review by mandate and notice thereof given the adverse party, until 10 days after final judgment in the mandamus proceeding. For good cause, the 10-day period to plead could be extended by the court for an additional period of not to exceed 20 days. (Sections 416.1 and 

416.3.) Only a limited time is provided for the litigant to seek review of an adverse ruling by petition for writ of mandate, if the time to plead is to be kept open, by virtue of the statute. (See Section 416.3.) 

In addition, the measure: (1) removes any uncertainty as to the effect of an order or stipulation extending the time to plead, in this situation, by providing that neither shall constitute a general appearance (Sec. 416.1); and (2) expressly provides that a motion for relief under Section 473, relating to setting aside a default, may be made without such act becoming in legal effect a general appearance. 

In federal practice, the litigant is not faced with the dilemma here. outlined. Rule 12(b) of the Rules of Civil Procedure in effect abolishes special appearances. Objections to jurisdiction and to the merits may be made in the same document. Adoption of the federal rule, however, is not advocated to meet the present problem, first, because of the general revision that would be necessary, and second, because of disadvantages that may exist in the matter of appellate review. The present state practice is to seek review by writ, before further proceedings are had. This has been preserved, with the designation of the particular writ as mandate.

A second change made by the measure is to make a motion to strike sufficient as a pleading and an appearance, to prevent default. Such a motion, however, must be noticed for prompt hearing. (See Section 

585, in particular.) Often a complaint or cross complaint alleges scandalous or other irrelevant matter which should be eliminated before the litigant is compelled to answer. At present, it is necessary to file a demurrer with the motion to strike, even though the demurrer is not pressed. The changes, however, do not extend the time to demur (Sec. 

585). Only the time to answer would be extended. Thus, a litigant could not first file a motion to strike, and then, if that be denied file a demurrer.

The amendments proposed to Section 581a apply principles similar to those stated in connection with the motion to quash, to a motion made under Section 581a. That section provides, in part, that an action shall be dismissed when summons has not been issued within one year or when summons has not been served and return made within three years, with certain exceptions. The section, however, contains wording that "such actions may be prosecuted, if appearance has been made." 

Uncertainty arises from the fact that "appearance" is not defined. 

It has been held in certain decisions of the District Court of Appeal that a motion to dismiss made pursuant to Section 581a is not a general appearance. (See e.g. Frohman v. Bonelli, 91 C. A. 2d 285, 291; 

Brock v. Fouchy, 76 C. A. 2d 363, 370.) However, the contrary view has also been indicated, at least if the motion to dismiss involves the "exceptions" in the section, such as absence from the state or concealment to evade process. (Bank of America v. Harrah, 113 C. A. 2d 

639, 641.)

A special appearance for the purpose of moving to quash service of. summons or purported service should not be a waiver of the dismissal provisions of Section 581a. Likewise, a motion to dismiss should not have that effect. To resolve the uncertainty in present Section 581a and declare the proper principle, Section 581a is amended (1) to add the word "general" before the word "appearance"; and (2) to add provisions that neither a motion to dismiss pursuant to the provisions of the section nor an extension of time to plead after such motion constitutes a general appearance.

CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE SECTION 948 Senate Bill 860 (41) Cash Deposit in Lieu of Undertaking Upon Appeal From Judgment of Superior Court. In 1927, Code of Civil Procedure Sec tion 948 was amended to provide, alternatively, that an appellant may deposit cash, in lieu of an undertaking (with sureties) to stay enforcement of a judgment against him, pending the appeal. (1927 Stats., p. 874.)

The wording of Section 948, however, is (1) ambiguous as applied to judgments other than those for the payment of money; and (2) insufficient in terms to make the cash deposit an adequate substitute for the undertaking. Section 948 now provides, in material part, that “in all cases where an undertaking is required on appeal by the provisions of this title, a deposit in the court below of the amount of the judgment

appealed from, shall be equivalent to filing the undertaking." (Emph. supplied.)

The purpose of Senate Bill 860 is to correct these defects by: (1) Providing that, except for money judgments (C. C. P. 942), an appellant may deposit with the court below cash in the same amount as that prescribed for an undertaking (whether such amount be fixed by statute or by court pursuant to statute); and (2) Providing that when an appeal from a money judgment is to be stayed, pursuant to Section 942, the court shall determine the amount of the cash deposit by various factors, including the probable interest during appeal and respondent's costs on appeal, with a maximum limitation of one and one-half times the amount named in the judgment.

Present Sections 943, 945 and 949 now provide in various terms for the filing of an undertaking to stay judgments other than a money judgment. As indicated, express authority would be conferred to deposit cash in the amount of the undertaking prescribed by law or fixed by the court pursuant to various sections other than Section 942. Similar, but not identical, provisions are now found in Code of Civil Procedure Sections 978 and 985, relating to appeals from justice courts and municipal courts, respectively.

In the case of appeal from a money judgment of a superior court (Sec. 942), the law presently requires an undertaking for one and onehalf times the amount named in the judgment, if the undertaking be that of an authorized corporate surety; otherwise, double such amount. 

However, as indicated, Section 948 appears to permit cash to be deposited only in the amount required to be paid by the judgment, without taking into consideration interest during the appeal and respondent's costs on appeal. As a result, the latter two items are often uncollectible, upon affirmance of the judgment. The necessity of requiring a greater cash deposit was recognized in Dowling v. Fedel, 116 C. 

A. 2d 849, 852. In that case, the appellate court, in issuing a discretionary stay by writ of supersedeas, required the appellant to add to his deposit of the exact amount of the judgment, the further sum of $1,000 for the purpose of covering interest and added costs. 

The changes herein proposed will relate only to appeals from superior courts by reason of the general limitation of Code of Civil Procedure Section 959, contained in the same "chapter" as Section 948. 

GOVERNMENT CODE SECTION 68841 Senate Bill 1061; Assembly Bill 376 

(51) Salary of Supreme Court Clerk. This bill increases the salary of the Clerk of the Supreme Court and ex officio Secretary of the Judicial Council from $12,000 to $15,000 per year. This places his salary more in line with comparable positions elsewhere and with the responsibilities of the office. The position requires an attorney of ability and experience. The incumbent fills these requirements to a high degree. 

The salary of the clerk of the highest court in the State of New York is $15,840, and in Pennsylvania it is $15,000. The salary of the Clerk of the Superior Court in Los Angeles is $14,040, that of the Secretary of

the Superior Court in San Francisco is $13,500, that of the Clerk of the Municipal Court in San Francisco is $13,200, and that of the Clerk of the Municipal Court in Los Angeles is $12,576. 

CIVIL CODE SECTION 1714, CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE SECTION 625 Senate Bill 1063; Assembly Bill 1845 

(4) Comparative Negligence. These bills provide for a full or true comparative negligence statute similar to that provided for in 1953 

Assembly Bill 406 and 1953 Senate Bill 1492, as amended. By these bills, the rule of comparative negligence would be substituted for that of contributory negligence. The explanations of, and the arguments for and against, comparative negligence have been fully stated and published in previous reports and articles and will not be repeated here. 

References (1) Senate Interim Judiciary Committee, Second Progress Report to the Legislature, 1953, pp. 51-75. This report includes an analysis by the 

Legislative Counsel, the report of the Conference Committee of the

State Bar of California on Comparative Negligence, a report of the California State Chamber of Commerce, a reprint from the Los Angeles 

Bar Bulletin of an article entitled, "Let Us Be Frank About Compara tive Negligence" by Richard Oliver, and a copy of the Contributory 

(Comparative) Negligence Act of 1945 of Great Britain. 

(2) "California Should Adopt a 'Comparative Negligence Law' "

by Ben C. Duniway, and "The Case Against Comparative Negligence” 

-by Harold R. McKinnon, 28 State Bar Journal, p. 22, January-February 1953. (3) "Comparative Negligence"- by William L. Prosser, Dean, School of Law, University of California (Berkeley), 41 Cal. L. Rev., Spring 1953, p. 1. (4) "Let Us Be Frank About Comparative Negligence" by Hon. 

William J. Palmer, Los Angeles Bar Bulletin, Nov. 1952, p. 37.

VEHICLE CODE SECTIONS 486 (NEW), 488 AND 488.5 Senate Bill 1254 

(58) Motor Vehicle Accident Reports. It is proposed to add a new 

Section 486 to the Vehicle Code which will require all peace officers who investigate any accident in the performance of their official duties to prepare a report of such accident containing the names and addresses of all drivers involved, owners of vehicles involved, persons injured, and witnesses to the accident. The report shall also contain a diagram of the scene, with pertinent measurements, and statements of all persons involved in the accident and of all other witnesses made to the officer. The report shall be filed within 48 hours with the police department if the accident takes place in a city, or with the California 

Highway Patrol if the accident takes place outside a city.

Vehicle Code Section 488 will be amended so as to provide that such reports may be examined by any person who has a proper interest therein, including the driver or drivers, the authorized representative of a driver, any person injured in the accident and the owners of any vehicles damaged in the accident.

Vehicle Code Section 488.5 will be amended to eliminate the provision prohibiting the release of such accident reports until after the expiration of six months from the date of the accident. 

The present state of the law prohibits persons involved in an accident. from securing important information regarding the accident at the time they need it most. Important data regarding an accident are obtained by the police officer or highway patrolman who investigates the accident. He is trained to locate the causes of the accident. The parties trust him and give him the information he requests. They are often too distraught or disabled to obtain this information themselves. Yet when they seek to obtain it from the local police station or highway patrol office, they are confronted with the situation that most of the important aspects of such information are not available during the most important period of time following the accident, that is, not until six months following the date of the accident.

C. 1955 LEGISLATIVE PROGRAM OF THE CALIFORNIA BANKERS ASSOCIATION 

The following bills were introduced in both the Assembly and the Senate as part of the legislative program of the California Bankers 

Association: 

Senate Bill 526; Senate Bill 528; Senate Bill 530; Senate Bill 531;

Assembly Bill 1306. 

D. EXPLANATIONS OF BILLS SPONSORED BY THE CALIFORNIA BANKERS ASSOCIATION 

The committee heard the various proposals for statutory changes recommended by the California Bankers Association. The committee believes that explanations of the effect of these proposed enactments and the reasons each is deemed desirable by the California Bankers Association will be of assistance to members of the bench and bar, as well as to Members of the Legislature, and, accordingly, there is hereinafter set forth the official explanation presented with each of the measures by representatives of the California Bankers Association:

MEMORANDUM ON PROPOSAL TO AMEND SECTION 955.1 OF THE CIVIL CODE 

Senate Bill 526

In 1953 the Legislature enacted Section 955.1 of the Civil Code, providing that the transfer of any right to payment shall be deemed perfected against third persons when there is executed and delivered to the transferee a written assignment, except when the right to payment is represented by a negotiable or non-negotiable instrument or is an account receivable as defined in Section 3017. 

Question has been raised as to whether or not this has the effect of taking the assignment of leases out of the application of the recording statutes, which was not the intention.

It is proposed to amend Section 955.1 to provide that its provisions are subject to any of the provisions of any applicable recording statute, so as to make sure that the section does not have the effect of taking out from under the recording statutes the assignment of leases, mort-

gages or other assignments affecting real property which are required to be recorded to give constructive notice. 

MEMORANDUM RE AMENDMENT OF CURATIVE STATUTE RELATIVE TO DEFECTS IN RECORDED INSTRUMENTS SO AS TO INCLUDE CHATTEL MORTGAGES IN ADDITION TO THE PRESENTLY COVERED INSTRUMENTS AFFECTING TITLE TO REALTY. (CIVIL CODE SECTION 1207) Senate Bill 528 

The section in question presently provides that an instrument affecting title to real property will impart notice despite defects in its execution or acknowledgment after one year from its recordation. 

This amendment simply extends the coverage of this curative statute to mortgages of personal property. It would appear that the same reason for such provision as is presently made for real property exists for mortgages of personal property, namely, that stability of transactions should not be disturbed by minor imperfections or technical defects in instruments after the curative period has elapsed. More serious defects, such as lack of authority to execute the instrument, are not, however, cured. 

Senate Bill 530

Senate Bill 530 amends the Trust Receipts Act in two particulars, both designed to facilitate the financing of inventories of dealers in machinery and heavy equipment.

The law of California, as was once the case, at least, in most states, does not permit a merchant to give a mortgage upon his inventory. 

The Trust Receipts Act was enacted a number of years ago in California and in all the other commercial states in order to provide a means whereby a merchant might finance the acquisition of goods. 

Under the Trust Receipts Act a merchant may give a trust receipt to a bank or other lender at the time he receives the goods, which in effect gives the lender a lien on the goods, effective in the event of bankruptcy but ineffective against purchasers from the merchant in the ordinary course of trade. However, a trust receipt transaction can only be used to finance a merchant acquiring the goods "for sale" and only a "buyer," that is, one to whom the goods are "sold," takes free of the lien of the lender.

Dealers in heavy equipment, machinery, and motor vehicles are now more and more "leasing" such equipment, machinery and vehicles rather than selling it. The first two amendments to the act, that is, to 

Sections 3013 and 3014, permit the use of trust receipt financing even though the dealer is in the business of leasing rather than selling the goods and protect a lessee of the goods from the lien of the bank or other lender in the same manner as a buyer is now protected. 

The second change, that is, the amendment to Section 3014.5, is designed to permit the financing of dealers in heavy equipment or machinery in the same manner as automobile and aircraft dealers are now financed.

Under the ordinary trust receipt transaction the dealer must give a trust receipt as part of the transaction by which he acquires the goods and at the same time. This requirement was originally designed to protect trade creditors who may rely upon the merchant's inventory in extending credit. An exception was made, however, in the case of auto-

mobile, trailer and aircraft dealers who are permitted to give a valid trust receipt on vehicles or aircraft already in their possession. Practically all automobile dealers are financing in this manner, which is commonly called "flooring." It has worked satisfactorily as public notice of the financing is given and most such dealers have one primary trade creditor who is paid through this type of financing. The amendment would extend the same rule as is now applicable to motor vehicle and aircraft dealers to dealers of durable goods having a unit value in excess of $1,000, thereby permitting dealers in heavy equipment and machinery to be financed in the same manner as automobile dealers. 

MEMORANDUM ON PROPOSAL TO AMEND SECTION 360 OF THE CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE, RELATING TO THE STATUTE OF LIMITATIONS 

Senate Bill 531

In 1947 the Legislature amended Section 360 of the Code of Civil Procedure so as to provide that a payment on account of principal or interest on a promissory note would be deemed a sufficient acknowledgment to toll the statute of limitations and that the statute of limitations would not begin to run until the last payment of principal or interest made prior to the time when the statute would otherwise have run. 

This was enacted to avoid the necessity of constantly renewing notes as long as the obligor kept up his payments. It was intended that regular payments on account of principal or interest would toll the statute but that if the statute once ran a payment on account of principal or interest would not revive the cause of action. 

In the recent case of Eilke v. Rice, 129 A. C. A. 559, the District 

Court of Appeal, however, construed the clause "would otherwise have run' as meaning had the statute not been enacted and held that only payments made within four years after the due date of the note had the effect of tolling the statute, and held that an action brought in 1953 on a promissory note on which payments had regularly been made up to 1952 was barred by the statute. This decision is obviously contrary to the original legislative intent and it is therefore proposed to amend the section so as to provide that any payment on account of principal or interest made prior to the time when the statute of limitations would have run had such payment not been made shall have the effect of tolling the statute. In other words, the statute will be tolled as long as payments are regularly made and not more than four years is allowed to lapse between payments, which was the original result intended by the amendment.

CALIFORNIA BANKERS' ASSOCIATION MEMORANDUM RE PROVISION FOR SERVICE OF COMPLAINT IN ATTACHING PROPERTY (CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE SECTION 542) Assembly Bill 1306 

The amendment is intended as a simple clarification of the present provision for service of the complaint, in addition to the writ and notice, in attaching property. Such service of the complaint is now required under subsection 6 of Code of Civil Procedure 542 where the demand as stated in the writ does not exceed $300. 3-L-1691

In amending to provide that the service must be on the particular branch of a bank, building and loan association, or title insurance company where the account or other property of the defendant is located, the above-mentioned requirement that the complaint be served was not repeated. It is apparent that the legislative intent was merely to designate the proper place for service where branches of banks, etc., were involved, and not to provide a new procedure for service. The proposed amendment is designed to give effect to this intent, and to eliminate an uncertainty in a statute which, by its nature, must be strictly followed.

E. 1955 LEGISLATIVE PROGRAM OF THE CALIFORNIA LAND TITLE ASSOCIATION
