The following bills were introduced in both the Assembly and the Senate as part of the legislative program of the California Land Title 

Association: 

Assembly Bill 3437; Assembly Bill 3439; Assembly Bill 2677; 

Assembly Bill 3438; Assembly Bill 3436, and Assembly Bill 2538. 

F. EXPLANATIONS OF BILLS SPONSORED BY THE CALIFORNIA LAND TITLE ASSOCIATION 

The committee heard the various proposals for statutory changes recommended by the California Land Title Association. The committee believes that explanations of the effect of these proposed enactments and the reasons each is deemed desirable by the California Land Title Association will be of assistance to members of the bench and bar, as well as to members of the Legislature, and, accordingly, there is hereinafter set forth the official explanation presented with each of the measures by representatives of the California Land Title Association. 

MEMORANDUM ON PROPOSED AMENDMENT TO SECTION 587 OF THE PROBATE CODE Assembly Bill 3437 

This section now provides for the dedication or conveyance of real property or an interest therein by the estate of a decedent to certain governmental units. Under Section 587, the court may order, upon appropriate petition and notice, that an executor or administrator may "whenever it is for the advantage, benefit and best interests of the estate" dedicate or convey, with or without consideration, any real property of the estate or interests therein to the state or any county or municipal corporation, for street or highway purposes. The proposed amendment would add "the United States of America or any agency or instrumentality thereof" to the three governmental units now named in this code section. 

The present section also provides that such an order may be obtained authorizing the dedication or conveyance of an easement over any real property of the estate "to the state or any county, municipal corporation, public district, any person, firm, association, or public or private corporation." Here also the "United States of America or any agency or instrumentality thereof" is to be added. Finally, provision is made

so that an executor or administrator may obtain an order authorizing him to "convey, release or relinquish to the state or any county or municipal corporation any access rights to any street, highway, or freeway.”

In all cases such order is to be based upon the petition of the executor or administrator or any person interested in the estate, showing that the proposed dedication or conveyance is for the advantage, benefit and best interests of the estate. Notice of the hearing thereon is to be given pur suant to Section 1200 of the Probate Code. 

Identical amendments should be made to Probate Code Section 1515, which is virtually identical to Section 587 except that it relates to guardians rather than executors and administrators. 

MEMORANDUM ON PROPOSED AMENDMENTS TO SECTION 664 AND 665 OF THE PROBATE CODE: HOMESTEADS Assembly Bill 3439 

Probate Code Sections 664 and 665 providing for the setting apart in probate of a homestead which had been selected and recorded prior to the death of the decedent. Prior to 1947 these sections, in referring to the value of the recorded homestead simply stated the amount as "five thousand dollars." The Legislature, however, in 1954 had raised the homestead value as stated in Section 1260 of the Civil Code to $6,000 (this figure has been raised several times since, having been set at $12,500 in 1953). This discrepancy was noted (see e.g. Witkin Summary of California Law, page 1708) and in 1947 the Legislature amended Sections 664 and 665, substituting the phrase "amount of the homestead exemption" for "five thousand dollars."

It is clear that the 1947 legislation was intended as a cross reference from these Probate Code sections to Civil Code Section 1260 which now sets the amount at $12,500. However, it is not clear whether the "value of the homestead exemption" referred to means the amount set by Civil Code Section 1260 in effect at the date of death of the decedent, or the amount set by Section 1260 as it read when the homestead was recorded.

Section 663 of the Probate Code, after specifying how the selected homestead vests on death of one of the spouses, states that "the homestead is not subject to the payment of any debt, or liability existing against the spouses, or either of them, at the time of the death of either of them, except as provided in the Civil Code." This appears to be a clear statement that the controlling amount is that which is specified by the statute in effect at the date of death of the decedent. 

The proposed amendments to Sections 664 and 665 would provide that the "amount of the homestead exemption" referred to in these sections is the amount of exemption which is authorized by the law which is in effect at the date of the decedent's death. 

NOTE: The California Law Revision Commission, pursuant to Assembly Concurrent Resolution No. 8 (1954 Session) has completed a study of Probate Code 

Sections 640 to 646, and made recommendations as to legislation with respect to these sections. In the course of its study, the commission also considered Sections 

660 to 668 of the Probate Code and the report of the commission (January 1, 1955) contains a thorough analysis of them, but makes no recommendation for legislation since Assembly Concurrent Resolution No. 8 did not direct the commission to do so.

MEMORANDUM ON PROPOSED AMENDMENT TO SECTION 1152 OF THE PROBATE CODE-PUBLIC ADMINISTRATOR: AUTHORITY OF SUCCESSOR IN OFFICE Assembly Bill 2677 

Section 1152 of the Probate Code provides that where a public administrator is paid by salary rather than by fees "the authority of such public administrator shall cease upon the termination of his office as such public administrator, and his authority shall vest in his successor."'

The statute is silent as to whether or not the vesting of authority in the successor administrator should be accompanied by an issuance of letters to the successor. Interpretations of this statute have not been uniform: Opinions differ as to whether new letters of administration should be issued to the successor. For example: (1) In some counties new letters of administration are issued upon petition by the successor. (2) In other counties new letters are issued without any petition. (3) In still other counties the successor does not obtain new letters of administration.

As amended, the section would provide that the superior court should order the issuance of new letters upon the written application of the successor. No notice is necessary. The letters are to be issued as of the date of the termination of tenure of the outgoing public administrator. It is felt that this amendment will eliminate the uncertainty which is evidenced by the several practices which prevail in different counties in the State.

MEMORANDUM ON PROPOSED AMENDMENT TO SECTION 1534a, PROBATE CODE Assembly Bill 3438 

This amendment would change Probate Code Section 1534a to provide that, where the court is asked to confirm a sale of real property by a guardian, the additional bond which shall be required by the court, need not, where an authorized surety company is surety, be approved by the judge. The section likewise applies to mortgages or deeds of trust under which a guardian is to borrow money. *** "" This amendment would conform Section 1534a to other sections of the Probate Code relating to bond requirements. Thus, Section 1480, dealing with the oath and bond required of a guardian at the time of his appointment, provides that the bond which is required is "to be approved by the judge if the surety is not an authorized surety company Again, Section 545, appearing in Chapter VII, and relating to the oaths and bonds required of executors and administrators, provides that, "if the surety is not an authorized surety company, all such bonds must be approved by a judge of the superior court before being filed." And Section 541, also on executors and administrators, requiring bond for executors or administrators likewise states that the bond is "to be approved by a judge of the superior court if the surety is not an authorized surety company **" Finally, Section 542, which relates to the requirement of an additional bond upon the sale of real property by an executor or administrator, does not specify that court approval of the bond is necessary, hence this situation is governed by the general provision of Section 545 to the effect that court

approval is unnecessary where the surety is an authorized surety company.

The failure to apply the same rule in Section 1534a as is applied in the other three sections requiring bonds is doubtless due to inadvertency. The same considerations which make court approval of a surety company bond unnecessary under these three other sections are equally applicable to this section.

MEMORANDUM ON PROPOSED AMENDMENT TO SECTION 542 OF THE CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE Assembly Bill 3436 

Section 542 relates to the manner in which property is attached. This proposed amendment would provide that in connection with attachments, underwritten title companies maintaining branch offices should be treated in the same manner as certain other corporations having branch offices, such as banks and savings and loan associations. 

More Detailed Explanation. Paragraph (6) of Section 542 now pro vides that debts owed to the defendant "by a banking corporation or association, building and loan association or title insurance company, maintaining branch offices, or credits or other personal property belonging to the defendant and in the possession of or under the control of such banking corporation or association, building and loan association or title insurance company, must be attached by leaving a copy of the writ and the notice with the manager or other officer * * * at the office or branch thereof at which the account evidencing such indebtedness of the defendant is carried, or at which such banking corporation or association, building and loan association or title insurance company has credits or other personal property belonging to the defendant in its possession or under its control; and no attachment shall be effective *** if the account evidencing such indebtedness is carried at an office or branch thereof not so served * * * 99 The proposed amendment would add to those financial institutions listed in the present section "underwritten title companies;" reference is made to Section 12402 of the Insurance Code for definition of the term "underwritten title company. That term is therein defined as meaning "each person engaged in the business of preparing title searches, title examinations, certificates or abstracts of title upon the basis of which a title insurer regularly issues title policies ***'' 

This amendment is desirable because the same policy reasons which induced the Legislature to enact the present provisions relating to banks, savings and loan associations and title insurance companies apply with equal force to underwritten title companies having branch offices. Briefly stated, those reasons are that any such institution having one or more branches located at places away from the home office are unduly burdened by problems of ascertaining the facts with respect to property which might be attached where service of the writ of attachment is made upon an office where neither the assets being attached nor any record of them is maintained. The responsibility for failure to respond promptly to the attachment served on some office other than that holding the obligation is unjust in view of the difficulty of immediately acting upon an attachment with respect to assets the existence of which

are not even known to the office served with the writ. These reasons, and others, led the Legislature in 1927 to adopt an amendment to Section 542 (Stats. 1927, Chapter 507) providing that service upon a bank with branches must be made upon the appropriate branch; in 1931, the 

Legislature made similar provisions for savings and loan asosciations and title insurance companies. Since that time a number of underwritten title companies have established branch offices, and it is reasonable to extend to such institutions these provisions. 

MEMORANDUM ON PROPOSED AMENDMENT TO SECTION 750, CODE OF CIVIL PROCEDURE Assembly Bill 2538 Prior to the enactment (in 1949) of Code of Civil Procedure Section 

749.1, Section 750 provided for the summons and method of service to be used in a quiet title action under Section 749 (20-year action). Section 749.1 (10-year action), like Section 749, depended upon the provisions of Section 750 as to the summons and method of service to be followed. Section 750, which refers to "the preceding section" is inaccurate because, since the enactment of Section 749.1, Section 750 contains provisions applicable both to Sections 749 and 749.1. The proposed amendment would pluralize this reference so that Section 750 refers to "the preceding sections" thus dispelling any uncertainty as to whether it is in fact applicable to both the preceding sections.

OTHER MATTERS CONSIDERED 

Several proposals were made to the committee for legislation along general lines and the following is the report of the committee in that connection. 

A. CONSOLIDATED PROBATES 

The committee did not undertake detailed study of the desirability of and mechanics by which probate proceedings could be consolidated where there are successive deaths without intervening probate proceedings. However, the matter was brought to the attention of committee, and it is recommended that this subject have the attention of succeeding interim judiciary committees.

In the meantime there is published herewith a brief opinion of the Legislative Counsel, dealing with the subject in general and indicating something of its complexities.

STATE OF CALIFORNIA OFFICE OF LEGISLATIVE COUNSEL 

SACRAMENTO, CALIFORNIA, January 26, 1954

HONORABLE EDWIN J. REGAN 

Weaverville, California

DEAR SENATOR REGAN: 

CONSOLIDATED PROBATES-No. 678 

Facts You have directed to our attention the fact that in many instances when a person dies who was the owner of a piece of real property of little value there is no administration of the estate. The ostensible heir simply takes, and continues in, possession of the property. This situation may recur several times as to the same property. Later, one of the heirs who may claim title by deeds from the others would like to dispose of the property and is confronted with a number of probate proceedings, perhaps as many as six or eight. Question

May the Legislature, by appropriate legislation, provide that the court could take jurisdiction of the entire matter in one proceeding? The title of the proceeding could indicate the names of all of the decedents, notice could be given to all of the heirs and to the creditors of all of the decedents and perhaps a limitation on the value of the property involved could be fixed, for example, at $2500. 

Opinion 

We have no doubt that the Legislature has the power to provide for such a consolidated probate. The net effect of the consolidated proceeding would be the same as if the several estates were probated separately. There would be no impairment of any vested right of any of the heirs of any of the decedents and the only change would be in the procedure. There are no constitutional problems. There would be some practical problems which would have to be solved in order to produce a workable statute.

Analysis 

The title to the property of a decedent passes upon his death to the persons entitled to succeed to his property by will or otherwise, subject to administration (Sec. 

300, Prob. C.). The Legislature may not, of course, impair a vested right. It may, however, change the procedure of probate and administration and make the change applicable to estates of previously deceased persons, so long as no vested rights are impaired (see Bryant's Estate, 3 Cal. 2d 58).

As we understand your proposal, it is simply to provide for the consolidation in one proceeding and in one court a number of proceedings affecting the same ( 71 )

property. We do not see how it possibly could be contended that this would impair any vested rights. The result would be that all of the persons who had an interest in the particular property would be ascertained and their rights declared. We have no doubt whatever that the Legislature could, by appropriate legislation, provide for such a consolidated probate. 

There would, however, be some problems involved in devising appropriate legislation for the purpose. The practical problems of counsel in attempting the probate of six or eight successive estates might be enormous, particularly if the decedents involved were numerous and left numerous heirs. The consolidation of the proceedings would neither add to or take from the number of such problems but it would raise some legal problems which should be solved in the statute. We will refer to some of these which have occurred to us so far. 

Jurisdiction of Court. Jurisdiction of the estate of a resident of this State is in the superior court of the county of his residence at the time of his death and jurisdiction of the estate of a nonresident is a variable depending on his place of death and the location of his property (Sec. 301, Prob. C.). Since all of the decedents may not have been residents or have died in the county in which the property is situated, it might be well to specify that the superior court of the county in which the property is situated has jurisdiction over the consolidated probate. 

Petitioner. When six or eight successive decedents are involved the person in possession of the property might not stand in such relationship to the more remote decedents as to have priority over the public administrator (see Sec. 422, Prob.

C.). To avoid any question it would be well to specify who may petition, for example, any person claiming an interest in the property. 

Scope of the Proceeding. Should it be limited to situations involving a single parcel of property or made applicable to two or more pieces of property as to which identical conditions exist? If a limit is placed on the value of the property there would be no great purpose in also limiting the number of parcels. If the value is limited it should be specified whether it is the appraised value or market value. 

Notice to Heirs. We assume from your question that these notices would be given in the consolidated proceeding in the same way as if the several probates were not consolidated.

Notice to Creditors. If a consolidated notice to creditors is to be used the logical place to publish it would be the county in which the proceeding is had, that is, the county in which the property is situated. In the case of some decedents that county will not be the county in which his estate would otherwise be probated, for example, if he was a resident of this State and a resident of some county other than the one in which the property is situated. We are not sure what the courts would hold in this respect. If they held that the creditor was entitled to have notice published in accordance with the law as of the time of the decedent's death, that is, in the county of decedent's residence, publication in the county in which the land is situated would not be sufficient. On the other hand, the consolidated proceeding would involve only a specific parcel, or parcels, of land and the proceeding would be somewhat a proceeding in rem. Since the res would be the specific property involved the publication in the county in which it is situated might be sufficient. Perhaps we could specify that the consolidated proceeding is in rem in the hope that such specification would be given sufficient weight if the question should arise. 

Inventory and Appraisal. Inasmuch as the same property will be involved in each of the successive estates no particular purpose would be served in filing separate inventories and appraisals. It would probably be sufficient to name all of the estates and have but one inventory and appraisal. 

Inheritance Tax. Apparently there has been some form of inheritance tax in this 

State since the collateral inheritance tax law of 1893 (1893:168:193). There is no statute of limitations in the present inheritance tax law (Sec. 14301, R. & T.C.) and, so far as we know, there was no such provision in any of the previous inheritance tax acts. Theoretically as to each estate the inheritance tax would depend upon the law in effect at the death of the decedent and upon the value of the property at that time. It is unlikely that there would be a tax due under the present law when the property was valued at $2500 or less. The specific exemptions (Secs. 

13801-13804, R. & T.C.) of the various classes of transferees (Secs. 13307-13310,

R. & T.C.) would take care of the situation where a spouse, lineal ancestor, or lineal descendent was the transferee (Secs. 13307 and 13801, R. & T.C.). The specific exemption of a brother or sister or descendant of a brother or sister of the decedent or of a wife or widow of a son or a husband or widower of a daughter is $2000 (Secs. 13308 and 13802, R. & T.C.). We do not think that the Legislature

could simply dispense with the tax entirely in the consolidated probate cases (see Estate of Stanford, 126 Cal. 112). Nor do we think it could arbitrarily impose the present tax on all of the estates involved because that might amount to a retroactive imposition of a higher tax. It might be that the petitioner could be given the option of having the inheritance tax calculated either on the basis of the law in effect and the value of the property at the time of the decedent's death or upon the basis of the present law and the present value of the property. Whatever remission of the tax and penalties this might involve could probably be justified on the theory that it serves a public purpose to bring the state claims for the tax into a current status (see California Employment Stabilization Commission v. Payne, 31 Cal. 

2d 210).

Under this arrangement when there was no tax due on any of the estates under present law the petitioner would elect to let that law apply. In such a case the inheritance tax appraiser's certificate that there was no tax due on any of the estates could be incorporated in one document. 

Form of the Bill. Probably the best thing to do would be to add a chapter to the Probate Code dealing with consolidated probates and making its specific provisions controlling. Once the bill was drafted the probate provisions in the remainder of the code could be examined to determine if they should be left applicable or a special provision made on the subject in the new chapter. This would disclose such matters as the following.

Fees. The statutory fees are based upon the value of the estate probated. Should they be allowed as to each estate probated? Also, it would seem that in a consolidated probate the administrator would normally have little or nothing to do, whereas the attorney would normally have a great deal more to do than in the case of a single estate of comparable value. Compare, for example, the amount of work ordinarily involved in probating a single $10,000 estate and that which would be expected in a consolidatated probate of four successive estates involving $2500 worth of property. 

Bond of Administrator.

The Administrator in a consolidated probate would rarely if ever handle any actual money or other assets. His bond could either be dispensed with or provision made for the court's dispensing with it when it appeared unlikely that any money would be handled by the administrator. 

Ordinarily there would be nothing for the administrator to ac- 

Accounting. 

count for.

The foregoing are matters which have occurred to us at this juncture. It is quite probable that, if and when we come down to drafting a bill, other questions will develop. As we understand your request, you are not at this time requesting us to draft a bill.

In conclusion, we may sum up by saying that we have no doubt as to the power of the Legislature to provide for consolidated probates and that, while the devising of a suitable bill will involve some difficulties, none of the difficulties we have so far perceived will prove insurmountable. 

Very truly yours, (Signed)

RALPH N. KLEPS 

Legislative Counsel

By JOSEPH W. PAULUCCI 

Deputy

B. MECHANICS LIENS 

The committee has found that there is a recurring problem of the desire of many people to change the existing mechanics liens structure in California. 

1. REPORT OF THE LOS ANGELES COUNTY GRAND JURY 

The matter received the attention of the 1953 Los Angeles County Grand Jury, and the final report of the criminal complaints committee of that body will indicate the problem and some suggested solutions.

Final Report of Criminal Complaints Committee To MR. HOWARD J. BRACE 

Foreman, and

Members of the 1953 Los Angeles County Grand Jury 

As of the date this report is written, the Criminal Complaints Committee has investigated 92 cases which have been brought to its attention during the current year by citizens of this county. In addition thereto we have on numerous occasions met with law enforcement officers and recommended hearings by the grand jury or have directed such officers to file complaints rather than occupy the time of the grand jury as a whole.

The foregoing complaints were on the average of a routine nature and appear to be quite normal for any grand jury under our state law. 

One case, however, deserves special mention, in that it brought forcibly to our attention what we consider to be a grave deficiency in our state law. We refer to the laws and procedures normally followed in the building of a home within our State and are of the opinion that the status quo leaves much to be desired. 

It is a fact that within the current year a number of citizens of this county were faced with grave financial loss or even the loss of their homes by individuals connected with the contracting business. The mere fact that such individuals now face prosecution under the laws of this State does not in any way lessen the loss these citizens have sustained. We have brought this matter to the attention of Members of our State Legislature and have been assured that the Senate Interim Judiciary Committee will proceed to hold public hearings in the matter in an effort to arrive at some solution to the problem. We specifically invited the committee's attention to the fact that of all parties concerned in the building of a home the only one who is not protected adequately by law is the citizen who desires to build his own home and which citizen eventually foots the bill. The lending institution, the contractor, the laborer, and the individual or the corporation who supplies the material are all protected under our law. We believe it is a sad criterion of our system that the individual citizen who makes possible this lucrative business to all of the foregoing, who are protected by law, gets no protection under the law and further that the very people who are protected under the law constantly oppose legislation to protect the man who puts the bread and butter in their mouth. 

Since our tenure of office expires on December 31st, our swan song will have been sung but we do urge succeeding grand juries of this and other counties within the State of California to support any effort by the Legislature to correct this abominable condition.

We further submit to the electorate and the press that it is their duty to support constructive legislation, regardless of what action pressure groups may take at Sacramento or in other parts of the State. And finally, we recommend legislation along the following lines: (a) All lending institutions should be required by law to hold the funds borrowed by an individual to build a home in the same manner that a trust fund is handled and to adopt a basic attitude that the borrower is the primary customer and not the contractor who brings the individual to the lending institution for the underwriting business; (b) To facilitate the lending institution the present mechanic's lien laws should be modified to require that any individual who is now permitted to file mechanic's liens should be required to furnish the lending institution and the home builder copies of all invoices which they submit for payment. Such invoices should be clearly marked to indicate that should the bill remain unpaid for 30 days after submission a mechanic's lien will be filed against the home. (c) The foregoing invoices should be mailed by registered United States mail within 10 days after the material is laid down at the building site or the labor has been performed; (d) The lending institution should be required by law to retain at least 20 percent of the total funds involved in escrow for a period sufficient to protect the home owner against the filing of liens after the completion notice of the home has been a matter of public record.

We urgently petition each and every member of the Legislature to correct this gross inequality in the law and we want to distinctly remind them that this grand jury has no quarrel with the principle that all the parties whose interests are now protected under the law should retain that protection. We submit, however, that

such protection can be retained and concurrently therewith the law could be amended to secure equal protection for the individual citizen who wants to build his own home and in whom this grand jury has a benevolent interest. 

Respectfully submitted,

CRIMINAL COMPLAINTS COMMITTEE (Signed) Frank S. Ross, Chairman (Signed) Evelyn Flyer 

(Signed) Harry W. Harrison 

(Signed) Don Thompson 

(Signed) A. B. Miller, Secretary 

(Signed) John D. Hackstaff 

(Signed) A. K. Pattison (Signed) James B. Williams 

2. VIEWS OF MCGILVRAY, MCGILVRAY & CAMERON 

As the result of several hearings held on the subject, the committee was given the detailed views of the law firm of McGilvray, McGilvray & Cameron who have a wide knowledge of the operation of the present Mechanics Lien Law and presented a detailed memorandum presenting their views. This communication is reprinted in full, as follows: 

LAW OFFICES OF MCGILVRAY, MCGILVRAY & CAMERON 

SACRAMENTO 14, CALIFORNIA, November 12, 1954 

Re: Report of Interim Committee, California State Legislature, Both the Senate and Assembly

JOHN A. BOHN, Counsel Senate Interim Judiciary Committee 

Benicia, California 

DEAR JOHN: As you will remember, the Building Material Dealers' Credit Association Committee which I represent together with Mr. James Dean, its Secretary- 

Manager, agreed to furnish to your committee certain information in regard to mechanics' liens in general and certain items in particular at a hearing held in San Diego on September 20, 1954. There was some delay in getting the information and verifying facts and then I was out of the State for a while. We are furnishing the following information in the nature of answering the questions raised and also as comments as to our position in the matter. In particular, there was a conflict raised as to the number of mechanics' liens that have been filed in Los Angeles County between figures given by Mr. Dean of the Building Material Dealers' Credit Associa tion of Los Angeles and Mr. Richard Howlett of the Title Insurance and Trust 

Company of Los Angeles. Mr. Howlett stated that he had checked the filing through his office and with the recorder of Los Angeles County and had found that approximately 9,000 liens had been filed in the immediately preceding year. Mr. Dean had testified that his records showed an excess of 17,000 liens had been filed. In this connection, Mr. Dean contacted Mr. Thomas of the "Southwest Builder and Contractor" whose office compiles the Daily Construction Report, which report is known to the trade as the "Green Sheet." That is, daily reports are clipped and placed on record in the Building Material Dealers' Credit Association files, and whether one lien or 20 is filed on a particular piece of property, the B. M. D. C. A. counts them only as one lien. Mr. Thomas reports to us that he feels there is absolutely no error in his record. He states that no one has ever questioned the information compiled by them, that they have been in business since 1917 and that the information furnished is as accurate as can be obtained. We, therefore, feel that the statements made by 

Mr. Dean based upon these facts are correct. In addition to that, Mr. Koontz of the Contractors' State License Board came to the office of the B. M. D. C. A. and reviewed the completion and lien files and stated that it was his opinion that he felt the record of the B. M. D. C. A. was correct and accurate and that he was so advising his Sacramento office. Mr. Dean further states that he does not believe his records are inaccurate due to the fact that they clip and paste the record of all liens filed and do not copy at any time. 

You will remember, that we were considerably startled by a statement made by Mr. Gordon Stater, Attorney for the Building Contractors' Association, to the effect that one out of every seven contractors had had a complaint filed against him. We,

at the time, expressed doubt as to this and Mr. Dean checked with Mr. Ed Ford, the State Registrar of Contractors, for the year 1953. I attach hereto a copy of the report of the Contractors' State License Board for the year 1953 and a portion of the year 1954. Apparently, the total number of cases wherein complaints had been alleged totaled 2,370 for the year 1953 and the first six months of 1954. These violations, as shown by the schedule which I am marking Exhibit A of this letter, cover a great number of sections of the Business and Professions Code commencing with Sections 7069 through 7122. In this schedule you will note that in disciplinary actions filed for the calendar year 1953 there were 402 and in this 402 there were only 84 cases wherein liens were filed. For the first six months of 1954, the number of disciplinary actions filed are 244 involving cases where liens were filed in 113 or a considerably higher proportion. I should like to call to your attention that for the combined figures, 367 of the complaints fall under Section 7116 of the Business and Professions Code. This section provides that anyone who wilfully or fraudulently causes substantial injury is subject to disciplinary action. 

Two hundred ninety-six (296) cases were filed under Section 7113, which section provides that disciplinary action may be taken for failure to complete a contract. 

Two hundred sixty-two (262) cases were filed under Section 7109, which provides for disciplinary action for a wilful departure from plans without consent. 

Two hundred eight (208) cases were filed under Sections 7108 which provides for disciplinary action for diversion or misapplication of funds or property. 

Two hundred one (201) cases were filed under Section 7120, which provides for disciplinary action where there is a lawful failure to pay money when due for materials or services or fails denial of liability to obtain a discount or other delay. 

Generally, all of these sections deal with cases where a contractor has wilfully or deliberately failed to fulfill his part of the contract or has diverted money or withheld funds. Mr. Ford of the Contractors' State License Board stated to Mr. Dean that in his opinion the year 1954 showed a decided increase over 1953 in disciplinary actions. I think this is born out by the statistics attached in Exhibit A. 

Mr. Gordon Stater also submitted to the committee a proposed amendment to the 

California Mechanics' Lien Law based upon some provisions of the Mechanics' Lien 

Law of the State of Washington. We furnished to the committee the Washington provision which apparently is their Section 60.04.02 of the Labor Laws of the State of Washington and bears the inacting data at the conclusion of the paragraph from which I have copied. I am attaching hereto as Exhibit B, this section. This section is rather well annotated in the Washington Labor Law volume but I am not quoting these annotations. However, I call them to the attention of the committee. If, by any chance, the volume that is in the hands of the committee does not contain these annotations, we will furnish them. It should be pointed out that Mr. Stater, in his proposed amendment, did not want the notice to contain the provision that a lien might be claimed for the value of the materials furnished. We submit that the proposed amendment of Mr. Stater is not a copy of the law of the State of Washington on this subject and is quite misleading. 

We also stress the fact that in our opinion a change such as that recommended by Mr. Stater, representing the Building Contractors' Association would be of little value to a home owner unless the home owner fully understood the full text of the Mechanics' Lien Law and what it would mean to him if the contractor should fail to live up to his obligations under his contract. To expect the average home owner who is building his first and probably only house to grasp the intrication of the Mechanics' Lien Law is to ask the impossible. It is the duty of the Legislature, as we see it, to protect these individuals. Men of consequence, public bodies and large groups are well protected because they require bonds in their construction work. The person that needs protection is the home owner who has struggled and saved and has probably finally accumulated enough to make a down payment of $2,000 or $3,000 and expects to build for his family the house of his dream. To him, it is his castle. The economical shock and set-back that can come to a family of modest means where a contractor diverts funds from one job to another, as is so often the case, can never be estimated.

In connection with the Washington Mechanics' Lien Law Proposal, Mr. Dean inquired of Mr. H. E. Busch, Supervisor of Industrial Relations, of the State of 

Washington, Department of Labor and Industries, as to the effect of the Mechanics' Lien Law in the State of Washington. His reply is attached hereto marked Exhibit 

C. You can see therefrom that they have no information as to the number of liens

filed in the state. Apparently, the information is unavailable, consequently, we do not see how Mr. Stater could make the statement he did that the Washington law provided the remedy that is sought by the Grand Jury of Los Angeles County. We believe that Mr. Stater should be called upon to furnish facts verifying his statement that the Washington law would be the cure-all for the present, bad situation that exists in the State of California. I have discussed this problem with many contractors. One man that I discussed this matter with has been in the building supply business all his life and his father before him. Other contractors have done likewise. I believe that the good contractors, if they would advertise that all of their jobs are bonded, that they could clean their own house. This would do away with the fly-by-nights. However, they have never been willing to clean house themselves or to discipline their own members. 

I should like to call to your attention that the Assembly Judiciary Subcommittee had a hearing in Los Angeles on October 6, 1954. This hearing was reported and a transcript of the testimony at this hearing is available through Mr. Robert Fort, their counsel, who has his office in the Nicolaus Building at Sacramento. At this 

Assembly subcommittee hearing a good number of the same people that were at your hearing were present. In addition, an attorney by the name of Joseph K. Horton, who represents Southern California groups. He suggested that it might be possible to make it the responsibility of the contractor to notify the financing institution and the owner as to who would furnish supplies on each individual job and subsequently be required to furnish the lending or financial institution with certified labor and material releases. What position the B. M. D. C. A. would take on this suggestion 

I am not yet authorized to state.

I can state, however, that we would not endorse any plan wherein suppliers of building materials would be required to notify the owner as to deliveries of materials to a particular job.

At Assembly subcommittee hearing, October 6, 1954, discussion was had as to 

Assembly Bill 2440. I am attaching to this letter as Exhibit D, copy of Assembly 

Bill 2440 for your information. This bill was introduced by the Assemblyman, Patrick McGee, at the request of B. M. D. C. A. The bill was never pressed but was referred to the Interim Committee for study. The purpose of this bill was to provide that bonds would be given on private work. 

Mr. Dean, in testifying at the Assembly subcommittee hearing, outlined that in 

1952 there were 280 bankruptcies involving the building industry with liability over eight million dollars and assets of approximately five million. In 1953, similar bankruptcies total 401 with liabilities over eighteen million and assets over ten million. 

For the year 1954, up until September 23, 1954, there were 443 similar bankruptcies with liabilities of over twenty million and assets of seven million and that the average recovery after allowing for preferred claims probably would not return to the creditors more than a 2 percent dividend. Mr. Dean further stated that the number of mechanics' liens recorded each month in Los Angeles County was inrceasing and that without question the year 1954 would show the largest number of recorded mechanics' liens.

The views of the various interested parties such as the California Bankers, represented by Mr. Ed Landells of San Francisco, whom you well know, Mr. Richard 

Tuttle of the California Land Title Association, and Mr. Floyd Cerini are all set forth in the transcription referred to above. In addition to being executive vice president of Land Title Insurance Company, Mr. Cerini also represents a great number of builders' organizations numbering over 3,000 builders. It is his opinion that a compulsory bond provision would add materially to the cost of each individual home. A rather startling statement was made by Mr. Carl Meyers, who is Secretary- 

Manager of Builders' Exchange at Santa Ana, California and who is a member of the State Builders' Exchange Legislative Committee. Mr. Dean reports to me that he stated at this hearing that contractors were diverting funds from one job to another and that in his opinion contractors would not be able to operate if they were forced to bond individual jobs and maintain separate job accounts and not divert funds from one job to another. To me, this is reprehensible and is not to be condoned and there is absolutely no reason for it. In talking to one banker on this situation just a few days ago, he made the comment that the only thing a home owner could do when he found himself in the all too familiar situation of having his job short, was to send the contractor to somebody else and get the contractor another job. This robbing Peter to pay Paul in our opinion should be stopped and should not be tolerated further.

Mr. Dean also states that Mr. Meyers, referred to above, made a statement that in 

Orange County there are over 2,000 contractors but that only 500 of them are licensed. This to me also seems to be a reprehensible situation and that the Contractors License Board should have augmented funds with which to completely investigate this situation and to institute criminal proceedings as they are certainly called for. One of the peculiar side-lines of Mr. Meyers' statement as reported to me by Mr. Dean is that he felt that each contractor should put up a bond with the 

State of California for faithful performances of his contracts. I am not authorized to state the views of the people I represent as to this suggestion, but, personally, I believe it has a great deal of merit. In conclusion I would like to state that it is still the position of B. M. D. C. A. that a bill should be presented to the Legislature that would provide for bonding on all construction work or in lieu thereof that the funds for construction would be escrowed and could only be paid out for a particular job. I should like to call to the attention of the committee that almost all other parties who handle funds such as escrow funds must keep separate accounts. 

Of this class, though, it probably is not exhausted, are those who deal with a collection of accounts under the Collection Agency Act. These funds must be segregated and may not be commingled. Attorneys must maintain funds of clients in a trust fund and may not commingle. Banks and trust companies must do the same. 

There is no reason why a contractor who accepts funds of the owner should not also be required to do the same unless, of course, he gives an adequate bond for the faithful performance of his contract. The Dean of the State Bar of California who is probably more familiar with the Mechanics' Lien Law of the State of California is Glen Behymer, 415 Auditorium Building, 427 W. Fifth Street, Los Angeles 13, California. He states in a letter to Mr. Dean of October 1, 1954, that he will "vig orously support a bond bill primarily for the reason that only in that manner can an owner be properly protected." I am attaching hereto a portion of his opinion to 

Mr. Dean of October 1, 1954, for your information and I am marking it Exhibit E. 

We have read with interest and have discussed at great detail the report of the Grand Jury of Los Angeles County with which you are familiar and feel that legislative action must be taken to cure a growing evil in the building industry in the State of California. We feel that it is one of the primary pieces of legislation that should be considered. We recognize, of course, that all of the allied groups must be brought into the discussion and that the views of bankers, land title companies, trust companies, escrow companies, surety company, building associations, labor, home building groups, contractors in general, and representatives of the Contractors State License Board must be considered and recognized. On behalf of our association, Mr. 

Dean and I will be available at hearings either before the beginning of the 1955 session or at any committee hearings that may be called. We should like to know if at all possible before the session commences in January what legislation may be proposed by the Senate Committee. We should also like to have your comments and views as to the matters presented in this letter. 

I am sending copies of this letter to Mr. Dean, Mr. Behymer, Mr. Robert Fort, the attorney for the Judiciary Subcommittee, and Mr. Connor. 

Very truly yours,

KZADRA An act to amend Section 1188.1 of, and to repeal and add Section 1188.2 to, the Code of Civil Procedure, relating to mechanics' liens.

1188.1. The liens provided for in this chapter; except as otherwise in this article provided, are preferred to any lien, 5 mortgage, deed of trust, or other encumbrance upon the premises and improvements to which the liens provided for in this 7 chapter attach which may have attached subsequent to the time 8 when the building, improvement, structure, or work of improve- 9 ment in connection with which the lien claimant has done his 10 work or furnished his material was commenced; also to any 

11 lien, mortgage, deed of trust, or other encumbrance of which 12 the lien claimant had no notice and which was unrecorded at the time the building, improvement, structure, or work of im- 14 provement on which such lien claimant has done his work or 15 for which he has furnished his material, appliances or power 16 was commenced; also to any mortgage, deed of trust or other 

17 encumbrance recorded subsequent to the effective date of this 18 section and before the commencement of the building, improve- 19 ment, structure or work of improvement in connection with 20 which the lien claimant has done his work or furnished his 21 material, but only to the extent that the loan or obligation 22 secured by such mortgage, deed of trust or other encumbrance 23 is made or incurred for the purpose of financing the construc- 24 tion, alteration, addition to or repair of such building, im- 25 provement, structure or work of improvement, including a work 26 of improvement of the character referred to in Section 1184.1

of this code, and a recital of such mortgage, deed of trust, or 2 other encumbrance, of the extent to which it so secures a loan or obligation for the purpose of financing a work of improve- 4 ment, or a recital that it does not secure a loan or obligation 5 for such purpose, shall be prima facie evidence thereof.

10 the owner of the land or the holder of any mortgage, deed of 11 trust, or other encumbrance thereon which otherwise would be 12 inferior to the liens provided for in this chapter shall procure 13 or cause to be procured, a bond, with good and sufficient sureties, 14 which shall be conditioned for the payment in full of the claims 15 of all persons performing labor upon or furnishing materials 16 to be used in, or furnishing appliances, teams or power contrib- 17 uting to, the building, improvement, structure, or work of 18 improvement, in connection with which the lien claimant has 19 done his work or furnished his material, then such mortgage, 20 deed of trust, or other encumbrance shall be prior and para- 21 mount to the liens provided for in this chapter arising out of 22 such building, improvement, structure or work of improvement, 23 irrespective of whether or not such mortgage, deed of trust, or 24 other encumbrance attached or was recorded before or after the 25 time when such building, improvement, structure or work of 

26 improvement was commenced. 28 27 Such bond given under this section must be a bond with good and sufficient sureties in an amount not less than fifty (50) 29 percent of the face principal amount of such mortgage, deed 30 of trust or encumbrance, and shall be given for the sole benefit 31 of persons performing labor upon, or furnishing materials to 32 be used in, or furnishing appliances, teams, or power contribut- 33 ing to, the building, improvement, structure, or work of im- 34 provement to which said bond refers, and by its express terms 35 shall give such persons a right to recover upon said bond in any 36 suit brought to foreclose the liens provided for in this chapter, 37 or in a separate suit brought on said bond. Exhibit E 

Because of my belief that first, the owner is the person who has the least protection under the present law and particularly needs it (I refer only to owners on private work); second, that the materialmen, subcontractors and laborers have little or no protection because they usually sit behind mortgages and trust deeds that absorb practically the entire value of the property; they need somewhat more realistic protection in order to carry out the constitutional mandate and the spirit of the decision in the Roystone v. Darling case; third, that the only legally sure way of giving the owner that protection is to use principles laid down in the Roystone v.

Darling case, and give the owner that protection through the carrying into effect of the letter and spirit of the favored classification made by the constitutional provisions which permit legislation for the benefit of lien claimants reached through the medium of regulating and governing the conduct and contracts of the owner of the property and the person contracting to construct buildings thereon. Incidentally by using that approach and only in that manner, in my opinion, can the proper interests of the owners be protected under the parlous conditions actually existing in the building industry and at the same time an effective remedy be given to laborers, subcontractors and materialmen in view of the legislative desire to give to many lending institutions priority over everybody else concerned in the matter and to the desire of such institutions to not have their priority in any wise conditioned upon the furnishing of a bond. The present law, as you know, provides that if the corporate surety bond is furnished it will be conclusively presumed to be a good and sufficient bond. This, however, does not require mandatorily that all such bonds be corporate surety bonds. My own opinion is that if the money lending group do not want to be the source of policing the picture, then the only other approach is by mandatorily requiring for the validity of any contract between owner and contractor that one of two things be true: (a) The contract provide, in connection with contracts over $1,000, as did the law prior to 1911, that 25 percent of the contract price be withheld in the owner's hands as a sort of cash bond for his protection and the protection of lien claimants until a reasonable period to be fixed in the statute after the expiration of the time for filing liens, or that the contractor shall have furnished a statutory labor material bond and a 50 percent completion bond to the owner. Contractors, therefore, who desire not to have 25 percent in cash withheld, and desiring a smaller retention of cash in the hands of the owner, will procure the bonds. Contractors who are indifferent as to the 25 percent retention, or who are unable to get the bond from a corporate surety, will then do either one of two things: Permit the 25 percent retention, or will furnish individual sureties interested in the success and welfare of the contractor. This would enable honest and competent contractors inadequately financed to furnish individual surety bonds. In my opinion, however, there will always be sufficient surety companies willing to write the bonds for honest and competent men, who write the bond even though their financial background and resources are relatively meager and will accept indemnitors for their additional security and protection. 

I am not going to review the situation more fully as you understand my views I think quite well but if I appear at the hearing, as I have promised to do, I want to be able to speak out fully on my views but if desired by the committee of the association, I will express those views merely as my own personal opinion, long interested in the building industry, who has watched its progress ever since the commencement of my practice, and who realizes the troubles with which it has been beset, and who is interested primarily in two things: first, that the owner obtain adequate protection; and secondly, that the laborer, subcontractor and materialman, if he is not to have direct protection through an adequate mechanic's lien as contemplated by the constitutional mandate, receive it through the indirect method of a bond provision, which I believe is the only possible way of protecting both the owner and the materialmen, subcontractor and laborer. The money lenders are fully protected, provided they take the necessary steps in connection with their loans, by having priority over everyone else. The contractor, if he is a reasonable man, should be willing to agree upon a program of legislation which will protect the owner and insure that the bills incurred on the job will be fully paid so that those who furnish labor or materials, or both are as well protected in the getting of their money as the money lender is in getting his. 

3. VIEWS OF GLEN BEHYMER 

The views of this firm are expressed in a series of communications, copies of which were forwarded to the committee, and in view of the wide experience of this office in matters affecting mechanics liens, their views are herewith set forth in full.

LAW OFFICES OF GLEN BEHYMER LOS ANGELES 13, CALIFORNIA, October 15, 1954 Re: Assembly Bill 2440 Relating to Mechanics' Liens Re: Mechanics' Liens Legislation Generally 

GENTLEMEN: In this letter I am going to address myself to a broader aspect of the picture than a mere discussion of Assembly Bill 2440, introduced by Assemblyman Patrick McGee at the 1953 Regular Session of the Legislature. I trust that this approach will be approved. The reason that I am indulging in approaching it from a broader picture is the intense interest now shown in the necessity for suitable revisions of the Mechanics' Lien Statutes. 

As you doubtless know, the Senate Interim Judiciary Committee, of which Senator Burt W. Busch is chairman, is considering, among other things, revisions in the 

Mechanics' Lien Statutes, especially in considering whether any revision is needed in the Mechanics' Lien Laws to afford better protection to the home owner and, if so, the nature of such change. 

This may be, in part, due to the interest of the District Attorney of Los Angeles County, the Criminal Division of the City Attorney's office of the City of Los Angeles and the interest of the Attorney General arising out of the many distressing failures of speculative builders and contractors and the consequent and terrific economic losses sustained by owners of property built by contractors and purchasers of such properties from speculative builders, and the consequent disastrous failures and losses sustained by laborers, subcontractors and materialmen. 

The two groups primarily adversely affected by the present unsatisfactory status of the laws are the owner, on the one hand, and the laborers, subcontractors and materialmen, on the other. Another group indirectly affected by the present situation is the group of contractors and subcontractors of standing, stability, competency, honesty and financial integrity who are hurt largely by the fact that under existing laws incompetent, dishonest and financially irresponsible contractors are encouraged to remain in business, with the inevitable end result that jobs in which they are engaged are abondoned incomplete with many bills owing to laborers, subcontractors and materialmen unpaid.

This places a black eye upon the entire contracting fraternity and keeps in business scalawags and incompetents who should be eliminated from the scene. Competition of this type of contractor results in many bankruptcies and failures, not only of themselves, but of the reputable and competent people who are compelled to compete with them.

The writer has specialized in matters affecting the building industry as a lawyer ever since he was admitted to practice in February, 1908, in the State of California, and has represented, over that period, owners, architects, contractors, subcontractors, materialmen, mortgage companies, speculative builders and, in fact, every group affected by this large and important industry, and from time to time has been called in to aid in the preparation of measures attempting to improve conditions. 

As a lawyer, I have reached the conclusion that the only constitutional approach to legislation which will cure the problems affecting the groups who, at this time, are the least protected is to make the approach based upon the fundamental principle, so far as the constitutional angle is concerned, laid down in the case of Roystone 

Company v. Thomas Darling, et al., 171 Cal. 526.

The lawyers on any legislative committee considering mechanic's lien legislation in the State of California should familiarize themselves fully with the historical, legal background of mechanic's lien legislation in California and, in doing so, give special consideration to the very leading case of Roystone Company v. Darling, and the many cases cited and referred to both in the major opinion in that case and in the concurring opinion written by Henshaw, J., and concurred in by Melvin, J. 

Many of the leading members of the bar specializing in the practice in Los Angeles County took part in that case and the legal problems were adequately briefed. The case and the law, from the constitutional angle, largely hinges upon the fact that mechanics, materialmen, artisans and laborers of every class are made into a special class by Section 15 of Article XX of the Constitution of 1879. Quoting from page 

543, of that opinion, you find that our Supreme Court recognizes that fact as a basic and fundamental thing in the following language: "The fact that the constitution confers upon persons performing labor or furnishing materials for the construction of a building the right to a lien thereon, at once establishes these persons as a class, and makes a constitutional distinc-

tion between them and all other persons making contracts. This justifies legislation for the benefit of such claimants and governing the conduct and contracts of the owner of the property and the person contracting to construct buildings thereon."

The case which we are considering was the first case to go into the constitutional angle insofar as the act of May 1, 1911 (Stats. 1911, p. 1313) is concerned, which revised and changed many of the provisions of the Mechanics' Lien Law and made radical changes in the law as it stood prior to 1911. 

Prior to the Constitution of 1879 the Mechanics' Lien Laws in California were based entirely upon acts of the Legislature. The constitutional provision was enacted in 1879, which provision reads as follows: 

"Mechanics, materialmen, artisans, and laborers of every class shall have a lien upon the property upon which they have bestowed labor or furnished material, for the value of such labor done and material furnished; and the legislature shall provide, by law, for the speedy and efficient enforcement of such liens."

The Legislature, in 1885, as stated by the Supreme Court in Roystone Company v. Darling, Supra, at p. 532 : "*** amended sections 1183 and 1184 of the code by providing that in all building contracts the contract price should be payable in installments at specified times after the beginning of the work, that at least one-fourth thereof should be made payable not less than thirty-five days after the completion of the work contracted for, that all such contracts exceeding one thousand dollars should be in writing, subscribed by the parties thereto, and should be filed in the office of the county recorder before the work was begun thereunder, that if these regulations were followed, liens upon the property for the erection of the structure should be confined to the unpaid portion of the contract price, but that all contracts which did not conform thereto, or which were not filed as provided, should be void, that in such case the contractor should be deemed the agent of the owner, and the property should be subject to a lien in favor of any person performing labor or furnishing material to the contractor upon the building for the value of such labor or material. This law, with some amendments not material to our discussion, remained in force until the enactment of the revision of 1911 aforesaid."

It will be observed that the provision for the retention in the hands of the owner of one-fourth of the contract price until after the expiration of the period for filing liens constituted a protection both to the owner and the laborer, subcontractor or materialman.

The contractors, however, felt that a 25 percent retention in cash was more than their financial standings would, in many instances, permit for the reason that they would not have cash or credit sufficient to carry them over until after the lien time had expired, and they should be able to get progress payments with a smaller retention of cash in the hands of the owner. 

They made the contention to the Legislature in 1911 that their profits did not amount to as much as 25 percent of the contract price and that it was not a mere matter of waiting for their profit. 

As a result of discussions between the interested groups the measure adopted in 1911 contemplated that the contractor would furnish a bond, would not be compelled to provide for a 25 percent retention and could make any provision in his contract as to time and method of payment and could even receive the contract price in kind. The amended sections, therefore, as observed by the Supreme Court in its opinion in Roystone Company v. Darling, (I quote from page 534): "*** do not prescribe the form of building contracts, nor do they fix the time or the manner of payment of the contract price or require its payment in installments. All these things are left to the will of the owner and the contractor as they may agree. They do not require that one-fourth of the contract price, or any part thereof, shall be made payable after completion. The parties are at liberty to contract for payment in advance, or in specific property. Such contracts are not declared void by the present law."

The bond suggested by the 1911 act is not mandatory but permits the owner to procure a bond and if he procures the bond, complying with the statute (a 50 percent labor and material bond), then the liability of his property for liens is limited

by the contract price, for the statute provides that where it would be equitable so to do and a good and sufficient bond has been furnished in the manner laid down by the statute, the court must "*** restrict the recovery under such liens to an aggregate amount equal to the amount found to be due from the owner to the contractor, and render judgment against the contractor and his sureties on said bond for any deficiency or difference there may remain between said amount so found to be due to the contractor and the whole amount found to be due to claimants for such labor or materials or both. No change or alteration of the work or modification of any such contract between the owner and his contractor shall release or exonerate any surety or sureties upon any bond given under this section. It is the intent and purpose of this section to limit the owner's liability, in all cases, to the measure of the contract price where he shall have filed or caused to be filed in good faith with his original contract a valid bond with good and sufficient sureties in the amount and upon the conditions as herein provided. 

It shall be lawful for the owner to protect himself against any failure of the contractor to perform his contract and make full payment for all work done and materials furnished thereunder by exacting such bond or other security as he may deem satisfactory."

This statute worked out well in practice for a few years because great publicity was given to the change and the contractors more or less lived up to their pledge that they would furnish bonds to the owners in the event the statute was amended, as they suggested, and the 25 percent retention was done away with. 

It became the custom, in nearly every contract that was written, to provide only for a 10 percent retention in the hands of the owner. However, as the years have gone on since then only in a few cases do the contractors furnish bond to the owners, except, of course, bonds are furnished for faithful performance and the payment of claims of laborers, subcontractors and materialmen on public work because of the mandatory requirements of all the various public work bond statutes. 

The doing away with the bond, in actual practice, on private jobs has brought into the field an endless number of incompetent, dishonest and irresponsible contractors.

The Supreme Court, in Roystone Company v. Darling, then goes on to say, at page 537: "We have shown that when this act was passed it was the established doctrine of this State that the Legislature cannot create mechanics' liens against real property in excess of the contract price, where there is a valid contract, but that it is within the legislative power, in order to protect and enforce the liens provided for in the constitution, and so far as for that purpose may be necessary, to make reasonable regulations of the mode of contracting, and even of the terms of such contracts, and to declare that contracts shall be void if they do not conform to such regulations." (Italics ours.) 

As stated by the Supreme Court in Roystone Company v. Darling (see page 538) the effect of the 1911 act, which is the present law: “*** is that persons contracting for the erection of buildings or structures on their property must require the contractor to furnish such bond, and must file the same with the contract in the recorder's office, or, as an alternative, he must see to it that the value of the work and materials used in the building by the contractor is paid to the persons who furnish the same. A contract not accompanied by such bond is not declared to be invalid, but it furnishes no protection to the owner against liens for labor and material on the building." 

The absence of the bond, therefore, not only leads to injury to the owner on private work, but also leads to injury to the laborers, subcontractors and materialmen, for neither a completion or labor or materialman's bond exists in such instances.

So far as the owner is concerned when the contractor goes sour, as he does in altogether too many cases, the job is plastered with liens, the money in the loan fund (on most jobs a loan is involved) is used to complete the structure, if sufficient in amount, and then, if anything remains it will be pro rated among the lien claimants who, by reason of certain other sections of the Mechanics' Lien Law, are in most instances holders of liens which are junior to the lien held by the financial institution making the building loan and the second trust deed, which has been

given in many instances either for the purchase price of the land or for additional funds.

The result is, there being no equity in the property, both the owner and the lien claimants get frozen out by foreclosure of the first trust deed, or a compromise results by which the lien claims are released on a small percentage basis and the owner perchance puts up some extra money beyond what he already has in the job. 

The Supreme Court, in the Roystone Company v. Darling case, in its opinion at page 539, indicates what is constitutionally practicable in mechanics' lien legislation in the following language: "In view of the fact that our constitution itself gives to workmen and materialmen a lien upon property for the value of the work and materials they bestow upon it and directs the legislature to provide for the efficient enforcement of such liens, it is obvious that any legislative provision to that end which offers to the property owner a reasonable and practical mode of improving his property through a contractor at a fixed price and without further liability, should be considered as a legitimate exercise of the constitutional mandate.” (Italics ours.)

As is pointed out by the Supreme Court in the Roystone Company v. Darling case, on page 539, the law prior to the 1911 act did not permit the owner to make any payment in advance of his work and required, for the contract to be valid, that the owner provide in the contract that three-fourths of the contract price should be paid in installments as the work progressed or at completion, and one-fourth thirtyfive days or more after completion. (The time limit to file liens was 30 days from completion.)

In many of the cases that have caused trouble in recent years the contractor has obtained from the unwary owner very large proportions of the contract price in advance of the performance of any work. To any really prudent person this would suggest that he not do business with the contractor who suggests such a program. 

All too often the owner falls into the trap. 

The major opinion reaches the conclusion that the 1911 act is constitutional. 

This conclusion is explained on page 540 of the opinion in the following language: "The law of 1911 here involved does not deprive the owner of the right to contract for the improvement of his property. It allows him to contract freely for such improvement and upon such terms as he may deem for his best interests. All it exacts from him, as a condition of such exemption from liability, and in order to make his contract effective, is that he shall provide a reasonable security for the constitutional lien given for labor and materials furnished to his contractor. It is not an unreasonable burden. It is one which we think the people have the power to impose, and which we believe to be within the scope of the constitutional mandate in the section conferring such liens, and of the police power." (Italics ours.)

The court also says that the act as enacted in 1911 does not bring about any unlawful infringement of Section 1, Article I of the Constitution, and that: 

"The facts involved in the case have no necessary bearing upon the question whether or not the Legislature could in any case require the contractor to file a bond for the benefit of persons having claims against him regarding the same before entering upon the work." 

It will be noted from this last expression that the court does not express an opinion as to whether it would be constitutional to merely bluntly pass a statute requiring a contractor, as a mandatory matter on, let us say, all contracts exceeding $1,000, to furnish a 50 percent labor and material bond and, let us say, a 50 percent completion bond. The opinion distinguishes the bond contemplated by the 1911 act and involved in the case of Roystone Company v. Darling from the bond involved in 

Shaughnessey v. American Surety Co., 138 Cal. 543, stating in that respect, the 

"There is a marked difference between the law denounced as void in these cases and the act of 1911. The provisions of Section 1203 do not appear to have had any direct relation to the validity or invalidity of the contract for the building. It was an additional requirement, an additional burden upon the parties. It had no connection with or relation to the constitutional mechanic's lien. The bond required to be given by the act of 1911, on the contrary, is provided for the express purpose of enabling the owner to escape liability for his

building in any sum in excess of the contract price. It has a direct relation to the constitutional lien and to the effect of the contract for the improvement. The efficacy of the contract as a protection against such liens is made to depend directly upon the act of securing and filing the bond with the building contract. 

We think these differences are sufficient to distinguish this case from the line of cases above cited. We are unable to perceive any constitutional objection to the expedient of providing that by the execution and filing of such bond the owner may be protected against the delinquencies of his contractor while, at the same time, lien claimants are afforded a security for the payment of their claims. 

We therefore hold that the foregoing decisions are not applicable, and that the provision for requiring the bond is not unconstitutional or invalid." (Italics ours.)

It, therefore, clearly appears that the only sure constitutional approach to furnishing real protection to the owner on private work and at the same time trying to give some real protection to laborers, subcontractors and materialmen is to approach the matter along the lines in which the Supreme Court indicates, that it has been constitutionally approached in the decision in Roystone Company v. Darling.

There appears to be no other way to give either adequate protection as a matter of fact, nor protection which would stand up with absolutely definite assurance against an attack on constitutional grounds. 

Is there any reason why the owner on private work requires, to any less extent, the protection of a faithful performance and labor and material bond that a public body does on contracts for the construction of public works? Certainly it would appear that the State should be as zealous in its attempt to protect the private owner as it is in its attempt to protect the public body who lets a contract for public work.

As to the laborer, subcontractor or materialman, the constitutional right of lien, so far as any real practical protection is concerned, is practically destroyed by reason of the following facts: through the public desire that the Federal Government afford a reservoir of credit or of funds to aid building construction, various laws have been enacted from time to time which have resulted in the extension by financial institutions and individuals of loans for the improvement of real property beyond the amounts that would be ordinarily advanced by such institutions if they had no outside protection than the mere security of the encumbrance upon the real property involved.

The result is that loans are made through this artificial stimulus far in excess of what the average financial institution or private money lender might feel safe. Also, such lenders of money who are not protected by such outside artificial means through the legislation of which I speak, in order to obtain outlets for their money or, in turn, for competitive reasons, make a loan that might prove disastrously unsound should there be any descending plunge in values or any sharp increase in the buying power of money.

With these lending practices the materialman, subcontractor and laborer in the normal picture stands behind trust deeds which makes the protection of his lien more or less illusory and the club of the mechanic's lien is chiefly of some value to make people honest who otherwise might not be as much inclined so to be. 

Therefore, with the lender being given the priority which he needs in order to safely loan the equity in the property for the lien claimant all too frequently is virtually nil and the constitutional provision is defeated, and certainly the legislation now upon our books does not provide by law for the speedy enforcement of a really effective lien.

It would, therefore, appear to be obvious that in order to bring about the writing of a bond on private work resort must be had to a constitutional enactment. One of those constitutional methods would be as is contemplated by Assembly Bill 2440, to condition the priority of the money lender upon whether or not a statutory bond has been furnished by the contractor. If it has been furnished he has priority. 

If it has not the measure contemplates that his encumbrance shall be junior. 

What does this do in practical effect? It means that the lender will then say to the borrower "We cannot make you a loan unless you put your contractor under bond." Today the lender is in a difficult position in that respect. The contractor has advised the owner that one of the services that he performs in connection with this contemplated contract is to arrange for the financing. The owner may be building once in his lifetime.

The contractor has friends in some lending institution and he steers the owner to that institution. He is bringing to that institution repeat business connected with contract after contract. The owner may have been told by a friend or relative, or his attorney, that he should put the contractor under bond. 

The minute that he hints that he wants a bond the contractor is then very careful (unless he is willing to furnish the bond and able to do so) to steer the owner into the lending institution, possibly primarily and ostensibly with some other aspect of the proposed loan and, incidentally, to say to the representative of the lending institution: "The owner here has been advised, Mr. Jones, that I should furnish a faithful performance and labor and material bond. You know, Mr. Jones, that 

I complete my jobs without trouble and free of liens and that this is an expenditure that is entirely unnecessary."
