The representative of the lending institution is thus put right on the spot and he gives assurances to the owner to the ultimate effect, however uttered, that a bond is unnecessary, that the lending institution will see to the payout of the money or recommends the supposed protection of some escrow agency or joint control operator who, in turn, is getting his repeat business from the contractor and the lending institution, although the owner is paying the cost of this escrow or joint control protection and the cost all too frequently amounts to substantially the exact amount of the premium ordinarily paid on a complete and labor and material bond to a surety company which takes whatever risk there is in exchange for the premium. 

The only real protection that can be afforded the two groups for whom I speak is a bond provision equivalent to the faithful performance and labor and material bonds required on public work. The money lender has indicated that he does not want the priority he holds conditioned upon anything and, therefore, he is opposed to a measure such as AB 2440.

There is one other avenue of approach and that is to in part take the provisions of the law as it stood prior to 1911 and in part to take the provisions of the 1911 Act, but in a manner which will lead to some degree of protection in every case to the owner and the laborer, subcontractor and the materialman. 

This could be by requiring for the validity of a contract for construction involving, say, $1,000 or more, that one of two setups exist (a) 25 percent retention in cash of the building funds until a period to be fixed by the statute that would be after the expiration of the time for filing liens, such as 35 days after the recording of the Notice of Completion, or if no Notice of Completion is recorded, then 100 days after the actual completion of the structure and other adequate protection in the event the job is abandoned incomplete, by retention for a reasonable period, to be fixed by the law, after the time for filing liens shall have expired on jobs that are abandoned incomplete or in connection with which a Notice of Abandonment or Cessation From Labor shall have been recorded; or (b) that the owner procure from the contractor and properly file for record a 50 percent completion bond and a 50 percent labor and material bond.

Such a provision would enable the contractor who had sufficient credit or finances or backing to take a job without furnishing a bond provided there was in effect a bond of 25 percent in cash in the hands of the owner until after the job was completed and the time for filing liens expired. 

This cash bond could be made to inure, first, to the benefit of the owner and, secondly, to the benefit of subcontractors, laborers and materialmen. If the contractor desired to have a smaller retention and was willing and able to furnish a bond he would then be permitted to enter into a contract providing any type of payment plan that he wanted to make merely as a matter of contract and any form of retention that he and the money lender might agree upon as satisfactory to the lending institution and a retention and progress payments satisfactory to the surety or sureties upon the bond.

Obviously, in order to get the bond such a contractor would have to be a man of some degree of ability, of honest and upright repute and with some financial backing or funds of his own. In many instances the bonding companies use the practice of having indemnitors from among those interested in the success of the contractor, this being the custom most generally followed by said companies, aforesaid, in instances where the contractor himself does not have sufficient financing to go ahead, even though the retention is only 10 percent, which amount should be the amount of his proper profit on any job. If he can't wait for his profit he certainly should not be a contractor. He should have capital enough to permit him to do that, at least.

It has been urged by representatives of the more conservative surety companies that they do not care to assume the job of policing the contracting industry and that they will screen contractors rather closely before writing statutory labor and material bonds. This is a consummation devoutly to be wished, as it would assist in eliminating from the field those contractors who should not be permitted to remain as wolves preying upon the unwary owners. 

On the other hand, it may be observed that surety ocmpanies, in underwriting contractors' bonds, are interested primarily in the following factors: First and foremost, has the contractor the ability to properly carry on the work of a contractor expeditiously, with a minimum of overhead expense and in a manner which will permit him to succeed in his chosen field of activity. 

Secondarily, the underwriter is interested in the integrity of the contractor and if he has a bad record will, of course, be most reluctant to underwrite him in the future. Lastly, they examine into his assets, cash and credit standing, in order to determine whether he has enough cash or credit to carry on his job until he receives his first progress payment. There will always be well capitalized surety companies eager and willing to write contractors' bonds if the contractor deserves that consideration at the hands of the surety.

Someone will have to do the screening of the contractors. A surety company is more competent to do so than the owner. The owner needs the protection of the bond on private work to the same extent, at least, as a public body does on public work.

The money lender and the joint control operator have shown by their conduct that they are either unwilling to, or unable to, screen those desiring to engage in contracting for the construction of buildings. 

It has been said by opponents of a bond measure that it may reduce the volume of building construction because it would reduce the number of contractors available to undertake all of the work offered. In answer to this argument it is quite significant that materialmen and subcontractor groups did recommend and would welcome, vigorously and with almost universal acclaim, a legislation which would virtually force the writing of bonds on most private work or would otherwise protect the owner, laborer, subcontractor and materialman through a sufficiently large retention to assure completion and payment of bills incurred. 

If the volume of business would contract materially as a result of a soundly set up program of legislation in connection with the building industry it might affect slightly, through the reduction in total volume, materialmen, subcontractors and laborers. Yet they eagerly urge the passage of such a measure. 

Is it not better for both owner and laborer, as well as subcontractor and materialman, that the whole economy of the State in this respect be improved, even though their self interest might be injured slightly through a reduction in a total volume of business? Certainly it is better for all honest and honorable and efficient contractors, as well as for the owner, laborer, subcontractor and materialman, that jobs be completed on time with bills paid, incurred in the building construction, than to keep in the field contractors, so called, who prey upon the unwary owner, fail to complete their jobs and leave those jobs plastered with liens. 

It might be wise to consider, in connection with the matter of the improvement of the laws with respect to the building industry and with respect to mechanics' liens, and for the suitable protection of the owner, the legislation in the State of Mississippi, which was before the United States Supreme Court in the case of Hartford Accident and Indemnity Company and Aetna Casualty & Surety Company v. N. 0. 

Nelson Mfg. Co., (291 U. S. 352-361) 78 Law Ed. 840. The able opinion in this case was written by Mr. Justice Cardozo.

This statute was based upon a three-fold scheme. It provided for a notice to be given by materialmen or laborers, in writing, to the owner in the nature of a Notice to Withhold or Stop Notice. It further provided that no contractor should make any assignment to others which would have priority over the Stop Notices of materialmen and laborers, but that this subordination of assignments would not occur in instances where a solvent bond for completion was furnished to the owner by the contractor.

Then, by the third section of the statute, it was provided that any bond for faithful performance must include a guarantee that the contractor shall make payment of all materialmen and laborers' bills and that even if the provision were omitted in the bond, the bond should inure to the protection of materialmen and laborers as if the provision were expressed therein.

A full copy of the opinion in this case will be appended to this letter for perusal by such members of the committee as may desire to be more exactly advised as to the full content of the opinion. The bond involved in the case expressly provided that it should not inure to the benefit of laborers and materialmen by the following language: "No right of action shall accrue upon or by reason hereof to or for the benefit of any one other than the obligee named herein." 

The obligee was the owner. The decision is to the effect that the Fourteenth Amendment to the Federal Constitution does not preclude the recognition of liens in favor of materialmen and laborers upon lands improved or affected by their material or labor even though there be no privity of contract between them and the owner. 

Justice Cardozo observed that liberty of contract is not an absolute concept but is relative to many conditions of time and place and circumstances, also, that the business of insurance is one peculiarily subject to governmental supervision and control.

The Supreme Court of the United States, with respect to the equities, relies in part upon the same authority which is quoted with approval by our Supreme Court in English v. Olympic Auditorium, Inc., 217 Cal. 631 at page 641, to wit, Jones v. 

Great Southern Fireproof Hotel Co., 86 Fed. 370, 385 (C. C. A. Sixth Circuit).

With respect to the equities in favor of the materialmen and laborers, Mr. Justice 

Cardozo says: 

"The fundamental liberties protected by the Fourteenth Amendment do not include immunity from restraint so deeply rooted in policy and justice. (Citations.) The owner contracting with a builder or making payments under the contract may be required to give heed to the equities of a subcontractor or a workman adding value to the land. The builder may be required to give heed to the same equities in contracting with the owner or in disposing of his contract or of the moneys paid thereunder. "The statute of Mississippi was framed in a genuine endeavor to make these equities prevail. Neither owner nor builder is commanded to give a bond, though decisions are not lacking that such a command will be upheld. (Citations.) All that the statute does by force of Section 3 is to standardize the form, at least in some particulars, when bonds are freely given, and to define the consequences attaching to the standard thus prescribed. The form shall include a clause for the protection of materialmen and laborers: the consequences shall include the exemption of the owner from the burden of a lien, and a like exemption of the builder. (Citations.) The security of the bond becomes a substitute for the security of the building contract and of the moneys due thereunder. 

No arbitrary restraint of liberty of contract is laid upon the owner. His personal liability toward materialmen and laborers is not greater by a dollar than it was at the beginning. To the contrary, it is less. By force of the new security he is relieved of the burden of a lien, yet he has priority of interest in the proceeds of any suit upon the bond. See Section 3 quoted supra. No arbitrary restraint of liberty is laid upon the builder. Upon the giving of a bond he is charged with a liability in favor of materialmen and laborers, a liability consistent with a fair dealing between men in that relation, but he is relieved of the duty of holding present and future payments as a fund impressed with a trust and devoted to specific uses. United States Fidelity & G. Co. v. Parsons, supra. Indeed, this very builder took advantage of that privilege, making an assignment of the contract and its proceeds to a bank; and because of the bond the assignment was upheld."

We are concerned, however, at the present time, with legislation which will protect the owner, in as many instances as can be constitutionally devised. It may be, as indicated by the language of Justice Cardozo, and as suggested in the decisions to which he refers in his opinion, that a blunt requirement would be upheld in California which mandatorily required a bond from a contractor on every job. 

However, we do not need to go that far, but might proceed more conservatively along the course which has been charted by the reasoning and decision in Roystone Company v. Thomas Darling, and also Hartford Accident & Indemnity Company and 

Aetna Casualty & Surety Company v. N. O. Nelson Manufacturing Company.

Some method should be devised, in the coming session of the Legislature, which will give real, substantial remedial legislation for both the owner and the labor, as

well as the subcontractor and materialman. It could be done by the approach afforded by Assembly Bill 2440, under which the lender would be the key man in forcing the writing of the bond, or it could be done through a measure which would leave in the real control of the owner, in cash, a substantial amount to insure the completion of the building and the payment of mechanics' liens that might be filed by those furnishing labor or materials, or both, or by encouraging the contractor of honesty and integrity to furnish a bond, if he desired to have a smaller retention of the contract price, until the full completion of the job and the discharge of all liens. 

The measure would not require that the surety be a corporate surety licensed to do business in this State, although the position of all parties would be much improved, from the legal aspect, should that be the case, for the present law Section 1200.1 (f) C. C. P.) provides that if the bond is furnished by corporate surety, licensed to do business in this State, it is conclusively presumed that the bond is a good and sufficient bond.

However, let us envisage the able, upright and honest contractor of very moderate means. He might be well able to furnish a statutory labor and material bond of individual sureties interested in his welfare and success, but be unable to get a bond from a surety company because he is unable to meet the requirements of the more conservative surety companies in the matter of the writing of bonds, because of an inadequate bankroll or other tangible assets. 

He might have, among the members of his family or friends, those willing to go on his bond in the old fashioned way, because of their desire to aid a man in the accomplishment of his life's ambition. This might eliminate expense of bond premium to the owner and, while it would not afford the medium of a conclusive presumption to the owner, the owner might feel adequately protected by the responsibility of the individual sureties whose standing, financially and otherwise, he could investigate. 

Or, he might suggest, if more conservative, that he be willing to pay the premium on the bond and that the proposed individual sureties become indemnitor to a corporate licensed surety, in accordance with the practices very commonly indulged in by such companies.

This would still enable the contractor to procure the bond and the underwriter of the surety company could then well feel that the conservative requirements of underwriting had been met. An analysis of Assembly Bill 2440 discloses that it is a measure amending Section 1188.1 C. C. P., and has to do with the relationship between mechanics' liens and other encumbrances. It makes no change in existing laws of any consequence, save and except with respect to encumbrances given to secure building loans, to wit: mortgages and trust deeds placed upon the property upon which the improvement is to be made, or is being made, the consideration for which encumbrance does not pass at the time when the encumbrance is placed upon the property, but is advanced from time to time thereafter. With respect to this particular class of encumbrances, new theories are adopted by the measure which sets up, if enacted, certain economic principles which will be beneficial to the entire community, and to all interests affected by this particular phase of the mechanics' lien law, and whether they be owners of the existing properties, the values of which are being destroyed and depreciated by the present unsound economic structure, or whether they be owners of new structures, lenders advancing funds for building purposes, laborers, subcontractors or materialmen, or title companies writing title policies in connection with building loans. 

Analyzing this measure, it will be found that the fundamental principle embodied therein is the proposition that on building loans, where the moneys are not advanced to the borrower at the time of making the loan, the priority of the mortgage, or trust deed, or other encumbrance with respect to the lien claimants is not regulated upon the present basis, to wit: upon the basis of whether or not the encumbrance has been recorded before the commencement of the structure, or afterwards, which present basis requires an examination off the record of the property itself, but is based upon the principle that the priority of the ordinary building loan, where the money is advanced in progress payments as the building progresses, is regulated with respect to lien claims, upon whether or not a bond inuring to the benefit of labor and materials has been furnished. 

The measure is so drawn that a loan can be made by the money lender after the building has commenced and still have priority over mechanics' liens, provided a bond, either under Section 1185.1 (on contractor built jobs) has been furnished, inuring directly for the benefit of labor and materials in a sum equal to 50 percent of the contract price, or a bond under Section 1188.1, inuring to the benefit of

laborers and materialmen, has been furnished. If such bond has been furnished and recorded in the recorder's office, the encumbrance, irrespective of whether it is recorded before or after the building is commenced is given priority over mechanics' liens. Neither the title companies nor the lenders, therefore, are required to make an examination of the premises and rely upon proof of facts off the record in order to establish their priority. All that either the lender or the title companies need do is to examine the records and ascertain whether a statutory labor and material bond has been filed of record, and then the title company can certify as to the priority of the encumbrance and write his title certificate. 

The present law (Sec. 1200.1 (f)) provides that if the bond is furnished by a corporate surety, licensed to do business in this State, that it is conclusively presumed that the bond is a good and sufficient bond. 

Under this measure all that title company representatives have to do is to examine into the amount of the bond and ascertain whether it has been furnished by a surety company licensed to do business in this State. Of course, if they care to, they can act upon such bonds furnished by individuals. If there is no labor and material bond under Section 1185.1, then in order to care for the jobs on which the contractor is building on a cost plus basis, and therefore furnishes no bond and has no definite contract price by which to fix the amount of his bond, then this measure, as prepared, permits on such jobs, a bond given by the owner as principal, inuring to the benefit of labor and materials. There being no contract price, in such instances, the penal obligation of the bond is measured by the face principal amount of the note or notes secured by the building loan in question. This gives a definite amount by which to measure the bond. In effect, this would lead to the furnishing of bonds on all jobs. It would protect the unwary owner from unscrupulous contractors, for the money lender on contractor built jobs would not permit the owner to be talked out of a bond by the contractor. 

The money lender, for his own protection and to preserve the priority of his paper would require that the contractor furnish a statutory labor and material bond. 

This rests with the money lender who sits in at the beginning and is able to dictate the terms upon which he will make the loan; all he has to do is to see to it that such bond is furnished, so that he may have priority over the labor and materials furnished, and that the laborer and materialman is protected by the writing of a statutory labor and material bond, the amount of which has been definitely fixed. 

The meaning of the language (the language is the same as the language under Section 1185.1 C. C. P.) of the statute relative to the labor and material bond has been interpreted by judicial construction. This is a simple matter for the money lender, and it is easy for the money lender or the title company to examine the bond and determine whether it is, within the meaning of the decisions, a statutory labor and material bond, and whether the penal obligation is the amount required by law.

This requirement on contractor built jobs will naturally improve the very distressing conditions which now exist in many instances where owners lose their property by permitting buildings to be erected thereon by dishonest contractors who have furnished no bonds. The owner's position is improved, the lender's position is not harmed but, in fact, is greatly improved, and the mechanics who have added to the value of the entire enterprise are fairly treated without injury to anyone else.

With respect to the owner built jobs, or the jobs on which there is no definite contract price, the bill requires the owner to furnish the bond. If the owner is honest and wants all bills on the job paid, he should be perfectly willing to give this protection; if he is dishonest, bitter experience has taught the necessity for the legislation in question.

The failures of speculative builders are proceeding apace with the result that owners who have purchased completed packages from the speculative builders are confronted with the loss of their down payment money, which is ordinarily not placed in escrow, unless they can obtain the cooperation of laborers, subcontractors and materialmen in foregoing part or all of their rights under the Mechanics' Lien Law.

In the instances of the speculative builders who turned sour, only those instances in which the patient subcontractors and materialmen forego their lien, wholly or in part, is it possible for the jobs to be completed and the owner to receive his property free of liens.

In many cases the subcontractors and materialmen have cooperated so that the unfortunate owner, who has made his payment outside of escrow to the speculative

builder, can procure the home which he desires to procure. But for this cooperation, either through a Chapter XI proceeding or agreements with creditors' groups, the owner would lose the down payment which he has made to the speculative builder. 

Mortgage companies, anxious to lend money, frequently cooperate with speculative builders who have no intention of paying their bills, and unfortunately some money lenders do not care whether the bills are paid or not, just so long as the building reaches a stage of progress where the value of the ground plus the structure is sufficient security for the money they have paid out under the building loan at that particular time. Under the second type of bond, referred to in Assembly Bill 2440, in this last mentioned class of cases, the requirements as to terms, are the same, except that the amount of the bond is measured by the face principal amount of the building loan. Lenders and title companies, therefore, can just as readily determine their situation as to priority by inspection of the bond and seeing to it that it is properly recorded at the proper time. 

This measure, as drafted, incorporates the principle of conditioning priority of the money lender's security upon his seeing to it that either the contractor or owner has furnished a labor and material bond. This principle is eminently just. It gives to the lender the priority which for practical business purposes he should have, but conditions his adequate protection in this respect upon his seeing to it (we must not lose sight of the fact that he dictates the terms upon which he will lend his money) that laborers and materialmen and subcontractors, who equally furnish value to the owner and security for his own loan, are protected. This requirement is a simple one and is eminently just and fair. 

The Supreme Court of the United States has upheld the constitutionality of the statutes which give priority to mechanics' liens over even old mortgages and trust deeds. Subcontractors, laborers and materialmen are not requesting that such priority be given at the hands of the Legislature. They are merely requesting that the priority of the money lender be conditioned upon more adequate protection being given them, and have been for some time. The worth of their labor and materials, or both, is just as much as the worth of the money lender's money, and is as essential as the carrying on of the building operation. The subcontractors, laborers and materialmen are willing to take a junior position to the money lender. They merely say that in exchange for taking the junior position, they should be given a measure of protection they do not receive under the present laws. 

A mechanic's lien sitting behind two or three trust deeds, experience has shown to be of little or no value. Some of the money lenders have said that a right of action against the surety company is worth little. The materialman, subcontractor and laborer are perfectly content with that right of action if it be afforded to them. 

A few large and reputable contractors have voiced some opposition to the measure on the theory that their financial standing is so unquestioned that they should not be required to furnish a bond, nor should the owner who has entire confidence in them and their responsibility be required to be burdened with the cost of a surety bond.

The statute does not require a surety bond, but merely states that if the bond is written by a corporate surety, licensed to do business in the Sate of California, the bond shall be conclusively presumed to be a good and sufficient bond. A large contractor of unquestioned responsibility should be perfectly willing to give a bond with private sureties, and it seems certain that such a contracting firm could obtain from within its own organization such personal sureties of unquestioned financial responsibility, or if such a contractor have a fixed fee, then the owner could furnish a bond under Section 1188.1, with unquestioned individual sureties. 

The Federal Government on public works, and all of the state governments require surety bonds from contractors of the most unquestioned responsibility, and the premium incurred in the writing of such a bond is considered good business practice and judgment. There is no reason why private individuals should not follow the sound business principles connected with such public work, and in the last analysis the premium cost is only a small element in the cost of the entire building construction and the good economic results that would follow from sounder programs of building construction, and make the expenditure of this small premium well worth while.

There is no logical reason for differentiating in the use of such bonds as between private work and public work. If reasonably required on public work, they are reasonably required on private work as well.

94

The measure of protection that is given by this bill does not injuriously affect the money lender, the honest contractor or the honest owner of speculatively built structures. It will tend to eliminate some of the dishonest and unscrupulous contractors. 

It will tend to eliminate the construction of buildings unsoundly financed, and to the extent that it may eliminate this last mentioned class of building endeavors, it directly affects the materialmen, subcontractors and laborers, for it decreases their market slightly. They are willing to have the market decreased in order that the unsound pictures be eliminated. This elimination will improve land values, sale values, rental values and does it in a manner that injures no one. 

Some legislative improvement is demanded in this situation both for the protection of the owner and the payment of bills incurred in the construction of the improvement. Whether this be done through the bond measure or whether it be done by some palliative such as providing, through some statute, presented with the approval of the Real Estate Commission, requiring mandatorily that all deposits made by buyers from sellers of a complete but unfinished package in the shape of a lot and building shall be placed in escrow with an independent escrow agent is a matter for consideration by your committee.

It may be that so far as the speculative builder is concerned that he might be omitted from Section 2440 and that the approach with respect to protecting G.I. and FHA on complete but unfinished packages should be something along the lines last suggested above.

Above all, however, there is a crying demand for a measure of protection to the owner which is urgent, whether that owner be an owner who has employed a contractor to build a building on land already owned by that owner, or whether that owner is a purchaser under contract who is acquiring a complete package from a speculative builder under a contract made, in most instances, long before the building has been completed.

It has been suggested that this protection can be obtained through what we call joint control or escrow arrangements. My own personal experience, over the years since this medium of fancied protection has become available through business custom, is that even with well managed joint control operations and protection afforded by the joint control is far less effective, far less satisfactory, far more delaying than the protection afforded by a statutory labor and material bond and a faithful performance bond.

The premium charged by joint control operators is, in most instances, approximately the same as the premium that would be charged by a surety company for REAL protection. The joint control operators do not desire to make a contract which would guarantee the completion of the building free of liens, for that would subject them to the jurisdiction of the Insurance Commission. 

All too frequently they lead the unfortunate owner to believe that the protection afforded by his joint control does guarantee completion of the property free of liens. 

I could refer your committee to job after job, on both wholesale and individual scales, where the fancied protection of the joint control or escrow has been most illusory. In the past few years, not only in retail batches but in wholesale lots, both contractors and speculative builders have been turning sour, with great distress to the unfortunate owners, and to the detriment, as well, of subcontractors, materialmen and, in many instances, laborers. 

The joint control operator gets his business from the contractor and the lender and, while the owner pays the expense of his services, the joint control operator is not in the position of disinterestedly protecting primarily the owner. He is only human and he is, therefore, more interested in getting the building completed (for this he must do to satisfy the lending institution) than he is in getting it completed free of all liens.

The money in the building loan, when the job turns sour, is primarily devoted to the completion of the structure and only secondarily, if any be left, to the payment of the bills incurred and the freeing of the property from liens. 

I respectfully submit, therefore, that the only real and complete protection to be afforded to the owner on private work is to have a protection tantamount to the same protection that the owner gets on public work through the medium of a faithful performance and labor and material bond. 

Whatever course of legislative approach is indulged in the end result should be, by some means, to force the writing of such bonds. Any other steps aimed at protection would be merely partial, a palliative and not a cure. 

Respectfully submitted,

GLEN BEHYMER

Opinion HARTFORD ACCIDENT & INDEMNITY COMPANY and Aetna Casualty & Surety Company, Appts., v. N. O. NELSON MANUFACTURING COMPANY, Assignee. (291 U.S. 352-361.)

Constitutional Law, §696-due process of law-interference with liberty of contract-making contractor's bond inure to benefit of laborers and materialmen. 

1. A state statute whereby the bond of a contractor guaranteeing to an owner the faithful performance of a contract for the construction of a building is declared to inure to the protection of materialmen and laborers as if a guaranty that the contractor shall pay them had been expressed therein, but which does not require that a bond be given, does not, as applied to a bond which expresses an intention to exclude materialmen and laborers, constitute an arbitrary interference with liberty of contract, with resulting violation of the Fourteenth Amendment. 

Judgment, 877-conclusiveness as to interveners. 

2. A surety on a contractor's bond who was a party to a suit by the owner of the property for a decree construing the bond in which it was adjudged that the bond inured to the benefit of materialmen and laborers, and hence that an assignment by the contractor of the proceeds of the contract was valid, is not estopped from renewing the contest as to its liability to materialmen and laborers upon an intervention by a materialman making claim to a proportionate share of the proceeds of the bond.

Appeal, 8709—jurisdiction of Supreme Court-state court's construction of state statute. 

3. The Supreme Court of the United States is bound by the construction given to a state statute by the highest court of the state. 

Constitutional Law, §696-due process of law-liens in favor of laborers and materialmen. 

4. The Fourteenth Amendment does not preclude the creation of liens in favor of materialmen and laborers upon lands improved or affected by their material or labor, even though there be no privity of contract between them and the owner, or of liens upon any moneys due the contractor from the owner or collected by the contractor from the owner.

Constitutional Law, §701—due process-control over insurance business. 

5. The business of insurance is one peculiarly subject to governmental supervision and control.

Constitutional Law, §696--liberty of contract. 

6. Liberty of contract is not an absolute concept, but is relative to many conditions of time and place and circumstance. 

*** Mr. Justice Cardozo delivered the opinion of the Court. 

The controversy hinges upon the validity of a statute of Mississippi whereby the bond of a subcontractor guaranteeing to an owner the faithful performance of a contract for the construction of a building shall inure to the benefit of persons furnishing material or labor, and this though the bond expresses an intention to exclude them.

The statute challenged by the appellants was enacted in March, 1918, and is framed for the protection of subcontractors, materialmen, laborers, and journeymen who have had a part in the making of buildings or of structures akin thereto. Laws of 1918, chap. 128, Mississippi Code of 1930. §§ 2274-2281. 

* (354) *By §1 (which amended §$3074 of the Code of 1906), materialmen or laborers, not paid by a contractor, may give notice in writing to the owner, and thereupon any amount due from the owner to the contractor shall be bound in the hands of the owner for the payment pro rate of claims covered by the notice. 

By §2, no contractor may "assign, transfer, or otherwise dispose of in any way, the contract or the proceeds theerof, to the detriment or prejudice" of materialmen or laborers, and "all such assignments, transfers, or dispositions" shall be in subordination to their rights, "provided, however, that this section shall not apply to any contract or agreement where the contractor or the master workman shall enter into a solvent bond" conditioned as provided for in §3 thereof.

By $3, any bond for the faithful performance of a building contract shall include a guaranty that the contractor shall make payment to materialmen and laborers, and if such a provision is omitted, the bond shall inure to the protection of materialmen and laborers as if the provision were expressed. The text of this section is quoted in the margin. *(355) *In October, 1926, Natchez Development Company, Inc., the owner of land in 

Natchez, Mississippi, made a contract with builders, J. V. and R. T. Burkes, for the construction of a hotel. The Burkes made a subcontract with Acme Engineering 

Company for the plumbing, heating and ventilating work, and the subcontractor assigned its contract to the N. O. Nelson Manufacturing Company, the appellee in this court. By the principal contract provision was made for the giving of a bond which was to secure materialmen and laborers as well as the owner. Thereafter the contractors did furnish a bond for the cost of the building ($316,822) with the Hartford Accident & Indemnity Company as surety, but a bond giving narrower protection, or so the surety contends, than the one that had been promised. The bond that was furnished refers to and incorporates the contract between the owner and the builders. It provides that if the principal shall indemnify the obligee against loss or damage directly caused by the failure of the * (356) principal faithfully *to perform the contract, the obligation shall be void, otherwise to remain in force, provided, however, that the obligee shall have complied with certain conditions precedent for the protection of the surety. One is that the terms of the building contract shall be faithfully fulfilled in so far as they call for performance on the part of the owner, the surety to be relieved of all liability in the event of a default. Another is that if the obligee shall have notice of any claim against the contractor for unpaid labor or material, no further payments shall be made by the obligee to the contractor until such claims are satisfied. Finally, in an effort to cut off materialmen and laborers, the bond provides that "no right of action shall accrue upon or by reason hereof to or for the benefit of any one other than the obligee named herein." 

The contractors for the building made default in the performance of their contract owing large sums of money to materialmen and laborers, including Acme Engineering Co., appellees assignor. Thereupon, the Investment Company, the owner, sued in the Chancery Court of Adams County, Mississippi, for a decree construing the bond, adjudging that it was subject to the rights and liabilities defined in §3 of the statute, and determining the proportionate interests of those entitled thereunder. The contractors, the surety, and various subcontractors, materialmen and laborers were joined as defendants, as well as an assignee of moneys due upon the contract. Other subcontractors and materialmen intervened and by cross bill and otherwise sought relief upon the bond. The Supreme Court of Mississippi held upon demurrer that the bond was one for the faithful performance of a building contract within §3 of the statute; that its effect was to substitute a new security for the protection of materialmen and laborers in place of that provided by §§1 and 2; and that by force of that substitution the contractor had become free to assign * (357) and dispose of the contract and the proceeds thereof. An assignment to a bank of moneys due from the owner to the amount of upwards of $26,000 was accordingly sustained. Hartford Acci. & Indem. Co. v. Natchez Invest. Co. 155 Miss. 31, 119 So. 

366. The cause having been remanded to the Court of Chancery, there was a trial of the issue, which was followed by a new appeal. Hartford Acci. & Indem. Co. v. 

Natchez Invest. Co. 161 Miss. 198, 219, 132 So. 535, 135 So. 497. On that appeal the court REITERATED its ruling as to the operation of the bond. It held that "none of the provisions of the bond had the effect of writing out of the contract" the provisions of the statute, "and could not have that effect." "All stipulations contrary to the statutory provisions must be disregarded so far as persons furnishing labor or material are concerned." An appeal to this court was dismissed for defect of parties. Hartford Acci. & Indem. Co. v. Bunn, 285 U. S. 169, 76 L. ed. 

685, 52 S. Ct. 354.

In the meantime, the N. O. Nelson Manufacturing Company, the present appellee, had intervened in the Court of Chancery by leave of that court, and had made claim to its proportionate share of the proceeds of the bond. The surety renewed

the contest, as it was privileged to do (Bigelow v. Old Dominion Copper Min. & 

Smelting Co., 225 U. S. 111, 127, 56 L. ed. 1009, 1021, 32 S. Ct. 641, Ann. Cas. 

1913E, 875), insisting that the bond was unaffected by the statute, and that there could be no holding to the contrary without an arbitrary interference with liberty of contract and a resulting violation of the Fourteenth Amendment. The Chancellor, overruling these contentions, gave judgment upon the bond in favor of the intervening claimant. The Supreme Court of Mississippi affirmed upon the authority of its earlier opinions. 147 So. 815, See U. S. Fid. v. Parsons, 147 Miss. 335, 112 So. 

469, 53 A. L. R. 88. An appeal to this court followed, the surety on the appeal bond joining as appellant with the surety on the bond in suit. Hartford Acci. & Indem. 

Co. v. Bunn, 285 U. S. 169, 76 L. ed. 685, 52 S. Ct. 354, supra. * (358) * As to the meaning of the statute now challenged as invalid the Supreme Court of Mississippi speaks with ultimate authority; Supreme Lodge, K. P. v. Meyer, 265 

U. S. 30, 32, 68 L. ed. 885, 887, 44 S. Ct. 432; Great Northern R. Co. v. Sunburst 

Oil & Ref. Co., 287 U. S. 358, 362, 77 L. ed. 360, 365, 53 S. Ct. 145, 85 A. L. R. 254; 

Guaranty Trust Co. v. Blodgett, 287 U. S. 508, 513, 77 L. ed. 463, 465, 53 S. Ct. 244.

We assume in accordance with its ruling that the statute was intended to apply to such a bond as the one in controversy here, and to blot out the clauses repugnant to the statutory scheme. The only question in this court is whether the result is consistent with the Constitution of the United States. Opposition is asserted by counsel for the surety. We think it is unreal. 

Materialmen and laborers may be secured by mechanics' liens upon land improved or affected by their material or labor, and this without reference to technical and ancient concepts of privity of contract. Great Southern Fire Proof Hotel Co. v. Jones, 

193 U. S. 532, 550, 48 L. ed. 778, 788, 24 S. Ct. 576; Jones v. Great Southern Fireproof Hotel Co. (C. C. A. 6th) 86 Fed. 370; Piedmont & G. C. Coal Co. v. Seaboard 

Fisheries Co., 254 U. S. 1, 9, 10, 65 L. ed. 97, 101, 41 S. Ct. 1. For like reasons they may be secured as against the owner by a lien upon any moneys due to the contractor, and secured as against the contractor by a lien upon any moneys collected from the owner. Hartford Acci. & Indem. Co. v. Natchez Invest. Co. 155 Miss. 

31, 51, 119 So. 366; United States Fidelity & G. Co. v. Parsons, 147 Miss. 335, 112 So. 469, 53 A. L. R. 88, supra; cf. United States use of Hill v. American Surety 

Co. 200 U. S. 197, 50 L. ed. 437, 26 S. Ct. 168; Mankin v. United States, 215 U. S. 

533, 54 L. ed. 315, 30 S. Ct. 174; Illinois Surety Co. v. John Davis Co. 244 U. S. 

376, 380, 61 L. ed. 1206, 1211, 37 S. Ct. 614. The fundamental liberties protected by the Fourteenth Amendment do not include immunity from restraint so deeply rooted in policy and justice. Hardware Dealers Mut. F. Ins. Co. v. Glidden Co. 284 

U. S. 151, 157, 76 L. ed. 214, 218, 52 S. Ct. 69; Advance-Rumely Thresher Co. v. 

Jackson, 287 U. S. 283, 77 L. ed. 306, 53 S. Ct. 133, 87 A. L. R. 285. The owner contracting with a builder or making payments under the contract may be required to give heed to the equities of a subcontractor or a workman adding * (359) * value to the land. The builder may be required to give heed to the same equities in contracting with the owner or in disposing of his contract or of the moneys paid thereunder. 4-L-1691 The statute of Mississippi was framed in a genuine endeavor to make these equities prevail. Neither owner nor builder is commanded to give a bond, though decisions are not lacking that such a command will be upheld. 3 cf. Gant v. Oklahoma City, 289 U. S. 98, 77 L. ed. 1058, 53 S. Ct. 530; Brazee v. Michigan, 241 

U. S. 340, 60 L. ed. 1034, 36 St. Ct. 561, Ann. Cas. 1917 C, 522. All that the statute does by force of § 3 is to standardize the form, at least in some particulars, when bonds are freely given, and to define the consequences attaching to the standard thus prescribed. The form shall include a clause for the protection of materialmen and laborers; the consequences shall include the exemption of the owner from the burden of a lien, and a like exemption of the builder. United States Fidelity & G. 

Co. v. Parsons, 147 Miss. 335, 112 So. 469, 53 A. L. R. 88, supra. The security of the bond becomes a substitute for the security of the building contract and of the moneys due thereunder. No arbitrary restraint of liberty of contract is laid upon the owner. His personal liability toward materialmen and laborers is not greater by a dollar than it was at the beginning. To the contrary, it is less. By force of the

new security he is relieved of the burden of a lien, yet he has priority of interest in the proceeds of any suit upon the bond. See § quoted supra. No arbitrary restraint of liberty is laid upon the builder. Upon the giving of a bond he is charged with a liability in favor of materialmen and laborers, a liability consistent with a fair dealing between men in that relation, but he is relieved of the duty of holding present * (360) * and future payments as a fund impressed with a trust and devoted to specific uses. 

United States Fidelity & G. Co. v. Parsons, supra. Indeed, this very builder took advantage of that privilege, making an assignment of the contract and its proceeds to a bank; and because of the bond the assignment was upheld. Hartford Acci. & 

Idem. Co. v. Natchez Invest. Co., 155 Miss. 31, 53, 119 So. 366. Plainly he is in no position to complain that the statute is invalid in its application to himself. Indeed, owner and builder do not declare themselves aggrieved, but through silence and inaction, if not otherwise, evince submission and consent. The only other person whose interests are affected is the surety on the bond. If the statute is valid in its application to owner and builder, to obligee and principal, there can be no privilege of the surety to contract on better terms. The secondary obligation must follow the primary one, and conform to its restraints. The surety has the alternative either to write its indemnities and guaranties upon the only terms permitted to obligee and principal, or to renounce the writing altogether. The business of insurance is one peculiarly subject to supervision and control. German Alliance Ins. Co. v. Lewis, 233 U. S. 389, 58 L. ed. 1011, 34 S. Ct. 612, L. R. A. 1915C. 1189; National Union 

F. Ins. Co. v. Wanberg, 260 U. S. 71, 67 L. ed. 136, 43 S. Ct. 32; Hardware Dealers 

Mut. F. Ins. Co. v. Glidden Co. 284 U. S. 151, 76 L. ed. 214, 52 S. Ct. 69, supra.

The Fourteenth Amendment does not make it necessary that materialmen and laborers shall be deprived of fair protection to the end that sureties for profit may be given an opportunity to diversify their bonds. 

Liberty of contract is not an absolute concept. Hardware Dealers Mut. F. Ins. 

Co. v. Glidden Co. 284 U. S. 151, 76 L. ed. 214, 52 S. Ct. 69, supra; Advance- 

Rumely Thresher Co. v. Jackson, 287 U. S. 283, 77 L. ed. 306, 33 S. Ct. 133, 87 

A. L. R. 285, supra; Atlantic Coast Line R. Co. v. Riverside Mills, 219 U. S. 186, 202, 55 L. ed. 167, 180, 31 S. Ct. 164, 31 L. R. A. (N. S.) 7; Chicago, B. & Q. R. 

Co. v. McGuire, 219 U. S. 549, 567, 55 L. ed. 328, 338, 31 S. Ct. 259; Mutual Loan 

Co. v. Martell, 222 U. S. 225, 235, 56 L. ed. 175, 179, 32 S. Ct. 74, Ann. Cas. 1913 

B, 529; Highland v. Russell Car * (361) & Snow Plow Co. 279 U. S. 253, * 263, 73 L. ed. 688, 691, 49 S. Ct. 314. It is relative to many conditions of time and place and circumstance. The constitution had not ordained that the forms of business shall be cast in imperishable moulds. There is no question here of the impairment of the obligation of a contract by later legislation. The act assailed by the appellants was in existence for many years before the bond in suit was written. Principal and surety in writing it became subject to the statutes then in force, and by these they must abide. 

The judgment of the Supreme Court of Mississippi is accordingly affirmed. 

KENNETH G. MCGILVRAY, Esq. 

Suite 714, Forum Bldg. 

Sacramento 14, Calif.

LAW OFFICES OF GLEN BEHYMER LOS ANGELES 13, CALIFORNIA, December 3, 1954 Re: Report of Interim Committee, California State Legislature, 

Both the Senate and Assembly 

DEAR MR. MCGILVRAY: I have received from your office, and also at the hand of

Mr. J. M. Dean, of the Building Material Dealers' Credit Association, a copy of your letter of November 12, 1954, addressed to Mr. John A. Bohn, counsel for the

Senate Interim Judiciary Committee. 

There is one item that possibly has escaped the attention of all of the interested parties and that is the fact that more Mechanics' Liens remain unfiled than are filed, all by reason of the fact that the party with the inchoate right to file the lien says to himself, "Oh, what's the use? The lien right is of no value because of the denial of the direction and mandate contained in the State Constitution, for I am with a lien right sitting behind a trust deed to which the Legislature has seen

fit to give priority over the lien right. There are numerous other lien claimants. 

The building is incomplete. The holder of the trust deed is about to foreclose it unless I consent to a ten cents on the dollar settlement. I am going to have to hire a lawyer in order to foreclose my lien and I will probably get wiped out by the trust deed."

The end result of that consideration is that he does not file the lien which he has the right to file in connection with the protection of his unpaid account. I have had materialman after materialman and subcontractor after subcontractor say to me "What's the use?" Sometimes I am able to persuade them to file their lien and attempt to protect their proper interests, and sometimes, despite all urging that they attempt to protect themselves in the clinches, they do not do so. 

I venture to say that at least half of the inchoate rights to Mechanics' Liens are not protected, both with respect to number of claims and the amounts owing. Therefore, statistics in regard to the amount of lien claims actually filed do not afford any proper yardstick of the actual amount represented by inchoate rights of lien, or the total unpaid claims of actual and potential lien claimants. 

I am sure that the reports made by the Southwest Builder and Contractor, as well as the reports of the Building Material Dealers' Credit Association, so far as Los Angeles County is concerned, are reasonably correct, but for the reasons indicated more than the mere liens themselves require consideration. 

As I have indicated in my letter of October 15, 1954, addressed to the Assembly Interim Committee on Judiciary, the real way to protect the owner with anything of true value and constructive worth is through the adoption of either one or two remedies, both of which can be sustained as a proper statute and undoubtedly constitutional on the theories expounded and advanced in 

Roystone Company v. Thomas Darling, et al., 171 Cal. 526, and the only way to give any real protection to either owner or to the laborers, subcontractors and materialmen is along lines that suggest themselves to anyone who reads the Roystone Company v. Darling case.

The Los Angeles County Grand Jury of 1953 suggested a 20% retention in the hands of the owner until after the time for filing liens has expired. This is similar to the 25% retention provided for in the statutes prior to the 1911 amendment and would be a good, practical equivalent for a faithful performance and labor and material bond.

The statute could be drawn, therefore, on a basis of affording to the contractor one of two options: (a) Allow a sufficiently large retention in cash within the control of the owner that would practically amount to a faithful performance and labor and material bond or, if he did not desire to permit as large a retention of cash by reason of the fact that he wanted to use a higher percentage of the contract price during the period of construction, he could, as an alternative (b) Furnish a 50% labor and material bond and a 50% faithful performance or completion bond, thereby protecting the owner and the laborers, subcontractors and materialmen and assuring the completion of the building free of liens. 

As a practical matter it would mean that if the contractor did not have sufficient integrity or ability or financial responsibility to procure a bond he would be compelled to permit the owner to retain the funds in his hands sufficient in percentage of the total contract price to reasonably assure completion and payment of liens.

There is no other way, in my opinion, of stabilizing the building industry, assuring the completion of buildings free of liens and weeding out those contractors who should not be in the business at all, either by reason of dishonesty, incompetency or too thin a financial picture.

A contractor who has sufficient standing with the owner and in the community to permit him to build on a cost plus basis could still remain in business even though he was not adequately financed and the owner could be protected by closely watching the payout and disbursement of his funds. 

As to the measure based along lines of the statutes of the State of Washington, it affords no real protection, would probably be considerably opposed by contractors and subcontracting groups and, experience has shown that the owner would not be sure of protection from any measure, even though it were drawn on more practical lines and would be so limited as to be possible of use from a practical standpoint.

You must remember that old Section 1184, as it existed for many years, from the time when the filing of a Notice to Withhold on private work mandatorily required the owner to withhold funds to meet the claims of laborers, subcontractors or materialmen furnishing same, as a measure did not work out at all or in practice from the standpoint of either the owner, contractor, subcontractor or materialman. 

After the Section was amended to change the withholding on private work to an optional withholding on the part of the owner the owner practically never exercised that option. The owner has always had and still has today, under the present statutes, the right to receive protection under old Section 1184 and its present replacement for the statute has always provided that the owner should have the right to demand, in writing, of every materialman or subcontractor, or other person furnishing labor or materials, or both, an equivalent of a Notice to Withhold, and that if the person served with that written demand by the owner failed to do so he deprived himself of the right to claim a Mechanic's Lien. That is the present law. 

In most instances the owner knows which are the various material firms making all major deliveries of materials and who are the subcontractors performing the various branches of the work. He can easily find out by inquiry and he can easily make the written demand.

However, it is impossible, in many cases, for the bookkeeping department and the credit department in any large material business to, within five days, ascertain the fact that delivery of materials has been commenced or to find out from the title companies who the owner of the property may be. 

Any short limitation of time within which to serve a Notice upon an owner would be very difficult of practical accomplishment and would, in many instances, deny the lien right itself. On Federal work the right to hold the prime contractor and his surety upon the Bond which he is compelled by law to give under the Miller Act is, in the cases of materialmen who have no direct contractual agreements with the prime contractor, made dependent upon furnishing to that prime contractor, within ninety days after the last delivery of materials, a statement of his account, etc. 

There is a wide difference between five days after the first delivery and ninety days after the last delivery.

Of course, on smaller jobs, as a rule, the structure is completed within a length of time which would require a shorter period of notification, if any such legislation is to be considered at all, than the period of notification provided in the Miller Act. 

The party who would have charge of any such notification in the average materialman business would be a credit man.

A material firm doing any large volume of business would have to resort to rather extraordinary techniques, utterly impractical, if the credit man was to find out, even within ten days, that the first delivery of materials had been made on a certain job, for the posting of the original entries do not reach the ledgers sometimes for several days after the goods have been shipped. 

A real small outfit could, as a virtually one man institution, comply with short notice. It might work in some of these smaller counties but it certainly would not work in the metropolitan area of Los Angeles County and its adjacent urban developments. In my opinion, such an unrealistic measure should be opposed by those who feel that they have the right to rely upon Section 15 of Article XX of the State Constitution and who do not want a further denial of that right enacted by the California Legislature.

The present law goes as far toward denial as to make the lien right of doubtful value at best.

I have taken the liberty of sending a copy of this letter to the Assembly Interim 

Committee on Judiciary, care of Mr. Gordon A. Fleury, and to Mr. John A. Bohn, counsel of the Senate Interim Judiciary Committee. 

Yours truly, (Signed)

GLEN BEHYMER 4. REPORT OF THE CONTRACTORS STATE LICENSE BOARD 

At the request of the committee, this board furnished certain statistical information as to matters affecting the construction industry and, paricularly, mechanics liens and related complaints against contractors. 

The committee wishes to express its appreciation to this board for its prompt and comprehensive response, which is hereinafter reprinted in full.

JOHN A. BOHN, Counsel Senate Interim Judiciary Committee 

640 First Street, Benicia, California 

DEAR MR. BOHN: Pursuant to your request made in Los Angeles following a meeting of your committee, we are enclosing statistics relative to disciplinary actions filed with this board state-wide. 

CONTRACTORS STATE LICENSE BOARD 

SACRAMENTO 14, October 13, 1954

These statistics reflect our activities for the calendar year 1953, the first six months of the 1954 calendar year, cases pending and not acted upon as of October 1, 1954, and the number of instances in which the various sections of the Business and Professions Code were alleged in said periods. 

You will note the preponderance of accusations alleging violations of Sections 7108, 7113, and 7120 of said code, which deal with finances. In addition, the highest number of allegations include Section 7116, which though dealing with wilful and fraudulent acts, generally pertain to monetary transactions. 

The figures on liens filed represent only allegations where liens actually had been filed. A great many instances occur where licensees are found guilty of violations of 7108 (diversion of funds), 7113 (failure to complete for the contract price), and 7120 (failure to pay bills), and in said accusations, it has been alleged and found that although liens had not been filed, threats of liens existed, following which in many cases payments were made by the home owners to prevent filing of liens and clouded titles.

A check has been made through our Los Angeles office of liens recorded in Los Angeles County. The figures being furnished were those which were shown in Daily 

Construction Reports (green sheets) as compiled and printed by the same organization that prints the South West Builder and Contractor. It is our understanding that this is one of the F. W. Dodge Corporation groups from which statistics are taken nation-wide relative to the construction industry. 

These statistics show that in Los Angeles County, 5,532 liens were recorded in the 1952 calendar year; that 11,865 liens were recorded in the 1953 calendar year; that 12,737 liens were recorded during the first six months of the 1954 calendar year; that 4,653 liens were recorded during July and August of 1954. 

It may interest you to know also that during the year 1953, 76,564 completion notices were filed as against 11,865 liens recorded; that 41,173 completion notices were filed as against 12,737 liens recorded for the first six months of 1954; and that 15,832 completion notices were filed as against 4,653 liens recorded during the months of July and August, 1954.

We trust this is the information you desire and are enclosing several copies of our own statistics for the use of your committee.
