6. REPORT OF THE LEGISLATIVE COUNSEL 

The Legislative Counsel was requested to make comments on the grand jury recommendations, and his report is herewith reprinted.

STATE OF CALIFORNIA OFFICE OF LEGISLATIVE COUNSEL 

LOS ANGELES 12, CALIFORNIA, November 10, 1953

HONORABLE JACK B. TENNEY 

112 State Building, Los Angeles 12, California

Mechanics' Liens-No. 300 

DEAR SENATOR TENNEY: Pursuant to your request we have conferred with Mr.

Ritzie of the District Attorney's office who is assigned to the Los Angeles County Grand Jury as its legal advisor, relative to the Grand Jury's investigation of mechanics' liens as they affect small home owners. 

The problem is substantially as follows: A owns a lot and he wishes to build a home on it. A arranges with B, a lending institution, for a loan secured by a trust deed. C, the contractor, builds the home. B advances the money to C usually by a series of progress payments. Thus, 20 percent of the money is paid when 20 percent of the work is completed. The subcontractors and the suppliers of material look to the contractor for payment. B pays out all or most of the funds to C, the contractor. C then gets into financial difficulties and does not pay the claims of the subcontractors and the suppliers of material, who, thereupon, file mechanics' liens against the property. The lending institution is protected since its encumbrance is prior to that of the mechanics' liens if it has been properly recorded. 

The Grand Jury has concluded that all of the parties are adequately protected in this situation except the home owner. To remedy this situation the Grand Jury recommends that certain legislation be enacted. These recommendations, as contained in the letter of Mr. Frank S. Ross to you, dated November 5, 1953, are discussed below. "(a) The agreement which is signed between the lending institution and the home builder should be such that the lending institution is required to handle the home builder's funds, which it retains in its hands, in trust throughout the transaction."

This recommendation is somewhat vague. Apparently what is desired is legislation imposing a duty on the lender to handle the funds loaned in such a manner that the interests of the borrower are protected. "(b) To assist the lending institutions to properly discharge their obligations as trustees of these funds we believe it is essential that the existing lien laws be amended as follows: "1. Those individuals or corporations who supply material or labor to the contractor or subcontractor should be required to furnish invoices covering these materials or labor to the lending institutions and the individual whose home is being built within a maximum of ten days after the material is placed on the building site, or the labor has been performed. Such invoices should be sent by U. S. registered mail to insure their delivery. Such invoices should also clearly show in large print that unless the bill is paid, a mechanic's lien can be filed against the building by the person submitting the invoice." 

Under the present law, lien claimants may file stop notices with the owner. Such a notice authorizes the owner to withhold payment from the contractor. The owner can require such notices to be filed, and if it is not filed, after a written demand from the owner, the lien claimant is deprived of his mechanic's lien. (Sec. 1190.1, Code of Civil Procedure.)

Under the legislation proposed by the Grand Jury every subcontractor, material man, and person who furnishes labor, in other words all possible lien claimants, would be required to furnish invoices covering the materials or services furnished to the lender and the owner. Presumably, failure to do so would result in the loss of lien rights. The argument for such legislation is that it would give the owner notice of the claims and would enable him to check to see if such claims had been paid by the contractor. If he discovered these claims had not been paid it would give the owner or the lender an opportunity to withhold further payments to the contractor until the claims were paid. The argument against such a proposal would seem to be that it imposes additional duties on the lien claimants. "2. The present law is to the effect that liens can be filed against a home up to and including thirty days after notice of completion. We believe this is basically wrong. We feel that the law should be amended to require the lien

period to commence to run from the date of completion of the particular type of service rendered and in no case more than thirty days after that specific service or material is furnished."

Under the present law the time for filing a claim of lien runs, in general, from the time of completion of the entire work of improvement (Section 1193.1, Code of 

Civil Procedure). If the owner files a notice of completion within ten days after completion the lien claimant has thirty days to file his claim. If notice is not so filed the lien claimants have ninety days after completion of the improvement to file their claims. Thus, there is generally only one completion date. The time for all lien claimants to file their claims is based on this single date. 

The proposed legislation departs from this principle of the present mechanic's lien law in that each claimant's lien-filing period would then date from the time the materials or services were furnished upon which the claim is based. "3. Under present practice most lending institutions advance funds to contractors upon the presentation of an affidavit that a certain amount of work has been completed. Should the contractor prove dishonest the home owner loses. We believe that this affidavit should be accompanied by receipted invoices covering the word and that the lending institution holding the funds in trust should be required by law not to dispense the funds until the receipted invoice is made a part of the affidavit." 

This recommendation refers to the present practice of lenders of making progress payments to the contractor when a certain stage of construction has been completed. 

Thus, when the work of improvement is 20 per cent completed, 20 per cent of the funds are advanced. We understand that the lender does not usually check to determine if the various subcontractors, materialmen, and laborers have been paid. This would require the contractor to furnish receipted invoices to show that the contractor has paid for the services rendered by others in achieving that particular stage of construction. "4. Members of the Grand Jury are also considering the feasibility of requiring the lending institutions to retain approximately 20 per cent of the funds involved until a period of thirty days has expired after the completion notice. Thus, insuring protection for the small home owner where bills remain unpaid and liens will be filed."

This proposal is self-explanatory. We understand that in the situations coming to the Grand Jury's attention the lender has paid out all of the proceeds of the loan to the contractor by the time the lien claims have been filed. By requiring the retention of a certain proportion of the loan proceeds until the time for filing liens has expired the owner would be protected in such a situation. 

Pursuant to your authorization, we also discussed this matter with Mr. Glen 

Behymer, an attorney in Los Angeles who is recognized as one of the leading authorities on the mechanic's lien law of this State. 

He has two suggestions, either of which he believes would protect the owner in this type of situation. 

1. Require the contractor to post a bond for the benefit of possible mechanic's lien claimants for every work of improvement. Such bonds are now required in connection with state public works projects (Government Code, Sec. 14371). 

The Assembly Interim Committee on Judicial System and Judicial Process con sidered such a proposal and in its 1951 Progress Report to the Legislature, on page 

16, discussed it as follows: "There is much to be said in favor of requiring a bond for the benefit of possible mechanic's lien claimants to be posted for every work of improvement. 

The lender benefits because he is freed from any problem of priority of his encumbrance over mechanics' liens. The lien claimant gains because it would seem that he has a speedier and surer recovery from a bonding company than from enforcement of his lien against the property. The landowner would find his property protected against liens to some extent, and the surety might assist the owner's business judgment by refusing a bond if the contractor has a poor record or by requiring a joint control of disbursements. There are already bond provisions in the present Sections 1183 (now Section 1185.1) and 1191 (now 

Section 1189.1). However, the committee did not feel justified in strait-jacketing landowners and developers to the extent of requiring bonds in all cases. The cost of a bond on an ordinary residence would probably be less than $100, but such a charge, together with the accompanying red tape and paper work, might be the straw that breaks the camel's back. We think that it is sufficient if the

bond be optional with the lender. If all concerned are satisfied to trust each other and save time and money thereby, why should the Legislature forbid it? 

Adequate machinery for their protection has been provided, if they want to use it."

Under the present law either the lender or the owner may require the contractor to take out such a bond (Sec. 1185.1, Code of Civil Procedure). 

2. Provide that, in order for a lender's loan to constitute a prior lien on the work of improvement, a completion and labor material bond must be required of the contractor. 

Under the present law (Sec. 1188.1, Code of Civil Procedure), a mortgage or trust deed takes priority over mechanic's liens if the mortgage or trust deed is entered into and recorded prior to commencement of the work of improvement. Even if these conditions are not met the trust deed or mortgage may still have priority if a bond for the benefit of possible mechanic's lien claimants is posted which meets certain requirements (Sec. 1189.1, Code of Civil Procedure). 

The effect of this proposal would be to compel the lender to require the contractor to take out a bond in order to preserve the priority of his mortgage or trust deed. 

The effect, of course, would be that such a bond would be required in the case of every work of improvement financed by a loan. 

We will be happy to prepare legislation along the lines of any of these proposals if you so desire. 

Very truly yours, 7. VIEWS OF A SUBCOMMITTEE OF THE LOS ANGELES CHAMBER OF COMMERCE RALPH N. KLEPS Legislative Counsel By OWEN K. KUNS, Deputy PAUL L. BURKHARD, CONTRACTOR, BUILDER, DEVELOPER GLENDALE 5, CALIFORNIA, September 16, 1954 MR. JOHN A. BOHN, Counsel, 

640 First Street, Benicia, California.

DEAR MR. BOHN: Absence from the city prevented me from replying promptly to your letter of September 13th relative to the Hearing of the Senate Interim Judiciary Committee to be held in San Diego on September 20th and 21st. I am particularly interested in certain proposed revisions and reforms in the mechanics' lien statutes.

A special subcommittee of the Los Angeles Chamber of Commerce on the Mechanics' Lien Law, of which I am chairman, held a meeting on April 26th, 1954, attended by representatives of A.I.A., A.G.C., Los Angeles Realty Board, Mortgage 

Bankers Association, Building Material Dealers Credit Association, Surety Underwriters Association, California Savings & Loan League, Home Builders Institute, Building Contractors Association of California, as well as representatives of the Bank of America, the California Bank and the Title Insurance and Trust Company. 

At that time it appeared to be the unanimous opinion of those who were present that certain reforms should be made in the Lien Law for the benefit of the public. 

It appeared to be the opinion of this committee that anyone who may be asked to pay a bill by a material supplier is entitled to know that a bill has been incurred on his behalf in order that he may have the opportunity of determining to his own satisfaction whether or not the bill has been paid. Further in the opinion of the committee, if any material supplier wishes to avail himself of the Lien Law, it should be incumbent upon him, at least on the occasion of the first delivery, to notify the owner from whom he may wish to enforce payment. This will not work a hardship on anyone and will give an owner an opportunity to protect himself by requiring evidence from those to whom he makes payments that bills incurred by others for improvements to be made on his property have been satisfied. 

I regret my inability to be in San Diego on September 20th and 21st as I should like to present my views personally. However, it is my understanding that the viewpoint expressed herein will be presented by others representing the Building Contractors Association of California, the Home Builders Council of California and the Home Builders Institute of Metropolitan Los Angeles. If there are to be hearings at

a later date in Los Angeles I should like to be advised in order that I may be able to appear at that time. 

Very truly yours, (Signed) 

8. VIEWS OF THE CALIFORNIA STATE BUILDERS EXCHANGE, LTD. 

CALIFORNIA STATE BUILDERS EXCHANGE, LTD. 

SACRAMENTO 14, CALIFORNIA, September 17, 1954

MR. JOHN A. BOHN, Counsel SENATE INTERIM JUDICIARY COMMITTEE 

San Diego, California

PAUL L. BURKHARD 

DEAR JOHN: Thank you for your letter of September 13th regarding the San

Diego hearings. I regret that we will not be able to have a representative present at that time, but we will definitely plan to participate in any future hearings that are scheduled on the subject of mechanics' lien statutes. 

In the meantime the committee may wish to have some idea of our current thinking on this subject. We believe that some more adequate protection to owners should be provided if it can be done without substantially increasing all building costs. 

Some of the proposals that have been advanced in recent months would increase the costs unreasonably in relation to the real risk involved. We are very seriously concerned with those cases where the owner suddenly finds himself in a fix where he may lose both his building funds and his property, or, if he is able, may have to pay twice for his construction work. This is a bad situation. But it is not sufficiently widespread to warrant protective measures that would saddle all buyers of construction services with substantial cost increases. 

While we are not prepared at this time to propose or support any specific legislation, we have studied various proposals made by other groups and we believe that a plan which will be presented to the committee by Mr. Gordon Stater on behalf of the Building Contractors Association of California merits very careful consideration. This proposal would put the owner on notice on the possibility of lien claims for materials and would enable the owner to protect himself before the damage is done.

If a record or report is to be prepared covering the San Diego hearings, we would appreciate receiving a copy. 

Sincerely yours, (Signed)

HAROLD G. REYNOLDS Executive Vice-President 9. SPECIFIC LEGISLATION SUGGESTED 

The following specific legislative proposal was suggested to the committee and is reprinted herewith, followed by an analysis with citations of the Washington statute, upon which the proposal is apparently based. 

Proposed Amendment to the California Mechanic's Lien Law An act to amend Chapter 2, Title IV, Part 3 of the Code of Civil Procedure, by adding Section thereto, providing that all persons intending to claim the benefit of the Mechanic's Lien Law must give notice to the owner of the property, and to the general contractor that such proposed claimant is furnishing materials or supplies for use on the property being improved; making the giving of such notice a condition precedent to the right to file a mechanic's lien; specifically exempting from the provisions of this enactment mechanic's liens for labor, and mechanic's liens by a general contractor. 

Every person, firm or corporation furnishing materials or supplies for the construction of improvements upon real estate must, not later than five (5) days after the date of the first delivery of material, serve a notice in writing, stating in substance that he or it has commenced to deliver materials or supplies for use thereon. 

Such notice shall be served personally, or by registered mail, upon the person, firm or corporation hereinafter mentioned. If the construction of the improvement or improvements on the real estate is being done by a general contractor, then service of the notice shall be upon both such general contractor and the owner; otherwise service of the notice shall only be made upon the owner. If there be more than one owner, service upon one of the owners shall suffice. Owner as used herein is de-

fined as in Section

Code of Civil Procedure. If the name or address of the general contractor, or the owner of the property is not known to the person, firm or corporation furnishing materials or supplies, then such notice shall be served upon the owner and upon the general contractor, if there be one, shown upon the building permit issued for the construction of such project, and at the address of such person or persons as shown on said building permit. Such notice shall state the name and address of the person, firm or corporation furnishing materials or supplies, and the name of the contractor or agent ordering said materials or supplies, and that a mechanic's lien may be claimed therefor, and no further or additional such notice shall be necessary.

Unless such notice is given within the time and manner hereinbefore stated, no mechanic's lien for materials or supplies shall be filed, enforceable, or enforced, and no suit or action to foreclose any such mechanic's lien can be maintained; and proof of the giving of such notice shall be a prerequisite to the enforcement of any such mechanic's lien. Such mechanic's lien may, however, be filed and enforced as to materials and supplies furnished within said five (5) days prior to the giving of said notice, and for materials and supplies thereafter furnished or supplied upon such real estate.

The provisions of this section shall apply only to materials and supplies, and shall have no application to labor or personal services. 

The general contractor on such a construction project shall not be required to give such notice to the owner in order for such general contractor to claim or establish the general contractor's own mechanic's lien. 

Washington Statute 

The Washington statute providing for notice of delivery of materials and that a lien may be claimed for such materials and supplies has been in force since 1909. 

The original statute merely provided for the delivery of Duplicate Statements, and was amended in 1911 to read substantially as the present statute, Sec. 1133, R. C. W. 60.04,020, adopted in 1951 and which reads substantially as follows:

Every person, firm or corporation furnishing materials or supplies as above specified shall, not later than five days after the date of the first delivery, deliver or mail to the owner or the reputed owner of the property upon which the materials or supplies are to be used, a notice in writing, stating in substance that he or it has commenced to deliver materials and supplies for use thereon, with the name of the contractor or agent ordering them, and that a lien may be claimed therefor, and no further notice shall be necessary. No materialman's lien shall be enforced unless the provisions of this chapter are complied with. 

The cases are definite that in order to claim a lien for materials furnished to a subcontractor; notice must be given to the owner. 

Robinson Tile & Marble Co. v. Samuels, (1928) 147 Wn. 445, 266 Pac. 701.

The notice need not be given where the materials were delivered on the order of the owner or his agent. 

Colby & Dickinson v. McCulloch (1927) 145 Wn. 54, 261 Pac. 86; Miller v. 

Schober (1920) 111 Wn. 631, 191 Pac. 800; and Rieflin v. Grafton (1911) 

63 Wn. 387, 115 Pac. 851.

However, such notice must be given if the materials are furnished at the order of the statutory agent of the owner, Stouffer-Bowman, Inc. v. Webber, (1943) 18 Wn. 2d 416, 139 P 2d 717; and such notice is not waived by the fact that the owner had actual notice of the delivery of the materials, Johnson v. Heirgood, (1913) 72 Wn. 120, 129 P. 909.

When the notice is mailed as required by the statute, the risk of loss in the mail falls on the owner. 

Building Supplies v. Gillingham (1943) 17 Wn. 2d 489, 135 P 2d 832.

A notice addressed to the owner at the place where the building is to be constructed, which is not the owner's residence, is not sufficient. 

Seattle Lbr. Co. v. Richardson & Elmer Co. (1912) 66 Wn. 671, 120 Pac. 517.

Where there is no evidence to show what proportion of a lien is for labor, the entire lien for labor and materials fail where the necessary notice of the material is not given. 

Hallett v. Phillips (1913) 73 Wn. 457, 132 Pac. 51.

Problems of this nature appear to be the most difficult to solve under the Washington statute.

10. COMMITTEE CONCLUSIONS 

The committee has found that a great many groups and individuals desire changes in the present mechanics lien laws and nearly all persons with whom the matter has been discussed and who have testified at committee hearings agree that changes should be made. However, there is almost no unanimity of opinion as to how the statutes should be amended, and there is sharp conflict between various groups interested in the field on nearly every specific proposal that has been suggested or considered.

Very substantial changes in the mechanics lien laws were heretofore made by the Legislature, and this committee believes that further changes should be considered with extreme care before being enacted into law. This is particularly true since the basic authority for mechanics lien legislation is in the Constitution and any change short of repealing the constitutional provision must be adopted within the framework specified by the Constitution.

The committee, therefore, does not propose any specific recommendations for changes in the mechanics lien law at this time but does recommend that the entire subject be given further study and an attempt made to provide a new approach to the problem which will be of practical protection to the home owner, without adding undue burdens to the lien claimant. In this connection the committee wishes to point out that many of the proposals submitted assume that the home owner is sufficiently versed in the perils of the mechanics lien statute to adequately protect himself if given timely notice. Although the committee has conducted no extensive surveys on the subject, it ventures to speculate that the whole principle of mechanics liens is so foreign to the normal debtor-creditor relationship that the owner must in some manner be protected without requiring him personally to take any affirmative steps.

It is pointed out that the fundamentals of the various relationships are as follows: 

1. The owner borrows from the bank upon the security of the home to be constructed and becomes the debtor of the bank only; 

2. The owner executes a contract with a building contractor who is not necessarily in privity with the lending institution, although the payments due from the owner to the contractor, as the building progresses, may coincide with the same progress of construction specified in the loan for advances to the owner on account of the deed of trust; 

3. Neither the owner nor the lending institution has any privity of contract with, the materialmen, subcontractors and laborers, yet it is upon the nonpayment of these items that the property of the owner may be subjected to lien; 

4. It may be speculated that the owner having received his progress payment from the lending institution and paying it over to the contractor no longer feels concerned about any further obligation, and even though he were notified that given materialmen or subcontractors had furnished materials, he would hardly be alarmed unless it were further explained to him that in order to protect himself he had to assume the further responsibility of seeing to it that this account was actually paid by the contractor in spite of the fact that the owner had already discharged his own contract liability to the builder.

C. REORGANIZATION OF STATUTES PERTAINING TO ADOPTION 

The committee received testimony pertaining to a needed revision of the statutes relating to adoption, and there was submitted to the committee from the Department of Social Welfare a draft of the proposed revisions.

At the same time the committee was informed that the State Bar had appointed a special committee to consider this subject, and it is believed that it will be the subject of substantial legislative proposals during the 1957 Session of the Legislature. 

There is accordingly herewith reprinted for further study a copy of the proposals submitted by the Department of Social Welfare, and the committee desires to express its appreciation to the honorable Edward E. Silveira, Acting Director of that department, for his cooperation in presenting pertinent material at its hearings.
